## Chapter 4 — Hypervisors, virtual machines and containers

**Objective covered:** **1.2 Describe the role and function of hypervisors, virtual machines, and containers.** The verb is *describe*: you will not configure a hypervisor in the exam, but you must explain how these technologies work and how they connect to the network. That connection matters for objective 2.2.d (switch ports for virtualized hosts) in Chapter 7.

### 4.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Define host, guest, hypervisor, virtual machine, container, image and virtual switch.
2. Compare Type 1 and Type 2 hypervisors and name examples of each.
3. Explain how VMs reach the physical network through vNICs, virtual switches, port groups and 802.1Q trunks.
4. Compare VMs and containers, and describe basic container networking (bridge, NAT, published ports).
5. Describe virtual network appliances (NFV) such as virtual routers and firewalls.
6. Recognise network problems caused by virtualization and say what to check.

### 4.2 Introduction

A college lab server in Kathmandu might run a web server, a database, a DHCP server and a monitoring tool. Twenty years ago each would need its own physical machine, mostly sitting idle. **Virtualization** lets one physical server run many isolated workloads at once, each believing it has its own computer. The result is fewer servers, less power, faster deployment and easier recovery.

For a network engineer, virtualization changes one thing above all: **one switch port may now carry traffic for dozens of machines in several VLANs.**

### 4.3 Key terms

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Host | The physical server (or laptop) that provides CPU, memory, storage and NICs |
| Hypervisor | Software that creates and runs VMs and shares the host's hardware among them (also called a virtual machine monitor) |
| Virtual machine (VM) | A complete software computer: virtual CPU (vCPU), memory, disk and NIC, running its own **guest operating system** |
| Guest OS | The OS inside a VM (Windows Server, Ubuntu, Cisco IOS XE in a virtual router) |
| Virtual disk | A file that acts as the VM's hard disk (.vmdk, .vhdx, .qcow2) |
| Snapshot | A saved point-in-time state you can roll back to |
| Live migration | Moving a running VM to another host without shutting it down (VMware vMotion, Hyper-V Live Migration) |
| Container | An isolated process (or group) that packages an application and its libraries but **shares the host's OS kernel** |
| Image | A read-only template from which VMs or containers are started |
| Virtual switch (vSwitch) | Software switch inside the host that connects VMs to each other and to the physical NICs |

### 4.4 Type 1 and Type 2 hypervisors

**Type 1 (bare-metal)** hypervisors run directly on the server hardware, with no general-purpose OS underneath. They are efficient and secure, and they run data centres. Examples: VMware ESXi, Microsoft Hyper-V, KVM (part of the Linux kernel), Xen.

**Type 2 (hosted)** hypervisors run as an application on top of an ordinary OS. They are convenient for labs and desktops but add a layer of overhead. Examples: Oracle VirtualBox, VMware Workstation, VMware Fusion, Parallels Desktop.

Figure 4.1 compares the three software stacks, including containers.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch04/fig-04-01-hypervisor-stacks.svg" alt="Figure 4.1 — Software stacks: Type 1 hypervisor, Type 2 hypervisor and containers" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 4.1</b> — Software stacks: Type 1 hypervisor, Type 2 hypervisor and containers</sub></p>

Read each column of Figure 4.1 from the hardware upward. A Type 1 hypervisor sits directly on the hardware; a Type 2 hypervisor needs a host OS beneath it. Containers drop the guest OS entirely, which is why they start in seconds and use far less memory.

|  | Type 1 (bare-metal) | Type 2 (hosted) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Runs on | Hardware directly | A host OS (Windows, macOS, Linux) |
| Performance | Near native | Lower; host OS competes for resources |
| Typical use | Production servers, data centres, cloud | Laptops, training labs, testing |
| Examples | ESXi, Hyper-V, KVM, Xen | VirtualBox, VMware Workstation, Parallels |
| Management | Central tools (vCenter, SCVMM) or web UI | Desktop application |

**Exam tip:** "bare-metal" always means Type 1; "runs on top of Windows/macOS" always means Type 2.

### 4.5 How VMs connect to the network

A VM has no physical cable. Its traffic travels through three pieces of software and then a physical NIC (Figure 4.2):

1. **vNIC** — the VM's virtual network card. It has its own MAC address, so the physical switch learns many MACs behind one port.
2. **Virtual switch (vSwitch)** — a Layer 2 switch inside the hypervisor. It forwards frames between VMs on the same host without them ever reaching the physical network, and sends everything else out an uplink. Examples: VMware vSphere Standard and Distributed Switch, Hyper-V Virtual Switch, Linux bridge, Open vSwitch.
3. **Port group** — a set of vSwitch ports sharing a policy, most importantly a **VLAN ID**. Put a VM's vNIC in the "Staff, VLAN 10" port group and its traffic belongs to VLAN 10.
4. **Uplinks (physical NICs)** — the host's real NICs, for example vmnic0 and vmnic1. Because VMs in different VLANs share them, the uplinks normally carry an **802.1Q trunk** to the physical switch. Two or more uplinks are often bundled (NIC teaming), which on the switch side may be an **EtherChannel/LACP** port channel.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch04/fig-04-02-virtual-switching.svg" alt="Figure 4.2 — Virtual switching: vNICs, port groups, vSwitch and an LACP trunk to SW1" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 4.2</b> — Virtual switching: vNICs, port groups, vSwitch and an LACP trunk to SW1</sub></p>

In Figure 4.2 arrows show outbound traffic. Each VM's vNIC sits in a port group, the vSwitch tags frames with that port group's VLAN, and the two highlighted uplinks carry the tagged frames to SW1 as one LACP bundle. SW1 must allow both VLANs on the trunk, or VMs in a missing VLAN are cut off.

**Step by step: VM-Web (VLAN 10) sends a packet to its gateway.**

1. VM-Web's vNIC sends an untagged frame into the Staff port group.
2. The vSwitch knows the port group is VLAN 10, so it adds an 802.1Q tag with VLAN 10 when sending the frame out vmnic0.
3. SW1 receives the tagged frame on its trunk and forwards it in VLAN 10 toward the gateway (an SVI or router).
4. Replies come back tagged VLAN 10; the vSwitch removes the tag and delivers the frame to VM-Web.

**Recognising virtual MAC addresses.** Hypervisors use their own OUIs, so a MAC table full of them tells you a port connects to a virtualization host:

| OUI (first 3 bytes) | Usually means |
| --- | --- |
| 00:50:56 | VMware VM |
| 00:15:5D | Hyper-V VM |
| 08:00:27 | VirtualBox VM |
| 52:54:00 | KVM/QEMU VM |

```
SW1# show mac address-table interface port-channel1
          Mac Address Table
-------------------------------------------
Vlan    Mac Address       Type        Ports
----    -----------       --------    -----
  10    0050.56a1.0b01    DYNAMIC     Po1      <- VM-Web
  10    0050.56a1.0b02    DYNAMIC     Po1      <- VM-DB
  99    0050.56a1.0b03    DYNAMIC     Po1      <- VM-Mgmt
```

Three VMs in two VLANs, all learned on one logical port: exactly the picture in Figure 4.2.

### 4.6 Containers

A **container** packages one application with exactly the libraries it needs and runs it as an isolated process on a shared OS kernel. The Linux kernel features **namespaces** (each container sees its own processes, network interfaces and file system) and **cgroups** (limits on CPU and memory) provide the isolation.

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| Image | Read-only template: the app, its libraries and settings (for example `nginx:latest`) |
| Container | A running instance of an image |
| Registry | Where images are stored and downloaded (Docker Hub, a private registry) |
| Container engine / runtime | Software that pulls images and runs containers (Docker, containerd, Podman) |
| Orchestrator | Runs many containers across many hosts, restarts failed ones, scales them (Kubernetes) |

**How containers are networked (Figure 4.3).** By default Docker on Linux:

1. Creates a Linux bridge called **docker0**, normally 172.17.0.1/16.
2. Gives each container a virtual interface on that bridge and an address such as 172.17.0.2.
3. Uses **NAT (masquerading)** on the host so containers can reach outside networks using the host's IP.
4. Makes a container reachable from outside only when a port is **published**: `-p 8080:80` maps the host's port 8080 to the container's port 80.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch04/fig-04-03-docker-networking.svg" alt="Figure 4.3 — Default Docker networking: docker0 bridge, NAT and a published port (-p 8080:80)" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 4.3</b> — Default Docker networking: docker0 bridge, NAT and a published port (-p 8080:80)</sub></p>

In Figure 4.3 the highlighted arrows trace a request from a laptop on the home LAN to the web container. Nothing outside the host can reach 172.17.0.2 directly; only the published port is exposed. This is the same NAT idea you will configure on routers in Chapter 21.

Other modes exist: **host** networking (the container uses the host's interfaces directly), **none** (no network), and **overlay** networks that span several hosts in an orchestrated cluster.

### 4.7 VMs vs containers

|  | Virtual machine | Container |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Includes | Full guest OS + app | App + libraries only |
| Kernel | Its own | Shares the host's |
| Size | Gigabytes | Megabytes |
| Start time | Tens of seconds to minutes | About a second |
| Isolation | Strong (separate kernels) | Process-level; weaker if the kernel is compromised |
| Run a different OS? | Yes (Windows VM on a Linux host) | Must match the host kernel type (Linux containers need a Linux kernel) |
| Best for | Different OSs, legacy apps, strong separation | Microservices, fast scaling, CI/CD pipelines |

They are not rivals: in practice containers often run **inside VMs**, especially in the cloud.

### 4.8 Virtual network appliances (NFV)

**Network Functions Virtualization (NFV)** runs network devices as software on standard servers instead of dedicated hardware. Examples from Cisco:

- **Catalyst 8000V** — a virtual IOS XE router (successor to the CSR 1000v), common in public clouds and data centres.
- **Secure Firewall Threat Defense Virtual (FTDv)** and **ASAv** — virtual firewalls.
- **Virtual wireless controllers** — controller software running as a VM (Chapter 7, objective 2.2.b).

Lab tools use the same idea: **Cisco Modeling Labs (CML)**, GNS3 and EVE-NG run real IOS images as VMs or containers. Packet Tracer is different: it **simulates** device behaviour rather than running real Cisco software, which is why some features are missing (as noted in Part 0.4).

Some Cisco switches and routers can also host containers themselves (Cisco IOx application hosting), but that is beyond the CCNA objectives.

### 4.9 What virtualization means for the network engineer

You rarely configure the hypervisor, but you configure the switch ports it plugs into, and you get the ticket when a VM "has no network". Three facts drive everything:

1. **One port, many MACs and VLANs.** A host port is usually a trunk carrying every VLAN its VMs use.
2. **VMs move.** Live migration can move a VM to another host at any moment, so every host in a cluster needs the same VLANs on its uplinks.
3. **The vSwitch and the physical switch must agree** on VLAN IDs, the native VLAN, and how uplinks are bundled.

**A preview of the switch configuration (taught fully in Chapters 5–7):**

```
SW1(config)# interface range gigabitethernet1/0/1 - 2
SW1(config-if-range)# description ESXi-01 vmnic0-1
SW1(config-if-range)# switchport mode trunk
SW1(config-if-range)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,99
SW1(config-if-range)# switchport nonegotiate
SW1(config-if-range)# spanning-tree portfast trunk
SW1(config-if-range)# channel-group 1 mode active      ! only if the host side uses LACP
```

Notes: newer IOS releases write the PortFast command as `spanning-tree portfast edge trunk`. Bundle the ports with LACP **only** when the hypervisor's teaming is set to LACP (for example a VMware distributed switch with a LAG); many teaming modes expect two independent trunk ports instead.

**Troubleshooting virtualized hosts**

| Symptom | Likely cause | What to check | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| VMs in one VLAN have no network; other VMs on the same host are fine | VLAN not allowed on the trunk, or not created on the switch | `show interfaces trunk`, `show vlan brief` | Add the VLAN to the switch and the allowed list |
| A VM sits in the wrong subnet | Wrong VLAN ID on its port group | Hypervisor port group settings; `show mac address-table address <vm-mac>` | Correct the port group VLAN |
| Intermittent loss; log shows `%SW_MATM-4-MACFLAP_NOTIF` | NIC teaming and switch bundling disagree, so VM MACs flap between ports | `show logging`, `show etherchannel summary` | Make teaming and switch configuration match (both LACP, or both independent) |
| VM loses network after live migration | Destination host's uplinks lack that VLAN | Compare trunks on all hosts in the cluster | Make trunks identical across the cluster |
| Two VMs fight over an address after cloning | Clone kept the same static IP or MAC | `show ip arp`, duplicate-address warnings | Regenerate the MAC, re-address the clone |
| Native VLAN mismatch messages | vSwitch untagged traffic and switch native VLAN differ | CDP log `%CDP-4-NATIVE_VLAN_MISMATCH` | Align native VLANs (or tag everything) |
| Container works from the host, not from the LAN | Port not published, or host firewall blocks it | `docker ps` (PORTS column) | Publish with `-p`, open the firewall |

### 4.10 Hands-on Lab 4.1 — VMs and containers on your own laptop (Beginner)

**Objective:** 1.2. **PT note:** Packet Tracer cannot run VMs or containers, so this lab uses free tools on your laptop. It is optional for the exam but makes every concept in this chapter concrete.

**You need:** Oracle VirtualBox (free), an Ubuntu Server ISO, about 20 GB of disk and 2 GB of spare RAM. Your laptop is the host; VirtualBox is a **Type 2** hypervisor.

Figure 4.4 shows the three VirtualBox network modes you will test.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch04/fig-04-04-virtualbox-network-modes.svg" alt="Figure 4.4 — VirtualBox NAT, Bridged and Host-only modes with example addresses" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 4.4</b> — VirtualBox NAT, Bridged and Host-only modes with example addresses</sub></p>

In Figure 4.4 the highlighted line is the VM's path. In NAT mode it hides behind the host; in Bridged mode it connects to the home router as if it were a separate PC; in Host-only mode it can reach only the host. The addresses are VirtualBox defaults and a typical home network; yours may differ.

**Part A — Build the VM**

1. In VirtualBox create a VM: Linux / Ubuntu (64-bit), 2 vCPU, 2048 MB RAM, 20 GB virtual disk.
2. Install Ubuntu Server from the ISO, accepting the defaults.
3. Log in and run `ip link show`. The interface (usually `enp0s3`) has a MAC starting **08:00:27** — the VirtualBox OUI from 4.5.

**Part B — NAT mode (the default)**

```
$ ip -4 addr show enp0s3
    inet 10.0.2.15/24 ... dynamic enp0s3
$ ip route
default via 10.0.2.2 dev enp0s3 ...
$ ping -c 2 1.1.1.1          # works if your laptop has Internet
```

From the laptop, try `ping 10.0.2.15`: it fails. The VM is hidden behind VirtualBox's NAT, just like a home PC behind a router.

**Part C — Bridged mode**

1. Power off the VM. In **Settings → Network → Adapter 1** choose **Bridged Adapter** and select the laptop's Wi-Fi or Ethernet card.
2. Start the VM and run `ip -4 addr show enp0s3`. It now has an address from your home router (for example 192.168.1.60).
3. From the laptop, `ping` that address: it succeeds. The VM is a full member of your LAN.
4. If it gets no address, some Wi-Fi and campus networks block extra MAC addresses on one connection; use Ethernet or Host-only mode instead.

**Part D — Host-only mode**

1. In **File → Tools → Network Manager** confirm a host-only network exists (by default 192.168.56.1/24 with DHCP).
2. Set Adapter 1 to **Host-only Adapter** and restart the VM. It receives an address such as 192.168.56.101.
3. The laptop can ping it, but the VM has no Internet. Add a second adapter in NAT mode if it needs updates.

**Part E — Containers inside the VM** (use Bridged mode so you can browse to it)

```
$ sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y docker.io
$ sudo docker run -d --name web -p 8080:80 nginx
$ ip -4 addr show docker0
    inet 172.17.0.1/16 ... docker0
$ sudo docker inspect -f '{{range .NetworkSettings.Networks}}{{.IPAddress}}{{end}}' web
172.17.0.2
$ sudo docker port web
80/tcp -> 0.0.0.0:8080
```

Now open `http://<VM address>:8080` in your laptop's browser: the nginx welcome page appears. You have just reproduced Figure 4.3 — a container on the docker0 bridge, reachable only through the published port. Your VM is the Docker host.

**Troubleshooting**

| Problem | Cause | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bridged VM gets no IP | Network blocks extra MACs, or wrong host adapter selected | Choose the correct adapter; use Ethernet or Host-only |
| `permission denied` on docker commands | User is not in the docker group | Use `sudo`, or `sudo usermod -aG docker $USER` and log in again |
| Browser cannot reach port 8080 | VM in NAT mode, or container not running | Use Bridged mode; check `sudo docker ps` |
| `docker run` cannot download nginx | VM has no Internet (Host-only mode) | Add a NAT adapter or switch to Bridged |

**Questions to answer**

1. Why could the laptop not ping the VM in NAT mode?
2. Which mode makes the VM look like a separate physical PC to your home router?
3. Which address does the outside world use to reach the nginx container: 172.17.0.2 or the VM's address? Why?

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] VM built; VirtualBox OUI 08:00:27 identified
- [ ] NAT, Bridged and Host-only addresses recorded and explained
- [ ] nginx container reached from the laptop through port 8080
- [ ] docker0 bridge address and container address identified

### 4.11 Chapter 4 summary notes (exam revision)

- **Hypervisor** = software that runs VMs and shares hardware among them. **Type 1** runs on bare metal (ESXi, Hyper-V, KVM, Xen); **Type 2** runs on a host OS (VirtualBox, VMware Workstation, Parallels).
- **VM** = full computer in software with its own **guest OS**: strong isolation, gigabytes, slower to start, can run a different OS.
- **Container** = app + libraries sharing the **host kernel**: megabytes, starts in about a second, weaker isolation. Image → container; registry stores images; Docker/containerd run them; Kubernetes orchestrates them.
- **VM networking:** vNIC (own MAC) → port group (VLAN ID) → vSwitch (adds 802.1Q tag) → physical NIC uplinks → **trunk** (often an LACP bundle) to the physical switch.
- **Container networking:** docker0 bridge (172.17.0.0/16 by default), NAT outbound, **published ports** (`-p host:container`) inbound.
- **NFV:** virtual routers and firewalls (Catalyst 8000V, FTDv, ASAv) and virtual WLCs run as VMs.
- **Virtual OUIs:** 00:50:56 VMware, 00:15:5D Hyper-V, 08:00:27 VirtualBox, 52:54:00 KVM.
- **Network impact:** trunks must allow every VM VLAN on every host in the cluster; teaming and switch bundling must match; clones can duplicate IPs.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "Containers are small VMs" | Containers have no guest OS; they share the host's kernel |
| "Type 2 means a second-generation hypervisor" | Type 2 means hosted on an OS; Type 1 means bare metal |
| "A host port should be an access port in the main server VLAN" | It is usually a trunk carrying every VLAN its VMs use |
| "VMs on the same host always talk through the physical switch" | VMs in the same VLAN on one host talk through the vSwitch without leaving the host |

### 4.12 Practice questions — Chapter 4

**1. (Recall)** Which hypervisor is Type 1?

A. Oracle VirtualBox  B. VMware Workstation  C. VMware ESXi  D. Parallels Desktop

**2. (Concept)** What does every virtual machine have that a container does not?

A. An IP address  B. Its own guest operating system kernel  C. A MAC address  D. An application

**3. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which are advantages of containers compared with VMs?

A. Faster start-up  B. Stronger isolation  C. Smaller size  D. Can run a different OS kernel from the host  E. Need no network

**4. (Concept)** What is the role of a virtual switch inside a hypervisor?

**5. (Configuration)** An ESXi host runs VMs in VLANs 10 and 20 through one uplink to SW1 Gi1/0/5. Which switch-port configuration fits?

A. `switchport mode access` and `switchport access vlan 10`  B. `switchport mode trunk` and `switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20`  C. `no switchport` with an IP address  D. `switchport mode access` with `switchport voice vlan 20`

**6. (Output interpretation)** `show mac address-table interface gi1/0/5` lists eight MAC addresses beginning 0050.56, in VLANs 10 and 20. What is most likely connected to Gi1/0/5?

**7. (Troubleshooting)** After a new port group for VLAN 30 is created on an ESXi host, its VMs cannot reach their gateway. VMs in VLANs 10 and 20 work. `show interfaces trunk` on the switch shows `Vlans allowed on trunk: 10,20`. What is the fix?

**8. (Troubleshooting)** A VM works on host ESX-A but loses its network every time it is live-migrated to ESX-B. What should you compare first?

**9. (Troubleshooting)** The switch logs `%SW_MATM-4-MACFLAP_NOTIF` for VM MAC addresses moving between Gi1/0/1 and Gi1/0/2, which connect to the same host. What is the likely cause?

**10. (Container networking)** A Docker container runs nginx on port 80 and was started with `-p 8080:80` on a host at 192.168.1.50. Which URL does a laptop on the LAN use?

A. http://172.17.0.2  B. http://172.17.0.2:8080  C. http://192.168.1.50:8080  D. http://192.168.1.50:80

**11. (Lab-based)** A VirtualBox VM shows address 10.0.2.15 and your laptop cannot ping it. Which adapter mode is it using, and which mode would make it reachable from your home LAN?

**12. (NFV)** Which is an example of network functions virtualization?

A. A Catalyst 9300 switch  B. A Catalyst 8000V router running as a VM in the cloud  C. A Cat6a patch cable  D. A PoE-powered access point

**13. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A network assistant reviewing a ticket says: *"VM-Web in VLAN 10 cannot reach its gateway. Change the ESXi uplink port to `switchport mode access` and `switchport access vlan 10`."* The same host also runs VM-Mgmt in VLAN 99. Is the advice correct?

### 4.13 Answer key with explanations

1. **C.** ESXi runs directly on server hardware. VirtualBox, Workstation and Parallels are Type 2 and run on a host OS.
2. **B.** Each VM boots its own guest OS kernel; containers share the host's kernel. Both have IP and MAC addresses (A, C) and run applications (D).
3. **A and C.** No guest OS means containers start quickly and are small. Isolation is weaker than VMs (B), they must use the host's kernel type (D), and they do need networking (E).
4. It is a software Layer 2 switch that connects VMs to each other and to the host's physical NICs, places each VM's vNIC in a VLAN (port group), and tags frames with 802.1Q on trunk uplinks.
5. **B.** VMs in two VLANs share one uplink, so it must be an 802.1Q trunk allowing both. A would carry only VLAN 10; C makes it a routed port; D is for IP phones.
6. A **VMware ESXi host**: 00:50:56 is the VMware OUI, and many MACs in several VLANs on one port is the signature of virtual machines behind a trunk.
7. Add VLAN 30 to the trunk: `switchport trunk allowed vlan add 30` (and create VLAN 30 on the switch if it does not exist). Using `add` keeps 10 and 20; typing `allowed vlan 30` alone would remove them.
8. Compare the **trunk configuration on both hosts' switch ports**, especially the allowed VLAN list. ESX-B's uplinks probably do not carry the VM's VLAN.
9. The host's **NIC teaming does not match the switch configuration** — for example the host load-balances across two links while the switch treats them as independent ports (or the reverse). Make both sides agree: an LACP bundle on both, or independent links on both.
10. **C.** Outside devices reach the container through the host's own address and the published host port 8080. 172.17.0.2 is private to the host's bridge.
11. It is in **NAT** mode (10.0.2.15 is VirtualBox's default NAT address), so the LAN cannot start connections to it. **Bridged** mode gives it an address on your home LAN.
12. **B.** A router running as software on a general-purpose server or cloud is NFV. The others are physical hardware.
13. **No.** An access port in VLAN 10 would fix VM-Web but cut off VM-Mgmt in VLAN 99. The correct check is whether VLAN 10 is allowed and active on the existing trunk (`show interfaces trunk`), and whether the VM's port group uses VLAN 10. Always test an assistant's suggestion against everything else using that port.

### 4.15 Chapter 4 progress record

**Objective completed:** **1.2** in full — hypervisor types, VMs, containers, virtual switching, container networking, NFV, and the network impact of virtualization.

**Related objectives that reuse this chapter:** 2.2.d switch ports for virtualized hosts (Chapter 7), 2.1.b trunks and 2.1.c LACP (Chapters 5 and 6), 5.3 cloud and automation-based management (Chapter 26).

**Next:** Chapter 5 — objectives **2.1.a, 2.1.b and 2.1.d**: Layer 2/Layer 3 physical interfaces, 802.1Q trunks and switch virtual interfaces (VLANs and inter-VLAN routing).

## Chapter 5 — VLANs, trunks, Layer 2/Layer 3 interfaces and SVIs

**Objectives covered:** **2.1 Configure network infrastructure connectivity (switch-to-switch and switch-to-router)**, specifically **2.1.a Layer 2/Layer 3 physical interfaces**, **2.1.b Layer 2 802.1Q trunk interfaces** and **2.1.d Switch virtual interface (SVI)**. 2.1.c (EtherChannel) follows in Chapter 6.

This is the first big *configure* chapter. Every later switching topic — EtherChannel, edge ports, STP, Layer 2 security — sits on top of the VLANs and trunks you build here.

### 5.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Explain why VLANs exist and how they split one switch into several broadcast domains.
2. Create VLANs and assign Layer 2 access ports.
3. Configure and verify 802.1Q trunks, including the native VLAN and the allowed VLAN list, and predict DTP results.
4. Convert a switch port into a Layer 3 routed port.
5. Configure SVIs for management and for inter-VLAN routing on a multilayer switch, and configure router-on-a-stick.
6. Troubleshoot VLAN, trunk and SVI problems from show-command output.

### 5.2 Introduction

Without VLANs, every device on a switch shares one broadcast domain. In a 300-user college building, every ARP request and DHCP Discover reaches every PC, printer and phone; a guest laptop sits on the same network as the accounts office. **VLANs (virtual LANs)** let you group ports logically — Staff, Wireless, Printers, Management — regardless of which switch they are on, and keep each group's broadcasts and traffic separate. Moving traffic **between** VLANs then requires a router or Layer 3 switch, which is where security policy can be applied.

### 5.3 VLAN concepts

**A VLAN is a broadcast domain** (Figure 5.1). A switch forwards a frame only to ports in the same VLAN. Each VLAN normally maps to one IP subnet; this guide uses 10.10.**VLAN**.0/24.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch05/fig-05-01-vlans-broadcast-domains.svg" alt="Figure 5.1 — Three VLANs on one switch: a broadcast stays inside its own VLAN" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 5.1</b> — Three VLANs on one switch: a broadcast stays inside its own VLAN</sub></p>

In Figure 5.1 the dashed boxes are VLAN memberships, not separate hardware. The arrows trace PC1's broadcast: SW1 floods it only to PC2, the one other port in VLAN 10. PC3, PC4 and the printer never see it, even though they are on the same switch.

| VLAN range | Use |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Default VLAN: every port starts in it; cannot be deleted. Also the default native VLAN |
| 2–1001 | Normal range: user VLANs |
| 1002–1005 | Reserved for legacy Token Ring and FDDI; cannot be deleted |
| 1006–4094 | Extended range (needs VTP transparent mode or VTPv3 on older switches) |
| 0 and 4095 | Reserved, never used |

**Benefits:** smaller broadcast domains, security separation (a firewall or ACL controls inter-VLAN traffic), logical grouping across buildings, and easier management (move a user by changing one port).

**Best practices used in this guide:**

- Do not put users in VLAN 1. Use a dedicated **management VLAN** (99).
- Use an unused VLAN as the trunk **native VLAN** (999), not VLAN 1.
- Shut down unused ports and put them in an unused "parking" VLAN.
- Allow only the VLANs you need on each trunk.

**About VTP.** VLAN Trunking Protocol can copy VLAN lists between switches, but it is not a v2.0 objective and a mistake can delete VLANs network-wide. This guide creates VLANs on each switch by hand (VTP transparent or off).

### 5.4 Creating VLANs and Layer 2 access ports (2.1.a)

A **Layer 2 interface** (a *switchport*) forwards frames by MAC address within a VLAN. An **access port** belongs to exactly one data VLAN and sends and receives **untagged** frames; it connects end devices.

**Step 1 — create and name the VLANs** (global configuration, then VLAN configuration mode):

```
SW1(config)# vlan 10
SW1(config-vlan)# name STAFF
SW1(config-vlan)# vlan 30
SW1(config-vlan)# name WIRELESS
SW1(config-vlan)# vlan 40
SW1(config-vlan)# name PRINTERS
SW1(config-vlan)# vlan 99
SW1(config-vlan)# name MGMT
SW1(config-vlan)# vlan 999
SW1(config-vlan)# name NATIVE
SW1(config-vlan)# exit
```

**Step 2 — make the port an access port and assign the VLAN:**

```
SW1(config)# interface fastethernet0/1
SW1(config-if)# description PC1 - Staff
SW1(config-if)# switchport mode access
SW1(config-if)# switchport access vlan 10
SW1(config-if)# exit
SW1(config)# interface range fastethernet0/3 - 24
SW1(config-if-range)# shutdown                      ! unused ports off
```

| Command | Mode | Purpose | Notes and common mistakes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `vlan <id>` | Global config | Creates the VLAN and enters VLAN config mode | Assigning a port to a VLAN that does not exist creates it automatically on most switches, but naming it is still good practice |
| `name <text>` | VLAN config | Names the VLAN | Names are local labels; they do not need to match between switches (but should) |
| `switchport mode access` | Interface config | Forces access mode and disables trunk negotiation (DTP) | Leaving the default dynamic mode can accidentally form a trunk |
| `switchport access vlan <id>` | Interface config | Puts the port in that VLAN | Applies only while the port is in access mode |
| `interface range fa0/3 - 24` | Global config | Configures many ports at once | Spaces around the hyphen are required on some IOS versions |
| `no vlan <id>` | Global config | Deletes a VLAN | Ports assigned to it stay assigned but become **inactive** and pass no traffic until the VLAN is recreated |

**Verification:**

```
SW1# show vlan brief
VLAN Name                             Status    Ports
---- -------------------------------- --------- ------------------------------
1    default                          active    Fa0/3, Fa0/4, Fa0/5, Fa0/6 ...
10   STAFF                            active    Fa0/1
30   WIRELESS                         active    Fa0/2
40   PRINTERS                         active
99   MGMT                             active
999  NATIVE                           active
1002 fddi-default                     act/unsup
...
```

**Exam tip:** trunk ports do **not** appear in `show vlan brief`; only access ports are listed. A port "missing" from every VLAN is probably a trunk.

```
SW1# show interfaces fastethernet0/1 switchport
Name: Fa0/1
Switchport: Enabled
Administrative Mode: static access
Operational Mode: static access
Access Mode VLAN: 10 (STAFF)
Trunking Native Mode VLAN: 1 (default)
Voice VLAN: none
...
```

`Administrative Mode` is what you configured; `Operational Mode` is what the port is actually doing. When they differ, negotiation (DTP) or an error is involved.

### 5.5 802.1Q trunks (2.1.b)

When VLAN 10 exists on two switches, the link between them must carry VLAN 10 — and VLAN 30, 40 and 99 too. Rather than one cable per VLAN, a **trunk** carries many VLANs over one link by **tagging** each frame with its VLAN ID. IEEE **802.1Q** is the standard (Cisco's old ISL is obsolete).

**The 802.1Q tag (Figure 5.2).** The sending switch inserts 4 bytes between the source MAC and the Type field:

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch05/fig-05-02-dot1q-tag.svg" alt="Figure 5.2 — 802.1Q tag position in the Ethernet frame and its four fields" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 5.2</b> — 802.1Q tag position in the Ethernet frame and its four fields</sub></p>

Figure 5.2 zooms into the highlighted tag from Figure 1.3's frame. The 12-bit VID is what tells the receiving switch which VLAN the frame belongs to; the 3-bit PCP is reused for voice priority in Chapter 7.

| Field | Size | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| TPID | 16 bits | Always 0x8100: "this frame is tagged" |
| PCP | 3 bits | Priority (Class of Service) 0–7; IP phones use 5 for voice |
| DEI | 1 bit | Drop eligible indicator (formerly CFI) |
| VID | 12 bits | VLAN ID: 1–4094 usable |

Because the frame changes, the switch recalculates the FCS. The maximum frame size grows from 1518 to 1522 bytes.

**How a frame crosses a trunk (Figure 5.3):**

1. PC1 sends an ordinary, untagged frame into its access port on SW1 (VLAN 10).
2. SW1 decides the destination is reachable through the trunk Gi0/1 and inserts an 802.1Q tag with VID 10.
3. SW2 receives the tagged frame, reads VID 10, and forwards it only to VLAN 10 ports.
4. SW2 removes the tag before sending the frame out the access port to PC3, which never sees a tag.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch05/fig-05-03-frame-across-trunk.svg" alt="Figure 5.3 — A VLAN 10 frame crossing an 802.1Q trunk between SW1 and SW2" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 5.3</b> — A VLAN 10 frame crossing an 802.1Q trunk between SW1 and SW2</sub></p>

Figure 5.3 matches the four steps above. Arrows show the direction of travel; the thick highlighted link is the trunk. The tag exists only on the trunk, which is why end devices on access ports never need to know VLANs exist.

**The native VLAN.** Frames in the trunk's **native VLAN** cross the link **untagged**, and untagged frames received on a trunk are placed in the native VLAN. The default is VLAN 1. Both ends must use the same native VLAN; otherwise traffic leaks between VLANs and CDP reports `%CDP-4-NATIVE_VLAN_MISMATCH`. Best practice: set it to an unused VLAN (999) on both ends.

**Configuring a trunk:**

```
SW1(config)# interface gigabitethernet0/1
SW1(config-if)# description Trunk to DSW1 Gi1/0/1
SW1(config-if)# switchport trunk encapsulation dot1q    ! only on switches that also support ISL
SW1(config-if)# switchport mode trunk
SW1(config-if)# switchport trunk native vlan 999
SW1(config-if)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
SW1(config-if)# switchport nonegotiate                   ! stop sending DTP
```

| Command | Purpose | Notes and common mistakes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `switchport trunk encapsulation dot1q` | Chooses 802.1Q | Needed only on platforms that support ISL (for example Catalyst 3560); if `switchport mode trunk` is rejected with "encapsulation is Auto", add this first. Catalyst 2960 and many newer switches support only 802.1Q and do not have the command |
| `switchport mode trunk` | Forces trunking | Both ends should be configured as trunks |
| `switchport trunk native vlan 999` | Sets the untagged VLAN | Must match on both ends |
| `switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99` | Replaces the allowed list | Typing `allowed vlan 50` later **replaces** the list with only 50 |
| `switchport trunk allowed vlan add 50` | Adds one VLAN to the list | The safe way to add; also `remove 50`, `except 50`, `all`, `none` |
| `switchport nonegotiate` | Disables DTP frames | Use on manually configured trunks and access ports |

**DTP (Dynamic Trunking Protocol).** A Cisco-proprietary protocol that lets ports negotiate trunking. Its modes and the result for each pair:

| Side A \\ Side B | access | trunk | dynamic desirable | dynamic auto |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **access** | Access | Mismatch (do not do this) | Access | Access |
| **trunk** | Mismatch | Trunk | Trunk | Trunk |
| **dynamic desirable** | Access | Trunk | Trunk | Trunk |
| **dynamic auto** | Access | Trunk | Trunk | **Access** |

The classic exam trap: two ports left at **dynamic auto** (a common default) both wait to be asked and never form a trunk. Best practice is to hard-code `switchport mode trunk` or `switchport mode access` and disable DTP, because an attacker's PC can otherwise negotiate a trunk (VLAN hopping).

### 5.6 Layer 3 routed ports on a switch (2.1.a)

A **multilayer (Layer 3) switch** such as a Catalyst 3650 or 9300 can also route. Any physical port can be turned into a **routed port**: it stops being a switchport, belongs to no VLAN, and takes an IP address like a router interface. Routed ports are used for switch-to-router and switch-to-switch links in routed campus designs.

```
DSW1(config)# ip routing                              ! enable IPv4 routing on the switch
DSW1(config)# interface gigabitethernet1/0/24
DSW1(config-if)# description Routed link to R1 G0/0/0
DSW1(config-if)# no switchport                        ! Layer 2 -> Layer 3
DSW1(config-if)# ip address 10.0.0.2 255.255.255.252
DSW1(config-if)# no shutdown
```

|  | Layer 2 switchport | Layer 3 routed port |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Command | `switchport` (default on Catalyst switches) | `no switchport` |
| Belongs to | A VLAN (access) or many VLANs (trunk) | No VLAN |
| Addressing | None on the port | IP address on the port |
| Typical link | To end devices, to other switches | To a router, or between Layer 3 switches |
| Spanning tree | Participates | Does not run STP on that port |

**`ip routing` is off by default on many Layer 3 switches.** Without it, SVIs and routed ports still get addresses, but the switch will not route between them — a very common lab fault.

### 5.7 Switch virtual interfaces and inter-VLAN routing (2.1.d)

A **switch virtual interface (SVI)** is a logical Layer 3 interface for a VLAN: `interface vlan 10`. It gives the switch an IP address *inside* that VLAN. SVIs have two uses.

**1. Management SVI on a Layer 2 switch.** An access switch needs one IP address so you can reach it with SSH, SNMP and syslog:

```
SW1(config)# interface vlan 99
SW1(config-if)# description Management
SW1(config-if)# ip address 10.10.99.11 255.255.255.0
SW1(config-if)# no shutdown
SW1(config-if)# exit
SW1(config)# ip default-gateway 10.10.99.1          ! a Layer 2 switch is a host: it needs a gateway
```

**2. Gateway SVIs on a multilayer switch (inter-VLAN routing).** Each VLAN's SVI is the default gateway for that VLAN, and the switch routes between SVIs internally:

```
DSW1(config)# ip routing
DSW1(config)# interface vlan 10
DSW1(config-if)# ip address 10.10.10.1 255.255.255.0
DSW1(config-if)# no shutdown
DSW1(config-if)# interface vlan 30
DSW1(config-if)# ip address 10.10.30.1 255.255.255.0
DSW1(config-if)# no shutdown
```

**When is an SVI up/up?** All of these must be true:

1. The VLAN exists in the switch's VLAN database and is active.
2. At least one port in that VLAN (an access port, or a trunk allowing it) is up and in the STP forwarding state.
3. The SVI is not administratively shut down (`no shutdown`; SVIs start shut on many switches).

This "autostate" behaviour is why an SVI shows `up/down` when its VLAN has no active ports.

**Comparing inter-VLAN routing methods (Figure 5.4).**

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch05/fig-05-04-inter-vlan-routing.svg" alt="Figure 5.4 — Inter-VLAN routing: router-on-a-stick (left) vs multilayer switch SVIs (right)" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 5.4</b> — Inter-VLAN routing: router-on-a-stick (left) vs multilayer switch SVIs (right)</sub></p>

The highlighted arrows in Figure 5.4 trace a packet from a VLAN 10 host to a VLAN 30 host. On the left it travels up the trunk to R1 in VLAN 10 and comes back down the same trunk in VLAN 30. On the right it is routed between the two SVIs inside DSW1 and never leaves the switch.

| Method | How | Pros | Cons |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Router-on-a-stick | One router interface, one 802.1Q subinterface per VLAN, trunk to the switch | Cheap; works with a Layer 2 switch | Single link carries traffic twice (hairpin); router CPU and link become bottlenecks |
| Multilayer switch SVIs | One SVI per VLAN, `ip routing` | Hardware speed, scales, no extra link | Needs a Layer 3 switch |
| Separate router interface per VLAN (legacy) | One physical router port per VLAN, access links | Simple | Wastes ports; does not scale |

**Router-on-a-stick configuration** (the switch side is a normal trunk):

```
R1(config)# interface gigabitethernet0/0/1
R1(config-if)# no shutdown                          ! enable the physical interface
R1(config-if)# interface gigabitethernet0/0/1.10
R1(config-subif)# description Gateway VLAN 10
R1(config-subif)# encapsulation dot1Q 10
R1(config-subif)# ip address 10.10.10.1 255.255.255.0
R1(config-subif)# interface gigabitethernet0/0/1.30
R1(config-subif)# encapsulation dot1Q 30
R1(config-subif)# ip address 10.10.30.1 255.255.255.0
R1(config-subif)# interface gigabitethernet0/0/1.999
R1(config-subif)# encapsulation dot1Q 999 native   ! match the switch's native VLAN
```

The subinterface number (.10) is only a label; the **`encapsulation dot1Q 10`** command is what ties it to VLAN 10. Making them match is good practice.

**Packet walk: PC1 (VLAN 10) to PC2 (VLAN 30) through DSW1 SVIs.**

1. PC1 sees 10.10.30.11 is in another subnet, so it ARPs for its gateway 10.10.10.1 and sends the frame to the VLAN 10 SVI's MAC.
2. The frame arrives on DSW1 in VLAN 10 (tagged on the trunk from SW1, or untagged on an access port).
3. DSW1 routes the packet: destination 10.10.30.0/24 is connected via `Vlan30`.
4. DSW1 ARPs for PC2 in VLAN 30 if needed, builds a new frame, and sends it out the VLAN 30 port or trunk.
5. PC2 receives it from the SVI's MAC as the source. The IP addresses never changed; the MAC addresses did (Chapter 1, Figure 1.2).

### 5.8 Verifying VLANs, trunks and SVIs

```
DSW1# show interfaces trunk

Port        Mode         Encapsulation  Status        Native vlan
Gi1/0/1     on           802.1q         trunking      999
Gi1/0/2     on           802.1q         trunking      999

Port        Vlans allowed on trunk
Gi1/0/1     10,30,40,99
Gi1/0/2     10,30,40,99

Port        Vlans allowed and active in management domain
Gi1/0/1     10,30,40,99
Gi1/0/2     10,30,40,99

Port        Vlans in spanning tree forwarding state and not pruned
Gi1/0/1     10,30,40,99
Gi1/0/2     10,30,40,99
```

Read the four blocks in order: (1) is it trunking, with which native VLAN; (2) which VLANs are **allowed**; (3) which of those **exist and are active** on this switch; (4) which are actually **forwarding** (not blocked by STP). A VLAN that is allowed but missing from block 3 has not been created on this switch.

```
DSW1# show ip interface brief | include Vlan|1/0/24
Vlan10                 10.10.10.1      YES manual up                    up
Vlan30                 10.10.30.1      YES manual up                    up
Vlan40                 10.10.40.1      YES manual up                    up
Vlan99                 10.10.99.1      YES manual up                    up
GigabitEthernet1/0/24  10.0.0.2        YES manual up                    up
```

| Command | Use it to check |
| --- | --- |
| `show vlan brief` | VLANs exist, names, access-port membership (trunks not listed) |
| `show interfaces <int> switchport` | Administrative vs operational mode, access VLAN, native VLAN, allowed VLANs |
| `show interfaces trunk` | Trunk status, native VLAN, allowed/active/forwarding VLANs |
| `show interfaces status` | Port VLAN column (`trunk` or a VLAN number), link status |
| `show ip interface brief` | SVIs and routed ports: address and up/up |
| `show ip route connected` | One C route per SVI/routed port subnet (needs `ip routing`) |
| `show mac address-table vlan 10` | Which MACs were learned in VLAN 10 and on which ports |

### 5.9 Troubleshooting VLANs, trunks and SVIs

Work from the host outward, as in Figure 5.5.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch05/fig-05-05-vlan-troubleshooting.svg" alt="Figure 5.5 — VLAN, trunk and gateway troubleshooting flowchart" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 5.5</b> — VLAN, trunk and gateway troubleshooting flowchart</sub></p>

Figure 5.5 assumes the host's IP settings are already correct (Figure 2.6). Each check moves one hop further from the host: its own port, the VLAN database, the trunks between switches, and finally the Layer 3 gateway.

| Symptom | Likely cause | Evidence | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Host cannot reach anyone, link is up | Port in the wrong VLAN | `show vlan brief` / `show interfaces switchport` | `switchport access vlan <correct>` |
| Hosts in a VLAN lost connectivity after a change | VLAN deleted, ports now inactive | `show interfaces switchport` shows "Inactive" | Recreate the VLAN |
| Same-VLAN hosts on two switches cannot talk | VLAN not allowed or not created on one side, or the link is not trunking | `show interfaces trunk` | Add the VLAN with `allowed vlan add`; create it; hard-code `mode trunk` |
| Link between switches is an access link | Both ends dynamic auto, or one end access | `show interfaces switchport`: operational mode static access | Set `switchport mode trunk` on both ends |
| `%CDP-4-NATIVE_VLAN_MISMATCH` and odd leaks | Native VLAN differs per end | `show interfaces trunk` on both switches | Same `native vlan` on both ends |
| SVI is up/down | VLAN missing, no active port in the VLAN, or not allowed on any trunk | `show vlan brief`, `show interfaces trunk` | Create the VLAN, connect/enable a port, allow it on the uplink |
| Hosts reach their gateway but not other VLANs | `ip routing` missing on the multilayer switch | `show ip route` returns "routing not enabled" or no C routes | `ip routing` |
| Router-on-a-stick: one VLAN fails | Wrong `encapsulation dot1Q` number, missing subinterface IP, or VLAN not allowed on the switch trunk | `show ip interface brief`, `show running-config interface g0/0/1.30` | Correct the VLAN ID or trunk |
| Management SVI unreachable from other subnets | No `ip default-gateway` on the Layer 2 switch | `show running-config \| include default-gateway` | Add the gateway |

```
hostname SW3
vlan 10

vlan 30

vlan 999

**Break it:** change `encapsulation dot1Q 30` to `encapsulation dot1Q 3` on G0/0/1.30. PC2 can no longer reach its gateway, although every interface is still up/up. *Lesson:* the subinterface number is a label; the encapsulation VLAN is what matters.

Load Lab 5.1, then apply these five changes on a copy without reading the answers. Each one produces a different symptom.

```

no ip routing

interface vlan 30

interface gigabitethernet0/1

**Answer key (hide until done)**

| Fault | Symptom | Found with | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `no ip routing` on DSW1 | No inter-VLAN traffic; hosts still ping their own gateway | `show ip route` (no routing table) | `ip routing` |
| VLAN 40 removed from the DSW1–SW2 trunk | Printer cannot reach its gateway | `show interfaces trunk` on DSW1 | `switchport trunk allowed vlan add 40` |
| Vlan30 SVI shut down | Wireless hosts cannot reach their gateway | `show ip interface brief` (administratively down) | `no shutdown` |
| Native VLAN 1 on SW1, 999 on DSW1 | `%CDP-4-NATIVE_VLAN_MISMATCH` messages | `show interfaces trunk` on both ends | `switchport trunk native vlan 999` |

### 5.13 Chapter 5 summary notes (exam revision)

- **VLAN = broadcast domain = (usually) one subnet.** Ranges: 1 default; 2–1001 normal; 1002–1005 reserved; 1006–4094 extended.
- **Access port:** `switchport mode access`, `switchport access vlan <id>`; untagged; one VLAN. Deleting a VLAN leaves its ports **inactive**.
- **Trunk:** `switchport mode trunk` (+ `switchport trunk encapsulation dot1q` on ISL-capable switches), `native vlan`, `allowed vlan` — use **`add`** to append. 802.1Q tag = 4 bytes: TPID 0x8100, PCP 3 bits, DEI 1 bit, **VID 12 bits**.
- **Native VLAN:** untagged on the trunk; must match both ends; default VLAN 1; best practice an unused VLAN.
- **DTP:** auto + auto = access; desirable + auto/desirable/trunk = trunk. Hard-code modes and use `switchport nonegotiate`.
- **`show vlan brief` lists access ports only; trunks appear in `show interfaces trunk`** (allowed → active → forwarding).
- **Routed port:** `no switchport` + `ip address`; no VLAN, no STP. **Multilayer switches need `ip routing`.**
- **SVI:** `interface vlan <id>`; up/up only if the VLAN exists, has an active forwarding port, and the SVI is `no shutdown`. Layer 2 switches need `ip default-gateway` for their management SVI.
- **Router-on-a-stick:** subinterface + `encapsulation dot1Q <vlan>` (+ `native` for the native VLAN); the physical interface must be `no shutdown`.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "`allowed vlan 50` adds VLAN 50" | It **replaces** the list; use `allowed vlan add 50` |
| "A trunk port is missing from `show vlan brief`, so it is broken" | Trunks are never listed there |
| "The subinterface number sets the VLAN" | `encapsulation dot1Q <id>` sets it |
| "An SVI with an IP is always up" | It needs an active, forwarding port in its VLAN |
| "A Layer 3 switch routes by default" | Many need `ip routing` first |

### 5.14 Practice questions — Chapter 5

**1. (Recall)** What is the default native VLAN on a Cisco 802.1Q trunk?

**2. (DTP)** Two connected switch ports are both left in `dynamic auto` mode. What is the result?

A. Trunk  B. Access link  C. err-disabled  D. EtherChannel

**3. (Output interpretation)**

```
Port        Vlans allowed on trunk
Gi0/1       10,20,99
Port        Vlans allowed and active in management domain
Gi0/1       10,99
```

Hosts in VLAN 20 cannot reach the other switch. What is the most likely cause?

**4. (Configuration)** Gi0/1 is a trunk allowing VLANs 10 and 30. Which command adds VLAN 50 without removing the others?

A. `switchport trunk allowed vlan 50`  B. `switchport trunk allowed vlan add 50`  C. `switchport access vlan 50`  D. `vlan 50 trunk`

**5. (Recall)** Which ports are listed by `show vlan brief`?

A. Access ports only  B. Trunk ports only  C. Both  D. Routed ports

**6. (Troubleshooting)** `interface vlan 40` shows `up/down`. VLAN 40 exists. What is the most likely reason?

**7. (Troubleshooting)** On a multilayer switch with SVIs for VLANs 10 and 30, hosts can ping their own gateway but not hosts in the other VLAN. What is missing?

**8. (Configuration)** Write the router-on-a-stick configuration on R1 G0/0/1 for VLAN 30 with gateway 10.10.30.1/24.

**9. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which fields are part of the 802.1Q tag?

A. TPID  B. TTL  C. VID  D. Checksum  E. Source port

**10. (Troubleshooting)** SW1's management SVI (VLAN 99, 10.10.99.11) answers pings from hosts in VLAN 99 but not from VLAN 10. What is missing on SW1?

**11. (Log interpretation)** What does `%CDP-4-NATIVE_VLAN_MISMATCH` tell you, and why does it matter?

**12. (Configuration)** Which command turns a Catalyst switch port into a Layer 3 routed port?

A. `ip routing`  B. `no switchport`  C. `switchport mode routed`  D. `interface vlan 1`

**13. (Recall)** VLAN 1500 belongs to which range?

A. Normal  B. Reserved  C. Extended  D. Invalid

**14. (Scenario)** An administrator deletes VLAN 30 by mistake. What happens to the access ports that were in VLAN 30?

**15. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A trunk allows VLANs 10, 30 and 99, and the printer VLAN 40 is missing. A network assistant suggests: *"Run `switchport trunk allowed vlan 40` on the trunk."* Is this safe?

### 5.15 Answer key with explanations

1. **VLAN 1.** Best practice is to change it to an unused VLAN on both ends.
2. **B.** Both sides wait for the other to start negotiating, so no trunk forms and the link runs as an access link.
3. VLAN 20 is **allowed but not active**: it has not been created on this switch (or is suspended). Create it with `vlan 20`.
4. **B.** A replaces the list with only VLAN 50, cutting off 10 and 30. C and D are not trunk commands.
5. **A.** Trunks are shown by `show interfaces trunk`; routed ports belong to no VLAN.
6. No port in VLAN 40 is **up and forwarding** (no access port connected, or VLAN 40 not allowed or forwarding on any trunk), so autostate keeps the SVI's line protocol down.
7. **`ip routing`.** Each SVI works as a gateway, but without IP routing the switch will not forward packets between them.
8. `interface g0/0/1` → `no shutdown`; `interface g0/0/1.30` → `encapsulation dot1Q 30` → `ip address 10.10.30.1 255.255.255.0`. The switch port facing R1 must be a trunk allowing VLAN 30.
9. **A and C.** TPID (0x8100) and VID are tag fields; PCP and DEI are the others. TTL and checksum belong to IP; source port belongs to TCP/UDP.
10. **`ip default-gateway 10.10.99.1`.** A Layer 2 switch is like a host: without a gateway it can answer only devices in its own subnet.
11. The two ends of a trunk use different native VLANs. Untagged frames from one VLAN arrive in a different VLAN on the other side, causing leaks and broken connectivity. Set the same native VLAN on both ends.
12. **B.** `ip routing` enables routing globally; A alone does not change a port. C does not exist; D creates an SVI.
13. **C.** 1006–4094 is the extended range.
14. The ports keep their `switchport access vlan 30` setting but become **inactive** and pass no traffic until VLAN 30 is recreated.
15. **No.** Without `add`, the command replaces the allowed list with only VLAN 40 and cuts off VLANs 10, 30 and 99. The safe command is `switchport trunk allowed vlan add 40`. Always check whether a suggested command replaces or appends.

| Figure | Title | Section | Status |

### 5.17 Chapter 5 progress record

**Objectives completed:** **2.1.a** Layer 2/Layer 3 physical interfaces, **2.1.b** 802.1Q trunks, **2.1.d** SVIs, plus VLAN fundamentals and inter-VLAN routing.

## Chapter 6 — EtherChannel with LACP

**Objective covered:** **2.1.c Configure Layer 2/Layer 3 LACP port-channel/EtherChannel** (switch-to-switch and switch-to-router connectivity, objective 2.1). LACP bundles for servers and virtualized hosts (2.2) reuse this chapter in Chapter 7.

### 6.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Explain why parallel links need EtherChannel, and how a port-channel interface works.
2. Describe per-flow load balancing and fast failover.
3. Compare LACP, PAgP and static mode, and predict which mode pairs form a bundle.
4. List the settings that must match on every member link.
5. Configure and verify Layer 2 (trunk) and Layer 3 (routed) EtherChannels with LACP.
6. Troubleshoot suspended, stand-alone and err-disabled members.

### 6.2 Introduction: two cables are not automatically twice the bandwidth

In Lab 5.1, SW1 has one 1 Gb/s uplink to DSW1. If you add a second cable for more bandwidth and redundancy, **Spanning Tree Protocol** (Chapter 9) sees a loop and **blocks** one of the links (Figure 6.1, left). You pay for two links but use one. **EtherChannel** bundles up to eight parallel physical links into one **logical** link called a **port-channel** (Po). STP, the MAC table and routing all see a single interface, so every member forwards traffic (Figure 6.1, right). If a member fails, traffic shifts to the survivors in well under a second without any STP recalculation.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch06/fig-06-01-stp-vs-etherchannel.svg" alt="Figure 6.1 — Two parallel links with STP (left) vs the same links bundled as EtherChannel Po1 (right)" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 6.1</b> — Two parallel links with STP (left) vs the same links bundled as EtherChannel Po1 (right)</sub></p>

In Figure 6.1 the cabling is identical on both sides; only the configuration differs. On the left, STP must block the red dashed link to prevent a loop. On the right, the ellipse marks the bundle: STP treats Po1 as one port, so both highlighted links carry traffic.

### 6.3 How EtherChannel works

1. You add physical ports to a **channel group** with `channel-group <n> mode <mode>`.
2. The switch creates **interface port-channel \<n>**, the logical interface you configure and monitor.
3. A negotiation protocol (LACP or PAgP) checks that the far end agrees and that the members are compatible.
4. Compatible members become **bundled**; the port-channel comes up.
5. Each frame is assigned to one member by a **hash** of its addresses (6.4), so one conversation always uses one link.

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| EtherChannel | Cisco's name for link aggregation |
| Port-channel (Po) | The logical interface representing the bundle |
| Member (bundled) port | A physical interface inside the bundle |
| Channel group | The number that ties members to their port-channel (Po1 = group 1) |
| LACP | Link Aggregation Control Protocol, IEEE 802.3ad (now 802.1AX), open standard |
| PAgP | Port Aggregation Protocol, Cisco proprietary (recognise it; v2.0 names only LACP) |

### 6.4 Load balancing: per flow, not per packet

EtherChannel does **not** split one transfer across all links. The switch runs a **hash** on fields of each frame — source and/or destination MAC, IP address or port — and the result picks one member. Every frame of the same flow produces the same hash, so it always uses the same link and arrives in order (Figure 6.2).

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch06/fig-06-02-per-flow-load-balancing.svg" alt="Figure 6.2 — Per-flow load balancing across the two members of Po1" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 6.2</b> — Per-flow load balancing across the two members of Po1</sub></p>

In Figure 6.2 the highlighted flows hash to member Gi0/1 and the grey flows to Gi0/2. Which flow lands on which link depends on the hash result, not on the order the flows started.

- One large file copy between two hosts is limited to **one member's speed** (1 Gb/s on a 2 × 1 Gb/s bundle).
- Many flows between many hosts spread across all members.
- If traffic is uneven, choose a hash with more variety. Between Layer 3 devices, `src-dst-ip` usually spreads better than `src-mac` (all routed frames share the router's MAC).

```
DSW1(config)# port-channel load-balance src-dst-ip      ! global; applies to all bundles
DSW1# show etherchannel load-balance
EtherChannel Load-Balancing Configuration:
        src-dst-ip
```

The default method and the available options vary by platform. Each end chooses its own method independently; they do not have to match.

### 6.5 Negotiation: LACP, PAgP or static

| Mode | Protocol | Behaviour |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `active` | LACP | Sends LACP packets and starts negotiation |
| `passive` | LACP | Answers LACP but never starts it |
| `desirable` | PAgP | Sends PAgP and starts negotiation |
| `auto` | PAgP | Answers PAgP but never starts it |
| `on` | None | Bundles unconditionally, no negotiation |

**Which pairs form a bundle?**

| Side A \\ Side B | active | passive | on | desirable / auto |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **active** | Yes | Yes | No | No (different protocols) |
| **passive** | Yes | **No** — both wait | No | No |
| **on** | No | No | Yes | No |

The same logic applies to PAgP: desirable + desirable or desirable + auto bundle; auto + auto does not.

**Best practice:** use LACP `active` on both ends. It is an open standard (works with servers, hypervisors and other vendors), detects mis-cabling, and removes a member that stops talking LACP. Avoid `on`: if one end bundles and the other does not, you can create a forwarding loop.

**LACP details worth knowing:** LACP can manage up to **16** links per bundle, of which **8** are active and up to 8 are hot-standby. Each side has a **system priority** (lower wins and decides which links are active) and each port a **port priority**; defaults are 32768.

### 6.6 Member requirements

All members of one EtherChannel must match **each other** and the **far end**, or they are **suspended** from the bundle:

| Must match | Example of a mismatch |
| --- | --- |
| Speed and duplex | One member hard-coded 100 Mb/s |
| Switchport type | One member Layer 2, another `no switchport` |
| Switchport mode | One member access, another trunk |
| Access VLAN (access bundles) | VLAN 10 on one port, VLAN 30 on another |
| Trunk native VLAN and allowed VLAN list (trunk bundles) | Allowed 10,30 on one member, 10,30,40 on another |
| Same channel-group number and compatible mode | Gi1/0/1 in group 1, Gi1/0/3 in group 2 |

The channel-group **number** is local: Po1 on DSW1 may connect to Po5 on SW1. Matching numbers is simply good documentation.

### 6.7 Configuring a Layer 2 EtherChannel (trunk)

Figure 6.3 (left) shows the result. Configure the **same** on both switches.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch06/fig-06-03-l2-vs-l3-etherchannel.svg" alt="Figure 6.3 — Layer 2 trunk EtherChannel (Po1) vs Layer 3 routed EtherChannel (Po3)" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 6.3</b> — Layer 2 trunk EtherChannel (Po1) vs Layer 3 routed EtherChannel (Po3)</sub></p>

Both panels of Figure 6.3 bundle two members with LACP; the difference is what the port-channel is. Po1 on the left is a trunk configured with switchport commands; Po3 on the right is a routed interface with an IP address at each end. Both appear again in Lab 6.1.

```
DSW1(config)# interface range gigabitethernet1/0/1 , gigabitethernet1/0/3
DSW1(config-if-range)# description Po1 members to SW1
DSW1(config-if-range)# switchport mode trunk
DSW1(config-if-range)# switchport trunk native vlan 999
DSW1(config-if-range)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
DSW1(config-if-range)# channel-group 1 mode active       ! creates Po1 and starts LACP
Creating a port-channel interface Port-channel 1
DSW1(config-if-range)# exit
DSW1(config)# interface port-channel 1
DSW1(config-if)# description Po1 to SW1
DSW1(config-if)# switchport mode trunk
DSW1(config-if)# switchport trunk native vlan 999
DSW1(config-if)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
```

**Order and inheritance.** Configure the members identically first, then add `channel-group`. After the bundle exists, make Layer 2 changes on **interface port-channel 1**; IOS copies them to every member, which keeps members consistent. Changing one member directly is the usual way to get it suspended.

| Command | Mode | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `channel-group <n> mode active` | Interface (member) | Adds the port to group n using LACP active; creates Po n if needed |
| `channel-group <n> mode passive` | Interface (member) | LACP, waits for the other side |
| `channel-group <n> mode on` | Interface (member) | Static bundle, no LACP (avoid) |
| `interface port-channel <n>` | Global config | Configures the logical interface |
| `port-channel load-balance <method>` | Global config | Sets the hash method |
| `lacp system-priority <n>` / `lacp port-priority <n>` | Global / interface | Influence which links are active when more than 8 |

### 6.8 Configuring a Layer 3 EtherChannel (routed)

Between two Layer 3 switches (or a Layer 3 switch and a router) the bundle can be a routed link with one IP address (Figure 6.3, right). The members must be routed ports **before** they join the group, so the port-channel is created as Layer 3.

```
DSW1(config)# interface range gigabitethernet1/0/21 - 22
DSW1(config-if-range)# description Po3 members to DSW2
DSW1(config-if-range)# no switchport                       ! first: make members Layer 3
DSW1(config-if-range)# channel-group 3 mode active
DSW1(config-if-range)# exit
DSW1(config)# interface port-channel 3
DSW1(config-if)# no switchport                             ! confirm the bundle is Layer 3
DSW1(config-if)# ip address 10.0.0.5 255.255.255.252
DSW1(config-if)# no shutdown
```

DSW2 is the mirror image with 10.0.0.6/30. The IP address goes on **interface port-channel 3**, never on the members.

|  | Layer 2 EtherChannel | Layer 3 EtherChannel |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Members | Switchports (access or trunk) | Routed ports (`no switchport`) |
| Addressing | None; carries VLANs | One IP address on the port-channel |
| STP | Runs on the port-channel | Not involved |
| Shown in summary as | `Po1(SU)` — S = Layer 2 | `Po3(RU)` — R = Layer 3 |
| Typical use | Access to distribution trunks; server/hypervisor bundles | Distribution to core; switch to router; routed campus |

### 6.9 Verifying EtherChannel

`show etherchannel summary` is the one command to memorise:

```
DSW1# show etherchannel summary
Flags:  D - down        P - bundled in port-channel
        I - stand-alone s - suspended
        H - Hot-standby (LACP only)
        R - Layer3      S - Layer2
        U - in use      f - failed to allocate aggregator
...
Number of channel-groups in use: 3
Number of aggregators:           3

Group  Port-channel  Protocol    Ports
------+-------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------
1      Po1(SU)         LACP      Gi1/0/1(P)   Gi1/0/3(P)
2      Po2(SU)         LACP      Gi1/0/2(P)   Gi1/0/4(P)
3      Po3(RU)         LACP      Gi1/0/21(P)  Gi1/0/22(P)
```

Read each line left to right: **Po1(SU)** = Layer 2 and in use; **Po3(RU)** = Layer 3 and in use; **(P)** after a member = bundled and forwarding. Healthy bundles show only P members.

| Flag on a member | Meaning | Usual cause |
| --- | --- | --- |
| P | Bundled | Healthy |
| I | Stand-alone: up but not bundled | Far end not running LACP (or both passive); forwards as a normal port |
| s | Suspended | Member settings differ from the others (VLANs, speed, mode) |
| D | Down | Cable, far end shut down |
| H | Hot-standby | More than 8 members; LACP holds the extras in reserve |

| Command | Use it to check |
| --- | --- |
| `show etherchannel summary` | Bundle and member state, protocol, Layer 2 vs Layer 3 |
| `show etherchannel port-channel` | Members, LACP state, how long each has been bundled |
| `show interfaces port-channel 1` | Combined bandwidth (BW 2000000 Kbit/sec for 2 × 1 Gb/s), counters |
| `show lacp neighbor` | The far end's system ID and port numbers — proves which switch you are bundled with |
| `show interfaces trunk` | Po1 (not its members) listed as the trunk |
| `show spanning-tree vlan 10` | Po1 appears as one STP port |

### 6.10 Troubleshooting EtherChannel

| Symptom | Likely cause | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Members show **I** (stand-alone); no Po up | LACP on one side only, both sides `passive`, or PAgP on one side and LACP on the other | Use `active` on at least one side (best: both) with the same protocol |
| One member shows **s** (suspended) | Its configuration differs (allowed VLANs, native VLAN, access VLAN, speed, duplex) | Make members identical; configure on the port-channel interface |
| Ports go **err-disabled**, log shows `%PM-4-ERR_DISABLE: channel-misconfig` | One side `on`, the other not bundling (EtherChannel misconfiguration guard) | Use LACP on both sides; then `shutdown` / `no shutdown` the members |
| Bundle forms but one member stays D | Physical fault on that link (Chapter 1) | Check cabling and counters |
| Layer 3 Po has no IP / shows Layer 2 (S) | `no switchport` not applied before `channel-group` | Remove members, set `no switchport` on members and Po, re-add |
| Bundle with the wrong switch | Members cabled to two different devices | `show lacp neighbor`; correct the cabling |
| One link saturated, the other idle | Hash method suits the traffic poorly | Change `port-channel load-balance` (for example to src-dst-ip) |

**Order for troubleshooting:** physical state of each member → same protocol and compatible modes → identical member configuration → correct neighbor → load-balance method.
