**New devices and cables:** 1 × Catalyst 3650-24PS (DSW2, add a power supply), and four extra copper crossover cables.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch06/fig-06-04-lab-6-1-topology.svg" alt="Figure 6.4 — Lab 6.1 topology: Po1 and Po2 Layer 2 trunks, Po3 Layer 3 bundle to DSW2" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 6.4</b> — Lab 6.1 topology: Po1 and Po2 Layer 2 trunks, Po3 Layer 3 bundle to DSW2</sub></p>

Figure 6.4 is Figure 5.6 with each access uplink doubled and a new distribution switch. Every highlighted pair is one port-channel; the member interfaces are listed beside each bundle and in the table below.

| Bundle | DSW1 members | Far-end members | Type | Addressing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Po1 | Gi1/0/1, Gi1/0/3 | SW1 Gi0/1, Gi0/2 | Layer 2 trunk, LACP active | native 999; allowed 10,30,40,99 |
| Po2 | Gi1/0/2, Gi1/0/4 | SW2 Gi0/1, Gi0/2 | Layer 2 trunk, LACP active | native 999; allowed 10,30,40,99 |
| Po3 | Gi1/0/21, Gi1/0/22 | DSW2 Gi1/0/21, Gi1/0/22 | Layer 3 routed, LACP active | DSW1 10.0.0.5/30, DSW2 10.0.0.6/30 |
| — | — | DSW2 Loopback0 | Test address | 10.255.0.2/32 |

**DSW1 additions**

```
configure terminal
port-channel load-balance src-dst-ip
interface range gigabitethernet1/0/1 , gigabitethernet1/0/3
 description Po1 members to SW1
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
 switchport nonegotiate
 channel-group 1 mode active
interface range gigabitethernet1/0/2 , gigabitethernet1/0/4
 description Po2 members to SW2
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
 switchport nonegotiate
 channel-group 2 mode active
interface port-channel 1
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
interface port-channel 2
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
interface range gigabitethernet1/0/21 - 22
 description Po3 members to DSW2
 no switchport
 channel-group 3 mode active
interface port-channel 3
 no switchport
 ip address 10.0.0.5 255.255.255.252
 no shutdown
exit
ip route 10.255.0.2 255.255.255.255 10.0.0.6
end
copy running-config startup-config
```

**SW1 additions** (SW2 is the same with `channel-group 2` and `interface port-channel 2`)

```
configure terminal
interface range gigabitethernet0/1 - 2
 description Po1 members to DSW1
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
 switchport nonegotiate
 channel-group 1 mode active
 no shutdown                     ! Gi0/2 was shut in Lab 5.1
interface port-channel 1
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
end
copy running-config startup-config
```

**DSW2**

```
enable
configure terminal
hostname DSW2
ip routing
interface range gigabitethernet1/0/21 - 22
 description Po3 members to DSW1
 no switchport
 channel-group 3 mode active
interface port-channel 3
 no switchport
 ip address 10.0.0.6 255.255.255.252
 no shutdown
interface loopback0
 ip address 10.255.0.2 255.255.255.255
exit
ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 10.0.0.5
end
copy running-config startup-config
```

**PT note:** Packet Tracer supports LACP and Layer 2 port-channels on 2960 and 3650 switches. Layer 3 port-channels on the 3650 work in recent versions; if yours rejects `no switchport` on the port-channel, build Po3 as a Layer 2 bundle in a new VLAN with SVIs instead, and note the difference.

**Verification and expected results**

| Check | Command (device) | Expected |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bundles | `show etherchannel summary` (DSW1) | Po1(SU), Po2(SU), Po3(RU), all members (P) |
| Bundle speed | `show interfaces port-channel 1` (DSW1) | BW 2000000 Kbit/sec |
| Neighbor | `show lacp neighbor` (SW1) | DSW1's ports Gi1/0/1 and Gi1/0/3 as partners |
| Trunk | `show interfaces trunk` (SW1) | Po1 trunking; Gi0/1 and Gi0/2 not listed separately |
| Routed bundle | `show ip route` (DSW1) | C 10.0.0.4/30 via Port-channel3; S 10.255.0.2/32 |
| End to end | PC1 `ping 10.255.0.2` | Success through Po1 and Po3 |

**Failover test:** start a long ping from PC1 to Printer1 (`ping -t 10.10.40.11` if your Packet Tracer accepts `-t`; otherwise repeat pings). Shut down DSW1 Gi1/0/2. Expect at most one lost reply, `show etherchannel summary` showing Gi1/0/2(D), and Po2 still (SU). Restore with `no shutdown`.

### 6.12 Lab 6.2 — Broken EtherChannel challenge

Apply to a copy of Lab 6.1, then fix using show commands only.

```
! SW2 and DSW1: Po2 members on both switches
interface range gigabitethernet0/1 - 2        ! (Gi1/0/2 , Gi1/0/4 on DSW1)
 channel-group 2 mode passive
! SW1
interface gigabitethernet0/2
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30
! DSW2
interface range gigabitethernet1/0/21 - 22
 channel-group 3 mode on
```

**Answer key (hide until done)**

| Fault | Evidence | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Both ends of Po2 passive | Members show (I) stand-alone; Po2 down | `channel-group 2 mode active` on at least one side |
| SW1 Gi0/2 allowed list differs | Gi0/2 shows (s) suspended; Po1 runs on one member | Reapply the allowed list on interface port-channel 1 |
| DSW2 members `on`, DSW1 members LACP active | Po3 never forms; possible err-disabled members | `channel-group 3 mode active` on DSW2, then bounce the members |

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] Po1, Po2 and Po3 bundled with all members (P)
- [ ] PC1 reaches DSW2's loopback
- [ ] Failover test shows at most one lost reply
- [ ] All three Lab 6.2 faults found and fixed
- [ ] File saved for Chapters 7–9

### 6.13 Chapter 6 summary notes (exam revision)

- **EtherChannel** bundles up to 8 active links into one logical **port-channel**; STP, MAC learning and routing see one interface. LACP can hold 8 more in hot-standby (16 total).
- **Load balancing is per flow** (hash of MAC/IP/port). One flow never exceeds one member's speed. `port-channel load-balance src-dst-ip` is a common choice.
- **Modes:** LACP `active`/`passive`; PAgP `desirable`/`auto`; static `on`. **passive + passive** and **auto + auto** never bundle; protocols cannot mix; `on` bundles only with `on`. Best practice: LACP active on both ends.
- **Members must match:** speed, duplex, switchport type, mode, access VLAN or native + allowed VLANs.
- **Layer 2 Po:** `channel-group n mode active` on members, then configure `interface port-channel n` (trunk settings are copied to members).
- **Layer 3 Po:** `no switchport` on members **before** `channel-group`; IP address on the port-channel.
- **Verify:** `show etherchannel summary` — Po(SU) Layer 2 in use, Po(RU) Layer 3 in use; members (P) bundled, (I) stand-alone, (s) suspended, (D) down.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "A 2 × 1 Gb/s bundle gives one download 2 Gb/s" | One flow uses one member: 1 Gb/s |
| "passive on both ends is the safe choice" | Neither side starts LACP; no bundle forms |
| "Put the IP address on each member of a Layer 3 bundle" | One IP, on the port-channel interface |
| "Channel-group numbers must match on both switches" | They are local; matching is only good documentation |
| "Fix a suspended member by changing that member" | Configure the port-channel so all members inherit identical settings |

### 6.14 Practice questions — Chapter 6

**1. (Modes)** Which LACP combination does **not** form a bundle?

A. active / active  B. active / passive  C. passive / passive  D. passive / active

**2. (Modes)** One switch uses `channel-group 1 mode active`, the other `channel-group 1 mode desirable`. What happens?

**3. (Output interpretation)**

```
Group  Port-channel  Protocol    Ports
------+-------------+-----------+-----------------------
2      Po2(SD)         LACP      Gi1/0/2(I)  Gi1/0/4(I)
```

What does this show, and what is the most likely cause?

**4. (Output interpretation)** `show etherchannel summary` shows `Po1(SU)  LACP  Gi1/0/1(P)  Gi1/0/3(s)`. What is wrong with Gi1/0/3?

**5. (Configuration)** Where is the IP address configured on a Layer 3 EtherChannel?

A. On every member  B. On the first member only  C. On the port-channel interface  D. On an SVI

**6. (Concept)** Two hosts copy one large file across a 2 × 1 Gb/s EtherChannel. What is the most throughput that copy can get?

**7. (Concept)** Why is `src-dst-ip` usually better than `src-mac` for a bundle between two Layer 3 devices?

**8. (Recall)** How many links can be **active** in one LACP EtherChannel?

A. 2  B. 4  C. 8  D. 16

**9. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which settings must match on all members of a trunk EtherChannel?

A. Interface description  B. Allowed VLAN list  C. Speed and duplex  D. Port number  E. CDP status

**10. (Configuration order)** A technician enters `channel-group 3 mode active` on Gi1/0/21–22 and then `no switchport` on the port-channel, but `show etherchannel summary` still shows Po3(SD) with S. What went wrong?

**11. (Output interpretation)** `show interfaces port-channel 1` reports `BW 2000000 Kbit/sec`. What does it tell you?

**12. (Troubleshooting)** Members go err-disabled with `%PM-4-ERR_DISABLE: channel-misconfig`. What is the likely configuration?

**13. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** Po2's members show (I). A network assistant recommends: *"Change both sides to `channel-group 2 mode on` to force the bundle up."* Is this the best fix?

### 6.15 Answer key with explanations

1. **C.** Passive only answers LACP; with no active side, nobody starts negotiation.
2. **No bundle.** LACP and PAgP cannot negotiate with each other. Use the same protocol on both ends (LACP).
3. Po2 is Layer 2 and **down**; both members are **stand-alone** (up, but not bundled). The far end is not negotiating LACP — for example both ends are passive, or the far end is not configured for EtherChannel at all.
4. Gi1/0/3 is **suspended**: its settings (allowed VLANs, native VLAN, speed or mode) differ from Gi1/0/1. Make it match, ideally by configuring interface port-channel 1.
5. **C.** Members are routed ports with no address; the bundle has one IP.
6. **1 Gb/s.** All frames of one flow hash to the same member.
7. Frames between two routers all carry the same pair of router MAC addresses, so a MAC-based hash sends everything down one member. IP addresses vary per conversation, so traffic spreads.
8. **C.** Eight active; LACP can hold up to eight more in hot-standby.
9. **B and C.** Descriptions, port numbers and CDP do not affect bundling.
10. The members were still **Layer 2** when they joined, so Po3 was created as a Layer 2 port-channel. Remove the members from the group, apply `no switchport` to the members, then re-add them (and confirm `no switchport` on Po3).
11. Two 1 Gb/s members are bundled: the port-channel's bandwidth is their sum (2 Gb/s). It does not mean one flow can reach 2 Gb/s.
12. One side is set to `on` (static) while the other side is not bundling (or uses LACP/PAgP). The EtherChannel misconfiguration guard err-disables the ports to prevent a loop. Use LACP active on both sides, then bounce the ports.
13. **No.** `on` hides problems: it skips the checks that LACP performs and can create a loop if the far end is mis-cabled or misconfigured later. The correct fix is `mode active` on at least one side (best: both), with identical member settings.

### 6.16 Chapter 6 figure checklist

All 4 figures were rendered, embedded beside the text they explain, and checked visually after rendering. Figure 6.4 had labels crossing the bundle links on first render; they were moved and re-checked.

| Figure | Title | Section | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 6.1 | STP-blocked parallel links vs EtherChannel | 6.2 | Rendered, embedded, checked |
| 6.2 | Per-flow load balancing across Po1 | 6.4 | Rendered, embedded, checked |
| 6.3 | Layer 2 trunk Po1 vs Layer 3 routed Po3 | 6.7 | Rendered, embedded, checked |
| 6.4 | Lab 6.1 topology | 6.11 | Rendered, embedded, checked after one fix |

**Shown as annotated command output instead of a drawing:** `show etherchannel summary` and its flags (6.9).

### 6.17 Chapter 6 progress record

**Objective completed:** **2.1.c** Layer 2/Layer 3 LACP port-channel/EtherChannel. With Chapter 5, **objective 2.1 is now complete** (2.1.a–d).

**Related objectives that reuse this chapter:** 2.2 edge ports with port channels and LACP for virtualized hosts and appliances (Chapter 7), 2.5 Rapid PVST+ seeing port-channels as single ports (Chapter 9).

**Next:** Chapter 7 — **2.2 Configure Layer 2 switch port attributes for edge-host connectivity (VLAN, PoE, port channel and LACP)**: desktops, printers, IoT, access points, IP phones, virtualized hosts and network appliances.

## Chapter 7 — Edge-host ports: VLAN, PoE, port channel and LACP

**Objective covered:** **2.2 Configure Layer 2 switch port attributes for edge-host connectivity (VLAN, PoE, port channel, and LACP)** for **2.2.a** desktops, printers and IoT appliances, **2.2.b** wireless access points (standalone and controller-based), **2.2.c** VoIP phones, **2.2.d** virtualized hosts and **2.2.e** network appliances.

This objective asks one practical question: *a device is plugged into this port — what should the port's configuration be?* The answer depends on how many VLANs the device needs, whether it needs power, and whether it uses more than one cable.

### 7.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Apply a secure baseline to every edge port.
2. Choose the right port profile for each of the five device types in the objective (Figure 7.1).
3. Configure and verify PoE, including power budgets and inline power states.
4. Configure a data VLAN plus a voice VLAN for an IP phone with a PC behind it.
5. Configure switch ports for standalone and controller-based access points.
6. Configure trunk and LACP edge ports for virtualized hosts and network appliances.
7. Troubleshoot edge-port problems from show-command output.

### 7.2 The edge-port baseline

An **edge port** connects to an end device rather than another switch. Every edge port in this guide starts from the same baseline; the rest of the chapter only adds to it.

```
SW4(config)# interface gigabitethernet1/0/1
SW4(config-if)# description PC-A Staff
SW4(config-if)# switchport mode access              ! no DTP, never becomes a trunk
SW4(config-if)# switchport access vlan 10
SW4(config-if)# spanning-tree portfast             ! forward immediately (Chapter 9)
SW4(config-if)# spanning-tree bpduguard enable     ! shut the port if a switch appears (Chapter 9)
SW4(config-if)# no shutdown
```

| Baseline item | Why | Covered in depth |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `description` | Documentation; checked against CDP/LLDP | Chapter 8 |
| `switchport mode access` | Stops DTP from forming an accidental trunk | Chapter 5 |
| `switchport access vlan` | Places the device in the right subnet | Chapter 5 |
| `spanning-tree portfast` | Hosts get a link in about a second instead of \~30 s, so DHCP does not time out | Chapter 9 |
| `spanning-tree bpduguard enable` | Protects against someone plugging in a switch | Chapter 9 |
| Port security, DHCP snooping, storm control | Limit MACs, stop rogue DHCP, contain floods | Chapter 23 |
| `shutdown` on unused ports | Nothing unknown can connect | Chapter 5 |

**Device profiles at a glance** (Figure 7.1 draws them):

| Device | Port mode | VLANs | PoE | Bundle | Special settings |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Desktop / laptop | Access | Data VLAN | No | No | Baseline only |
| Printer | Access | Printer VLAN | Sometimes | No | Static IP or DHCP reservation |
| IoT device / camera | Access | Separate IoT VLAN | Often | No | Isolate from users with ACLs |
| IP phone (+ PC) | Access with **voice VLAN** | Data untagged + voice tagged | Yes | No | `switchport voice vlan` |
| AP, standalone | Trunk | Management (native) + one VLAN per SSID | Yes (PoE+ often) | Sometimes | Native VLAN = AP management |
| AP, controller-based (local mode) | Access | AP management VLAN only | Yes | No | Client traffic tunnelled to the WLC |
| Virtualized host | Trunk | All VM VLANs | No | Often LACP | `spanning-tree portfast trunk` |
| Network appliance (firewall, WLC, load balancer) | Trunk or access | Inside VLANs | No | Often LACP | Match the appliance's HA design |

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch07/fig-07-01-edge-port-profiles.svg" alt="Figure 7.1 — Edge-device port profiles for objective 2.2.a–e" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 7.1</b> — Edge-device port profiles for objective 2.2.a–e</sub></p>

Figure 7.1 sorts the eight devices by what their port needs. Everything on the left is an access port; on the right the line style shows whether the device needs a trunk or a trunk inside an LACP bundle. The controller-based AP is the exception on the right: its port is a plain access port because client VLANs travel inside the tunnel to the WLC.

### 7.3 Desktops, printers and IoT appliances (2.2.a)

These devices need one VLAN and an untagged access port: the baseline from 7.2 with the right VLAN.

```
SW4(config)# interface gigabitethernet1/0/3
SW4(config-if)# description Printer1 - 2nd floor
SW4(config-if)# switchport mode access
SW4(config-if)# switchport access vlan 40
SW4(config-if)# spanning-tree portfast
SW4(config-if)# spanning-tree bpduguard enable
SW4(config-if)# power inline never                ! printer is mains-powered; no PoE
```

**Design points:**

- **Printers** usually keep a fixed address (static, or a DHCP reservation) because users and print servers point at it.
- **IoT appliances** (cameras, badge readers, sensors, smart TVs) go in their **own VLAN**. They are often poorly secured, so ACLs (Chapter 20) should limit what they can reach. Many are PoE-powered.
- **Speed and duplex** stay on auto for all three (Chapter 1).
- **Configure groups of identical ports once** with `interface range`, for example `interface range gigabitethernet1/0/5 - 20`.

### 7.4 Power over Ethernet (PoE)

**PoE** delivers DC power over the same twisted-pair cable as data, so phones, APs, cameras and IoT sensors need no power socket. The switch is the **PSE** (power sourcing equipment); the device is the **PD** (powered device).

**How a PoE port powers a device:**

1. **Detection** — the switch applies a low test voltage and looks for a PD's signature resistance. A PC NIC has none, so it never receives power.
2. **Classification** — the PD signals a power class (and with LLDP-MED or CDP can negotiate a more exact amount).
3. **Power-up** — if the switch's remaining **power budget** covers the request, it supplies power; otherwise it denies power and logs it.
4. **Monitoring** — if the PD is unplugged or draws too much, power is removed.

| Standard | Common name | Power at the switch port | Typical devices |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IEEE 802.3af (Type 1) | PoE | 15.4 W | IP phones, basic cameras |
| IEEE 802.3at (Type 2) | PoE+ | 30 W | Most Wi-Fi 5/6 APs, PTZ cameras |
| IEEE 802.3bt Type 3 | PoE++ / 4-pair | 60 W | High-end APs, video phones |
| IEEE 802.3bt Type 4 | PoE++ | 90 W | Displays, small switches |

The device receives slightly less than the switch sends (cable loss). Cisco's UPOE and UPOE+ are proprietary equivalents at 60 W and 90 W.

**Configuration** (interface mode; most PoE switches default to `auto`):

| Command | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| `power inline auto` | Detect and power any PD up to the port's maximum (default) |
| `power inline auto max 15400` | Same, but cap the port at 15.4 W (value in milliwatts) |
| `power inline static max 30000` | Reserve 30 W for this port in advance, even before a device connects |
| `power inline never` | Disable PoE on the port |

**Verification** (example output; columns and values vary by platform):

```
SW4# show power inline
Module   Available     Used     Remaining
          (Watts)     (Watts)    (Watts)
------   ---------   --------   ---------
1           390.0       21.7       368.3
Interface Admin  Oper       Power   Device              Class Max
                            (Watts)
--------- ------ ---------- ------- ------------------- ----- ----
Gi1/0/1   auto   off        0.0     n/a                 n/a   30.0
Gi1/0/2   auto   on         6.3     IP Phone 7960       2     30.0
Gi1/0/3   off    off        0.0     n/a                 n/a   30.0
Gi1/0/4   auto   on         15.4    AIR-AP              3     30.0
```

Read it as: the switch has 390 W of PoE budget, 21.7 W is in use; the phone on Gi1/0/2 and the AP on Gi1/0/4 are powered; Gi1/0/1 has a PC (no PD detected, so `off`); Gi1/0/3 was set to `never` (Admin `off`).

**PoE troubleshooting:** a device with no lights on a PoE port means check (1) the port is not `never`, (2) the switch's remaining budget, (3) the device's class against the port's maximum (an AP needing PoE+ on a PoE-only port may boot with reduced radios or not at all), (4) the cable. A state of `faulty` or `err-disable` usually means overcurrent or a bad cable or device.

### 7.5 IP phones and the voice VLAN (2.2.c)

Offices save cabling by plugging the PC into the back of the IP phone, so **one switch port serves two devices in two VLANs**: the phone in the voice VLAN and the PC in the data VLAN. Separating voice lets you give it priority (QoS) and keep it away from user traffic.

**How it works (Figure 7.2):**

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch07/fig-07-02-voice-vlan.svg" alt="Figure 7.2 — IP phone and PC sharing one switch port: voice VLAN 20 tagged, data VLAN 10 untagged" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 7.2</b> — IP phone and PC sharing one switch port: voice VLAN 20 tagged, data VLAN 10 untagged</sub></p>

In Figure 7.2 the dashed arrow is the CDP or LLDP-MED message that tells the phone its voice VLAN. The highlighted link between switch and phone carries both kinds of frame shown in the cards beneath it, and it also carries PoE power. Only untagged data crosses the phone-to-PC link.

1. The switch powers the phone with **PoE**.
2. The switch tells the phone its voice VLAN using **CDP** (Cisco phones) or **LLDP-MED** (other vendors): "use VLAN 20 for voice".
3. The phone contains a small **three-port switch**: one port to the wall, one for the PC, one internal for the phone itself.
4. The phone sends its own traffic **tagged with VLAN 20** and **CoS 5** (the 802.1Q PCP field from Figure 5.2).
5. The PC's frames pass through the phone **untagged**, and the switch places them in the access VLAN 10.
6. The phone gets its IP address by DHCP in the voice subnet (10.10.20.0/24 in this guide).

**Configuration:**

```
SW4(config)# vlan 20
SW4(config-vlan)# name VOICE
SW4(config-vlan)# exit
SW4(config)# interface gigabitethernet1/0/2
SW4(config-if)# description IP phone + PC-B
SW4(config-if)# switchport mode access
SW4(config-if)# switchport access vlan 10            ! data VLAN for the PC
SW4(config-if)# switchport voice vlan 20             ! voice VLAN for the phone
SW4(config-if)# spanning-tree portfast
SW4(config-if)# spanning-tree bpduguard enable
SW4(config-if)# power inline auto
```

**Verification:**

```
SW4# show interfaces gigabitethernet1/0/2 switchport
Name: Gi1/0/2
Switchport: Enabled
Administrative Mode: static access
Operational Mode: static access
Access Mode VLAN: 10 (STAFF)
Voice VLAN: 20 (VOICE)
...
SW4# show cdp neighbors
Device ID        Local Intrfce     Holdtme    Capability  Platform     Port ID
SEP0001C7A1B2C3  Gig 1/0/2         150             H P M  IP Phone    Port 1
```

**Exam points:**

- The port is still an **access port** (`static access`); it is *not* a trunk and does **not** appear in `show interfaces trunk`, even though voice frames are tagged.
- The voice VLAN must exist on the switch and be carried on the uplink trunks (`allowed vlan add 20`).
- Older Cisco documentation called this an "auxiliary VLAN".
- QoS trust settings for voice (for example `mls qos trust device cisco-phone` on some platforms) are beyond the v2.0 objectives.

**Troubleshooting phones:** phone dark → PoE (7.4); phone gets an address in the data subnet → voice VLAN missing on the port, or CDP/LLDP disabled so the phone never learned VLAN 20; phone works but PC does not → access VLAN wrong; both fail beyond the switch → VLAN 20 or 10 not allowed on the uplink trunk.

### 7.6 Wireless access points (2.2.b)

An AP bridges Wi-Fi clients onto the wired network. Each **SSID** (wireless network name) maps to a VLAN — for example *Staff-WiFi* to VLAN 30 and *Guest* to VLAN 35. Where that mapping happens decides how the switch port is configured (Figure 7.3).

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch07/fig-07-03-standalone-vs-lightweight-ap.svg" alt="Figure 7.3 — Standalone AP on a trunk (left) vs controller-based AP on an access port with a CAPWAP tunnel (right)" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 7.3</b> — Standalone AP on a trunk (left) vs controller-based AP on an access port with a CAPWAP tunnel (right)</sub></p>

On the left of Figure 7.3 the AP itself maps each SSID to a VLAN, so its port must carry all of them as a trunk. On the right the dashed CAPWAP tunnel carries client traffic from the AP across the switched network to the WLC, which maps SSIDs to VLANs on its own trunk; the AP's port needs only VLAN 99.

**Standalone (autonomous) AP.** The AP is configured individually and bridges each SSID straight onto its VLAN. Its switch port must therefore be a **trunk**: the AP's own management address is usually in the **native VLAN** (untagged), and each SSID's VLAN is tagged.

```
SW4(config)# interface gigabitethernet1/0/4
SW4(config-if)# description AP-Lobby (standalone)
SW4(config-if)# switchport mode trunk
SW4(config-if)# switchport trunk native vlan 99          ! AP management, untagged
SW4(config-if)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 30,35,99
SW4(config-if)# spanning-tree portfast trunk
SW4(config-if)# power inline auto
```

**Controller-based (lightweight) AP in local mode.** The AP is managed by a **wireless LAN controller (WLC)**. It builds a **CAPWAP** tunnel to the WLC — control on UDP 5246 (encrypted with DTLS) and data on UDP 5247 — and sends every client frame inside that tunnel. The WLC places client traffic on the right VLAN. The AP's switch port therefore needs only the **AP management VLAN**, as an **access** port:

```
SW4(config)# interface gigabitethernet1/0/5
SW4(config-if)# description LAP-1 (controller-based, local mode)
SW4(config-if)# switchport mode access
SW4(config-if)# switchport access vlan 99
SW4(config-if)# spanning-tree portfast
SW4(config-if)# power inline auto
```

The **WLC's** own uplink is a **trunk** carrying the client VLANs (30, 35) and its management VLAN, often bundled for redundancy. Older AireOS controllers bundle with static `mode on` (no LACP), while Catalyst 9800 controllers support LACP — check the platform before choosing the channel mode.

| AP type | Switch port | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Standalone (autonomous) | Trunk; native = AP management; SSID VLANs tagged | The AP itself puts each SSID on its VLAN |
| Controller-based, local mode | Access in the AP management VLAN | Client traffic is tunnelled to the WLC |
| Controller-based, FlexConnect (local switching) | Trunk, like standalone | The AP switches client traffic locally at a branch |
| Cloud-managed (for example Meraki) | Usually trunk | Managed from the cloud; traffic is bridged locally |

**How a lightweight AP joins a WLC (step by step):** the AP boots on PoE → gets an IP address by DHCP in VLAN 99 → discovers the WLC (DHCP option 43, DNS name `CISCO-CAPWAP-CONTROLLER`, or broadcast) → builds the CAPWAP tunnel → downloads its configuration → starts its SSIDs.

**Troubleshooting APs:** AP has no power or reduced radios → PoE class or budget (7.4); standalone AP clients get no address in one SSID → that SSID's VLAN missing from the trunk; lightweight AP never joins → no DHCP or route to the WLC, or UDP 5246/5247 blocked; lightweight AP on a trunk with the wrong native VLAN → it gets an address in the wrong subnet.

### 7.7 Virtualized hosts (2.2.d)

A hypervisor host carries VMs in several VLANs over its uplinks (Chapter 4, Figure 4.2), so its ports are **trunks**, usually **two or more** for redundancy. Whether they form an LACP bundle depends on the host's NIC-teaming mode: bundle only when the host uses LACP.

**Two-uplink host using LACP:**

```
SW4(config)# interface range gigabitethernet1/0/11 - 12
SW4(config-if-range)# description ESXi-01 vmnic0-1
SW4(config-if-range)# switchport mode trunk
SW4(config-if-range)# switchport trunk native vlan 999
SW4(config-if-range)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,99
SW4(config-if-range)# switchport nonegotiate
SW4(config-if-range)# channel-group 11 mode active
SW4(config-if-range)# exit
SW4(config)# interface port-channel 11
SW4(config-if)# switchport mode trunk
SW4(config-if)# switchport trunk native vlan 999
SW4(config-if)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,99
SW4(config-if)# spanning-tree portfast trunk           ! host does not bridge between its NICs
```

**Host using independent teaming (no LACP):** configure both ports as identical trunks *without* `channel-group`, each with `spanning-tree portfast trunk`.

Newer IOS releases write the PortFast command as `spanning-tree portfast edge trunk`. Use PortFast on host trunks only when the host does not run STP or bridge between its NICs, which is normal for hypervisors.

### 7.8 Network appliances (2.2.e)

**Network appliances** are dedicated devices that provide a service: firewalls, wireless LAN controllers, load balancers, intrusion prevention systems, WAN optimizers. They commonly connect in one of three ways:

| Connection | Switch port | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| One interface per network | Access ports in different VLANs | Small firewall: inside port in VLAN 10, DMZ port in VLAN 50 |
| Subinterfaces on one link | Trunk | Firewall "router-on-a-stick" for several inside VLANs |
| Bundled for capacity and redundancy | Trunk inside an LACP port-channel | Data-centre firewall or WLC with two or four uplinks |

```
SW4(config)# interface range gigabitethernet1/0/21 - 22
SW4(config-if-range)# description FW1 inside, Po21 members
SW4(config-if-range)# switchport mode trunk
SW4(config-if-range)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
SW4(config-if-range)# channel-group 21 mode active
```

**Design notes:** appliances are often deployed in **high-availability pairs**; each unit connects to the switches the same way, and the pair shares a virtual IP. Appliances in transparent (bridging) mode can create Layer 2 loops, so do not enable PortFast or BPDU filtering on them without following the vendor's design.

### 7.9 Verifying edge ports

| Command | Use it to check |
| --- | --- |
| `show interfaces status` | Each port's VLAN (or `trunk`), speed, duplex, link state |
| `show interfaces <int> switchport` | Access VLAN, **voice VLAN**, mode, native and allowed VLANs |
| `show power inline` | PoE budget and per-port power state, class and device |
| `show cdp neighbors detail` / `show lldp neighbors detail` | Which device is really connected (phone, AP, host), its IP and platform |
| `show interfaces trunk` | Trunks to APs, hosts and appliances (phone ports are **not** listed) |
| `show etherchannel summary` | Host and appliance bundles with members (P) |
| `show mac address-table interface <int>` | Phone and PC MACs in VLAN 20 and 10 on one port; many VM MACs on a host trunk |

### 7.10 Troubleshooting edge ports

| Symptom | Likely cause | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| IP phone dark | PoE disabled (`never`), budget exhausted, or class too high for the port | `power inline auto`; check `show power inline` |
| Phone gets a data-VLAN address | No `switchport voice vlan`, or CDP/LLDP disabled | Add the voice VLAN; enable CDP or LLDP |
| PC behind phone has no network | Wrong access VLAN, or data VLAN not on the uplink trunk | Correct `switchport access vlan`; `allowed vlan add` |
| Standalone AP: one SSID's clients get no address | That VLAN missing from the AP trunk | Add it to the allowed list |
| Lightweight AP never joins the WLC | AP VLAN has no DHCP or no route to the WLC; CAPWAP blocked | Fix DHCP/routing; permit UDP 5246–5247 |
| AP boots with reduced radios | PoE class higher than the port supplies | Use a PoE+ (or higher) port; raise the port maximum |
| Host VMs flap or lose connectivity | Teaming mode and switch bundling disagree | Match LACP on both sides, or remove `channel-group` |
| Port err-disabled after connecting a device | BPDU guard saw a switch (or an appliance in bridge mode) | Remove the switch or redesign; bounce the port (Chapter 9) |
| Printer unreachable from users | Printer in its own VLAN but no inter-VLAN routing or an ACL blocks it | Check gateway SVI and ACLs |

### 7.11 Packet Tracer Lab 7.1 — A PoE access switch for every edge device (Intermediate)

**Objectives:** 2.2.a–e. **You will:** configure edge ports for a desktop, an IP phone with a PC behind it, a printer, an access point with a wireless laptop, and (configuration only) a virtualized host bundle; then verify VLANs, the voice VLAN, PoE and CDP. This is a new file; it does not change Lab 6.1.

**Devices:** 1 × Catalyst 3650-24PS (SW4, add an AC power supply), 2 × PC (PC-A, PC-B), 1 × IP Phone 7960, 1 × Printer, 1 × AccessPoint-PT, 1 × Laptop with a wireless NIC.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch07/fig-07-04-lab-7-1-topology.svg" alt="Figure 7.4 — Lab 7.1 topology: one PoE switch with five edge-device profiles" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 7.4</b> — Lab 7.1 topology: one PoE switch with five edge-device profiles</sub></p>

Figure 7.4 places every device in its own column, with its switch port and VLAN on the second line. PC-B and the laptop do not connect to SW4 directly: PC-B reaches it through the phone, and the laptop through the AP.

| Port | Device | Profile | Addressing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Gi1/0/1 | PC-A | Access VLAN 10 | DHCP from SW4 |
| Gi1/0/2 | IP phone, PC-B on the phone's PC port | Access VLAN 10 + voice VLAN 20, PoE | Phone: DHCP in 10.10.20.0/24; PC-B: DHCP in 10.10.10.0/24 |
| Gi1/0/3 | Printer1 | Access VLAN 40, PoE off | Static 10.10.40.21/24, gw 10.10.40.1 |
| Gi1/0/4 | AccessPoint-PT (SSID Staff-WiFi) | Access VLAN 30 (see PT note) | Laptop: DHCP in 10.10.30.0/24 |
| Gi1/0/11–12 | ESXi-01 (no device; configuration practice) | Trunk in LACP Po11 | — |

**PT notes:** Packet Tracer's AccessPoint-PT bridges a single SSID and does not tag VLANs, so it is connected as an access port in VLAN 30; the trunk configuration for a multi-SSID standalone AP in 7.6 is practised on paper. The IP phone will receive an address but will not register for calls (that needs a call-control server, which is beyond the CCNA). If the phone does not power on from the switch, add its power adapter in the Physical tab and note that your version does not simulate PoE. `show power inline` is available only in some Packet Tracer versions.

**SW4 configuration**

```
enable
configure terminal
hostname SW4
ip routing
vlan 10
 name STAFF
vlan 20
 name VOICE
vlan 30
 name WIRELESS
vlan 40
 name PRINTERS
vlan 99
 name MGMT
vlan 999
 name NATIVE
exit
interface vlan 10
 ip address 10.10.10.1 255.255.255.0
 no shutdown
interface vlan 20
 ip address 10.10.20.1 255.255.255.0
 no shutdown
interface vlan 30
 ip address 10.10.30.1 255.255.255.0
 no shutdown
interface vlan 40
 ip address 10.10.40.1 255.255.255.0
 no shutdown
exit
! DHCP server (explained in Chapter 14)
ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.10.1 10.10.10.10
ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.20.1 10.10.20.10
ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.30.1 10.10.30.10
ip dhcp pool STAFF
 network 10.10.10.0 255.255.255.0
 default-router 10.10.10.1
ip dhcp pool VOICE
 network 10.10.20.0 255.255.255.0
 default-router 10.10.20.1
ip dhcp pool WIRELESS
 network 10.10.30.0 255.255.255.0
 default-router 10.10.30.1
exit
! Edge ports
interface gigabitethernet1/0/1
 description PC-A Staff
 switchport mode access
 switchport access vlan 10
 spanning-tree portfast
 spanning-tree bpduguard enable
interface gigabitethernet1/0/2
 description IP phone + PC-B
 switchport mode access
 switchport access vlan 10
 switchport voice vlan 20
 spanning-tree portfast
 spanning-tree bpduguard enable
interface gigabitethernet1/0/3
 description Printer1
 switchport mode access
 switchport access vlan 40
 spanning-tree portfast
 spanning-tree bpduguard enable
 power inline never
interface gigabitethernet1/0/4
 description AP Staff-WiFi
 switchport mode access
 switchport access vlan 30
 spanning-tree portfast
interface range gigabitethernet1/0/11 - 12
 description ESXi-01 vmnic0-1
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,99
 channel-group 11 mode active
interface port-channel 11
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,99
 spanning-tree portfast trunk
end
copy running-config startup-config
```

If `switchport mode trunk` is rejected, add `switchport trunk encapsulation dot1q` first. If your version does not offer the DHCP server on the 3650, give the PCs static addresses and skip the phone's address test.

**Device settings:** connect PC-B to the phone's PC ("Switch") port; set PC-A, PC-B and the laptop to DHCP; set the AP's SSID to **Staff-WiFi** and configure the same SSID on the laptop's wireless NIC.

**Verification and expected results**

| Check | Command / action | Expected |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Port profiles | `show interfaces status` | Gi1/0/1 and 1/0/2 in VLAN 10, Gi1/0/3 in 40, Gi1/0/4 in 30; Gi1/0/11–12 notconnect |
| Voice VLAN | `show interfaces gi1/0/2 switchport` | Access Mode VLAN 10, Voice VLAN 20 |
| Phone detected | `show cdp neighbors` | The phone listed on Gi1/0/2 |
| Addresses | `show ip dhcp binding` | Leases in 10.10.10.0, 10.10.20.0 and 10.10.30.0 |
| Two VLANs on one port | `show mac address-table interface gi1/0/2` | Phone's MAC in VLAN 20 and PC-B's MAC in VLAN 10 |
| Phone ports are not trunks | `show interfaces trunk` | Only Po11 (if anything); Gi1/0/2 absent |
| PoE (if supported) | `show power inline` | Gi1/0/2 on; Gi1/0/3 admin off |
| Connectivity | Laptop `ping 10.10.40.21` | Success, routed by SW4 between VLAN 30 and 40 |

**Break-fix exercises**

1. Remove `switchport voice vlan 20` from Gi1/0/2 and renew the phone's address. *Expected:* the phone gets an address in 10.10.10.0/24. Restore the voice VLAN.
2. Change Gi1/0/4 to `switchport access vlan 40`. *Expected:* the laptop receives no DHCP address (no pool for VLAN 40). Restore VLAN 30.
3. Connect a second switch to Gi1/0/1. *Expected:* BPDU guard err-disables the port (Chapter 9). Remove it and bounce the port.

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] Every edge port matches its profile in the table
- [ ] Phone and PC-B share Gi1/0/2 in VLANs 20 and 10
- [ ] Laptop associated to Staff-WiFi and reached the printer
- [ ] Host bundle Po11 configured (and shown down, with no device)
- [ ] All three break-fix exercises explained

### 7.12 Chapter 7 summary notes (exam revision)

- **Every edge port:** description, `switchport mode access` (or trunk where needed), correct VLAN, `spanning-tree portfast`, `spanning-tree bpduguard enable`, unused ports shut.
- **Desktops, printers, IoT (2.2.a):** access ports; IoT in its own VLAN; printers static or reserved.
- **PoE:** PSE (switch) powers PD; detection → classification → power if budget allows. 802.3af 15.4 W, 802.3at (PoE+) 30 W, 802.3bt 60/90 W. `power inline auto | never | static`; verify with `show power inline`.
- **IP phone (2.2.c):** `switchport access vlan 10` + `switchport voice vlan 20`; phone learns VLAN via CDP/LLDP-MED; voice tagged (CoS 5), PC untagged; port stays **access** and is not in `show interfaces trunk`.
- **APs (2.2.b):** standalone → **trunk** (native = AP management, SSID VLANs tagged); controller-based local mode → **access** in the AP VLAN, CAPWAP (UDP 5246/5247) to the WLC; FlexConnect local switching → trunk. WLC uplink = trunk.
- **Virtualized hosts (2.2.d):** trunk with all VM VLANs; LACP bundle only if the host's teaming uses LACP; `spanning-tree portfast trunk`.
- **Appliances (2.2.e):** access per interface, a trunk, or a trunk inside an LACP bundle; often in HA pairs.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "A port with a voice VLAN is a trunk" | It is an access port with a voice VLAN |
| "Every AP needs a trunk" | Controller-based local-mode APs use an access port |
| "PoE will power any device" | Only detected PDs, and only within the switch's budget and the port's maximum |
| "Always bundle a server's two NICs" | Bundle only when the host's teaming uses LACP |

### 7.13 Practice questions — Chapter 7

**1. (Configuration)** Gi1/0/7 connects to an IP phone with a PC behind it. Data is VLAN 10 and voice is VLAN 20. Which two interface commands are essential besides `switchport mode access`?

**2. (Concept)** Does Gi1/0/7 from question 1 appear in `show interfaces trunk`? Why or why not?

**3. (Concept)** How do the phone's frames and the PC's frames differ on the link between the switch and the phone?

**4. (Recall)** Which standard is PoE+ and how much power can the switch port supply?

A. 802.3af, 15.4 W  B. 802.3at, 30 W  C. 802.3bt, 60 W  D. 802.3ad, 30 W

**5. (Output interpretation)** `show power inline` shows `Gi1/0/1  auto  off  0.0  n/a`. A PC is connected and working. Is there a fault?

**6. (Design)** A standalone AP broadcasts Staff-WiFi (VLAN 30) and Guest (VLAN 35); its management address is in VLAN 99. How should its switch port be configured?

**7. (Design)** A lightweight AP in local mode is managed by a WLC. Which switch-port configuration is typical, and why?

**8. (Recall)** Which UDP ports does CAPWAP use?

A. 67 and 68  B. 161 and 162  C. 5246 and 5247  D. 4789 and 4790

**9. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which protocols can tell an IP phone its voice VLAN?

A. CDP  B. DTP  C. LLDP-MED  D. VTP  E. STP

**10. (Troubleshooting)** A new IP phone receives an address in 10.10.10.0/24 (the data subnet) instead of 10.10.20.0/24. What is the most likely cause?

**11. (Troubleshooting)** A new Wi-Fi 6 AP boots but only one radio comes up. The port is on an older switch. What should you check first?

**12. (Configuration)** A hypervisor host uses LACP teaming on two NICs connected to Gi1/0/11–12 and carries VLANs 10, 30 and 99. Outline the switch configuration.

**13. (Design)** Where should a building's IP security cameras be placed, and what else should protect them?

**14. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** On a standalone AP's trunk port, Guest clients (VLAN 35) get no address while Staff clients work. A network assistant recommends: *"Change the AP port to `switchport access vlan 35`."* Is this a good fix?

### 7.14 Answer key with explanations

1. `switchport access vlan 10` and `switchport voice vlan 20`. (PortFast, BPDU guard and PoE `auto` are also normal.)
2. **No.** The port is an access port with a voice VLAN (operational mode `static access`), so it is not listed as a trunk even though the phone's frames are tagged.
3. The phone tags its voice frames with **VLAN 20 and CoS 5**; the PC's frames are **untagged** and placed in access VLAN 10.
4. **B.** 802.3af is the original 15.4 W PoE; 802.3bt is the higher-power standard; 802.3ad is link aggregation (LACP), not PoE.
5. **No.** `off` with no device class means no powered device was detected — a PC does not request PoE. Admin `auto` means the port would power a PD if one were connected.
6. As a **trunk**: native VLAN 99 (AP management, untagged) and allowed VLANs 30, 35 and 99, with `spanning-tree portfast trunk` and PoE.
7. An **access port in the AP management VLAN**. Client traffic is carried inside the CAPWAP tunnel to the WLC, which maps SSIDs to VLANs, so the AP's port does not need the client VLANs.
8. **C.** 5246 is CAPWAP control, 5247 is data. 67/68 are DHCP, 161/162 SNMP, 4789 is VXLAN.
9. **A and C.** CDP (Cisco phones) and LLDP-MED (multi-vendor) advertise the voice VLAN. DTP negotiates trunks; VTP shares VLAN lists; STP prevents loops.
10. The port has **no `switchport voice vlan 20`**, or CDP/LLDP is disabled, so the phone never learns its voice VLAN and requests an address untagged in the data VLAN.
11. **PoE**: the AP probably needs PoE+ (30 W) or more, but the port supplies only 802.3af (15.4 W) or the switch's budget is short. Check `show power inline`.
12. `interface range gi1/0/11 - 12` → `switchport mode trunk`, `switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,99` (plus native VLAN) → `channel-group 11 mode active`; then on `interface port-channel 11` repeat the trunk settings and add `spanning-tree portfast trunk`.
13. In a **separate IoT/camera VLAN**, usually PoE-powered, with **ACLs** (Chapter 20) allowing only the video recorder and management systems to reach them.
14. **No.** An access port in VLAN 35 would carry only Guest traffic and break Staff-WiFi and the AP's management. The likely fault is VLAN 35 missing from the trunk's allowed list (or not created); fix it with `switchport trunk allowed vlan add 35`.

### 7.16 Chapter 7 progress record

**Objectives completed:** **2.2** and **2.2.a–e** — edge-port baseline, desktops, printers, IoT, PoE, IP phones and voice VLANs, standalone and controller-based APs, virtualized hosts and network appliances.

**Related objectives that reuse this chapter:** 2.3 validating documentation with CDP/LLDP (Chapter 8), 2.5.c–d PortFast and BPDU guard (Chapter 9), 1.5 wireless principles (Chapter 16), 4.7 Layer 2 security on edge ports (Chapter 23).

**Next:** Chapter 8 — **2.3 Validate the accuracy of network documentation using CDP and LLDP**.

## Chapter 8 — Validating network documentation with CDP and LLDP

**Objective covered:** **2.3 Validate the accuracy of network documentation using CDP and LLDP.** The verb is *validate*: the exam gives you a topology drawing or port table plus neighbor output and asks what is wrong.

### 8.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Explain what CDP and LLDP are, what they advertise, and which devices they can and cannot see.
2. Read `show cdp neighbors`, `show lldp neighbors` and their `detail` versions without mixing up local and remote ports.
3. Enable, disable and tune CDP and LLDP globally and per interface.
4. Compare CDP with LLDP and explain LLDP-MED.
5. Follow a repeatable process to compare documentation against discovered neighbors and correct the right thing.
6. Decide where to disable discovery protocols for security.

### 8.2 Introduction: documentation drifts

A network drawing is correct on the day it is made. Then a technician moves a cable to a spare port, a switch is replaced by a different model, a management address changes — and nobody updates the drawing. Months later someone troubleshoots from the wrong picture. **CDP and LLDP** let devices describe their own direct connections, so you can check the documentation against what is really cabled.

Both are **Layer 2** protocols: a device sends periodic advertisements out each interface, and the directly connected neighbor stores them. Advertisements are **never forwarded**, so a device learns only about its **directly connected** neighbors (Figure 8.1). They also work only between devices that run the protocol: switches, routers, IP phones and APs do; most PCs and printers do not.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch08/fig-08-01-discovery-scope.svg" alt="Figure 8.1 — Discovery scope: each device learns only its directly connected CDP/LLDP neighbors" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 8.1</b> — Discovery scope: each device learns only its directly connected CDP/LLDP neighbors</sub></p>

In Figure 8.1 the arrows are advertisements on each link, in both directions. Because DSW1 does not pass SW1's advertisement on to R1, building a full network map means logging in to each device in turn and following the neighbors hop by hop. PC1 is invisible to both protocols, so end hosts are documented from the MAC address table instead.

|  | CDP | LLDP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Standard | Cisco proprietary | IEEE 802.1AB, any vendor |
| Default on Cisco IOS | Enabled | Often disabled (enable with `lldp run`) |
| Advertisement timer | 60 s | 30 s |
| Holdtime | 180 s | 120 s |
| Destination MAC | 01:00:0C:CC:CC:CC | 01:80:C2:00:00:0E |
| Extras | Native VLAN, duplex, VTP domain, voice VLAN, power | Extensible TLVs; **LLDP-MED** for phones (voice VLAN, PoE) |

### 8.3 CDP (Cisco Discovery Protocol)

CDP runs on Cisco routers, switches, IP phones and APs, and is **enabled by default** on every interface. CDP version 2 is the default.

**What CDP advertises:** device ID (hostname), management IP address(es), local port the advertisement left from, platform (model), capabilities (router, switch, phone…), software version, and on CDPv2 the **native VLAN**, **duplex**, VTP domain, and for phones the voice VLAN and power request.

**`show cdp neighbors`** — one line per neighbor (Lab 6.1, on DSW1):

```
DSW1# show cdp neighbors
Capability Codes: R - Router, T - Trans Bridge, B - Source Route Bridge
                  S - Switch, H - Host, I - IGMP, r - Repeater, P - Phone,
                  D - Remote, C - CVTA, M - Two-port Mac Relay

Device ID        Local Intrfce     Holdtme    Capability  Platform   Port ID
SW1              Gig 1/0/1         155             S I    WS-C2960-  Gig 0/1
SW1              Gig 1/0/3         155             S I    WS-C2960-  Gig 0/2
SW2              Gig 1/0/2         161             S I    WS-C2960-  Gig 0/1
SW2              Gig 1/0/4         161             S I    WS-C2960-  Gig 0/2
DSW2             Gig 1/0/21        143           R S I    WS-C3650-  Gig 1/0/21
DSW2             Gig 1/0/22        143           R S I    WS-C3650-  Gig 1/0/22
R1               Gig 1/0/24        170           R B S I  ISR4331    Gig 0/0/0
```

| Column | Belongs to | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Device ID | Neighbor | Neighbor's hostname |
| **Local Intrfce** | **This device** | My port the neighbor is connected to |
| Holdtme | — | Seconds left before the entry expires if no new advertisement arrives |
| Capability | Neighbor | What the neighbor is (R, S, P…) |
| Platform | Neighbor | Neighbor's model (often truncated) |
| **Port ID** | **Neighbor** | The neighbor's port at the other end of the cable |

**The classic exam trap:** "Local Intrfce" is yours; "Port ID" is the neighbor's. Read the line above as: *my Gi1/0/24 connects to R1's G0/0/0.* Note also that each **member** of an EtherChannel appears separately — CDP runs on physical links, not on the port-channel.

**`show cdp neighbors detail`** (or `show cdp entry SW1`) adds the IP address, full platform, software version, native VLAN and duplex:

```
DSW1# show cdp entry SW1
-------------------------
Device ID: SW1
Entry address(es):
  IP address: 10.10.99.11
Platform: cisco WS-C2960-24TT-L,  Capabilities: Switch IGMP
Interface: GigabitEthernet1/0/1,  Port ID (outgoing port): GigabitEthernet0/1
Holdtime : 151 sec

Version :
Cisco IOS Software, C2960 Software ...        <- exact version text varies

advertisement version: 2
Native VLAN: 999
Duplex: full
```

**Configuration and tuning:**

| Command | Mode | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `cdp run` / `no cdp run` | Global config | Enable / disable CDP on the whole device |
| `cdp enable` / `no cdp enable` | Interface config | Enable / disable CDP on one interface |
| `cdp timer 60` | Global config | Advertisement interval (default 60 s) |
| `cdp holdtime 180` | Global config | How long neighbors keep the entry (default 180 s) |
| `show cdp` | Privileged EXEC | Global status, timer, holdtime, version |
| `show cdp interface` | Privileged EXEC | Which interfaces run CDP |
| `show cdp traffic` | Privileged EXEC | Packets sent and received, errors |

CDP also produces two useful log messages you met earlier: `%CDP-4-DUPLEX_MISMATCH` (Chapter 1) and `%CDP-4-NATIVE_VLAN_MISMATCH` (Chapter 5). Both are documentation clues too.

### 8.4 LLDP (Link Layer Discovery Protocol)

LLDP (IEEE 802.1AB) does the same job as CDP but is **vendor-neutral**, so it discovers non-Cisco switches, servers, firewalls and phones. Information is carried in **TLVs** (type-length-value fields). Mandatory TLVs are chassis ID, port ID and time-to-live; optional ones include system name, system description, system capabilities, port description and management address.

On many Cisco IOS devices LLDP is **off by default**. Turn it on globally; each interface then both transmits and receives unless you say otherwise:

```
DSW1(config)# lldp run                                ! enable globally
DSW1(config)# interface gigabitethernet1/0/24
DSW1(config-if)# no lldp transmit                     ! stop sending on this port
DSW1(config-if)# no lldp receive                      ! stop listening on this port
```

| Command | Mode | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `lldp run` / `no lldp run` | Global config | Enable / disable LLDP |
| `lldp transmit` / `lldp receive` | Interface config | Per-direction control (both on once LLDP runs) |
| `lldp timer 30` | Global config | Advertisement interval (default 30 s) |
| `lldp holdtime 120` | Global config | How long neighbors keep the entry (default 120 s) |
| `lldp reinit 2` | Global config | Delay before LLDP initializes on a port (default 2 s) |
| `show lldp` | Privileged EXEC | Status and timers |
| `show lldp neighbors [detail]` | Privileged EXEC | Neighbor table / full TLVs |
| `show lldp interface` | Privileged EXEC | Per-interface transmit/receive state |

```
DSW1# show lldp neighbors
Capability codes:
    (R) Router, (B) Bridge, (T) Telephone, (C) DOCSIS Cable Device
    (W) WLAN Access Point, (P) Repeater, (S) Station, (O) Other

Device ID           Local Intf     Hold-time  Capability      Port ID
SW1                 Gi1/0/1        120        B               Gi0/1
SW2                 Gi1/0/2        120        B               Gi0/1
R1                  Gi1/0/24       120        R               Gi0/0/0
...
Total entries displayed: 7
```

The same rule applies: **Local Intf** is yours, **Port ID** is the neighbor's. LLDP capability codes differ from CDP's — a switch is **B** (bridge), a phone **T**, an AP **W**.

**LLDP-MED** (Media Endpoint Discovery) extends LLDP for endpoints such as IP phones: the switch advertises the **voice VLAN** and QoS settings in a Network Policy TLV, and the phone reports its **PoE** needs and inventory. It is how non-Cisco phones learn their voice VLAN (Chapter 7).

### 8.5 CDP or LLDP? And when to turn them off

| Situation | Use |
| --- | --- |
| All-Cisco network | CDP (already on); LLDP optional |
| Mixed vendors, servers, Linux hosts | LLDP |
| Non-Cisco IP phones | LLDP-MED |
| Cisco IP phones | CDP (or LLDP-MED) |

**Security.** Advertisements reveal hostnames, models, software versions and management IPs — useful to an attacker planning an exploit. Best practice:

- Disable on ports facing untrusted networks: the Internet edge, ISP links, guest-facing ports (`no cdp enable`, `no lldp transmit`).
- Keep them on infrastructure links and on IP phone ports, where the voice VLAN depends on them.
- Some organisations disable CDP globally and use LLDP only where needed.

### 8.6 A repeatable validation process

Figure 8.2 shows the loop. In words:

1. **Collect the documentation:** topology drawing, port-to-port connection table, IP addressing plan, device inventory (models, software).
2. **Gather the evidence** on every switch and router: `show cdp neighbors detail` and/or `show lldp neighbors detail`, plus `show interfaces description` and `show interfaces status`.
3. **Compare field by field** for every link: neighbor hostname, **local port**, **remote port**, platform/model, management IP, and (CDP) native VLAN and duplex.
4. **For each mismatch, decide what is wrong.** If the network works and follows the design standard, the **documentation** is wrong: update it. If the documented design is the approved standard (for example a cabling plan), the **network** is wrong: fix it through change control, then re-check.
5. **Hosts that do not run CDP/LLDP** (PCs, printers): verify with `show mac address-table interface <port>` and the DHCP or ARP tables.
6. **Publish** the corrected documentation with the date and who verified it, and update interface `description`s so the next audit is faster.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch08/fig-08-02-documentation-validation-workflow.svg" alt="Figure 8.2 — Documentation validation workflow with CDP and LLDP" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 8.2</b> — Documentation validation workflow with CDP and LLDP</sub></p>

Figure 8.2 runs once per link. Matching links go straight to the published record; mismatches go either to a documentation fix or, when the network breaks the approved design, to a change that is re-checked from step 2.

### 8.7 Worked example: auditing DSW1

The documentation for the Lab 6.1 campus was written months ago. Comparing it with `show cdp neighbors detail` on DSW1 gives the picture in Figure 8.3.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch08/fig-08-03-dsw1-audit.svg" alt="Figure 8.3 — DSW1's documented neighbors (left) vs what CDP discovered (right), mismatches outlined in red" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 8.3</b> — DSW1's documented neighbors (left) vs what CDP discovered (right), mismatches outlined in red</sub></p>

Figure 8.3 lays the same four links side by side. Each box reads "neighbor name / my port to its port / model or address". Only DSW2 matches; the table below records what differs and which side to correct.

| DSW1 port | Documented neighbor and port | Discovered by CDP | Result | Action |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Gi1/0/1 | SW1 Gi0/1, mgmt 10.10.99.21 | SW1 Gi0/1, IP 10.10.99.11 | IP differs | SVI is .11 by design: correct the documentation |
| Gi1/0/2 | SW2 Gi0/2, WS-C3650 | SW2 Gi0/1, WS-C2960 | Remote port and model differ | Model: documentation wrong. Port: check the cabling plan; Lab 6.1 bundles Gi0/1–2, so record both members |
| Gi1/0/21 | DSW2 Gi1/0/21, WS-C3650 | DSW2 Gi1/0/21, WS-C3650 | Match | Mark verified |
| Gi1/0/24 | R2 G0/0/0 | R1 G0/0/0 | Hostname differs | Router was renamed R1: correct the documentation |

Three lessons from this audit:

- Read the **Port ID** column for the far end; reading the local column instead is the most common error.
- CDP lists **each EtherChannel member**, so a bundle appears as two lines; the documentation should record every member and the port-channel number.
- A mismatch does not automatically mean the network is wrong. Decide which one matches the approved design.

### 8.8 Troubleshooting CDP and LLDP

| Symptom | Likely cause | Check / fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Neighbor missing from `show cdp neighbors` | Link down; CDP disabled globally or on either interface; neighbor is not Cisco | `show interfaces status`; `show cdp interface`; use LLDP for non-Cisco devices |
| Nothing in `show lldp neighbors` | LLDP not enabled (`lldp run`) on one or both sides | `show lldp`; enable it |
| Neighbor appears only one way | One side has `no lldp transmit` or `no cdp enable` | `show lldp interface` / `show cdp interface` on both ends |
| Old neighbor still listed after re-cabling | Entry has not aged out yet (holdtime 180 s CDP, 120 s LLDP) | Wait, or `clear cdp table` / `clear lldp table` |
| Router two hops away is not listed | Expected: advertisements are not forwarded | Log in to the next device and continue hop by hop |
| PCs and printers never appear | They do not run CDP/LLDP | Use `show mac address-table`, ARP and DHCP tables |
| `%CDP-4-NATIVE_VLAN_MISMATCH` or `%CDP-4-DUPLEX_MISMATCH` | Configuration mismatch on the link | Fix the trunk or duplex settings (Chapters 1 and 5) |

### 8.9 Packet Tracer Lab 8.1 — Documentation audit (Intermediate)

**Objective:** 2.3. **Start from:** your saved Lab 6.1 file (Figure 6.4). **You will:** enable LLDP, gather CDP and LLDP evidence on every network device, and find the seven errors planted in the documentation below.

**Step 1 — enable LLDP everywhere** (DSW1, DSW2, SW1, SW2, R1):

```
configure terminal
lldp run
end
```

**Step 2 — gather evidence** on each device: `show cdp neighbors detail`, `show lldp neighbors`, `show interfaces description`. On SW2 also run `show mac address-table interface fastethernet0/2`. (Packet Tracer supports CDP and LLDP; LLDP details shown may be shorter than on real IOS.)

**Step 3 — audit this documentation.** Mark each row Match or Mismatch, and say which side you would correct.

| # | Device / port | Documented neighbor | Documented neighbor port | Other documented fact | Your finding |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | DSW1 Gi1/0/1 | SW1 | Gi0/1 | — |  |
| 2 | DSW1 Gi1/0/3 | SW1 | Gi0/3 | — |  |
| 3 | DSW1 Gi1/0/2 | SW3 | Gi0/1 | — |  |
| 4 | DSW1 Gi1/0/21 | DSW2 | Gi1/0/22 | — |  |
| 5 | DSW1 Gi1/0/24 | R1 | G0/0/1 | — |  |
| 6 | DSW1 Gi1/0/4 | SW2 | Gi0/2 | SW2 model WS-C3650 |  |
| 7 | SW1 (management) | — | — | Vlan99 address 10.10.99.12 |  |
| 8 | SW2 Fa0/3 | Printer1 | — | — |  |

**Step 4 — security change.** On DSW1 enter `interface gigabitethernet1/0/24` then `no cdp enable`. After about three minutes (the holdtime), `show cdp neighbors` on R1 no longer lists DSW1. Explain why this is good practice on links toward untrusted networks, then restore `cdp enable`.

**Answer key (hide until done)**

| # | Result | Evidence | Correct value |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Match | CDP: SW1, Port ID Gi0/1 | — |
| 2 | Mismatch | Port ID Gi0/2 | SW1 Gi0/2 (second Po1 member) |
| 3 | Mismatch | Device ID SW2 | SW2 Gi0/1 |
| 4 | Mismatch | Port ID Gi1/0/21 | DSW2 Gi1/0/21 (Gi1/0/22 pairs with Gi1/0/22) |
| 5 | Mismatch | Port ID G0/0/0 | R1 G0/0/0 |
| 6 | Mismatch (model) | Platform WS-C2960 | Port is correct; model is 2960 |
| 7 | Mismatch | CDP entry address 10.10.99.11 | 10.10.99.11 |
| 8 | Mismatch | MAC table: printer's MAC on Fa0/2 | SW2 Fa0/2 (printers do not run CDP, so the MAC table is the evidence) |

In every case the network matches the Lab 5.1/6.1 design, so the **documentation** is what you correct.

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] LLDP enabled on all five network devices
- [ ] CDP and LLDP neighbor tables collected from each device
- [ ] All seven mismatches found and the correct values recorded
- [ ] CDP disabled and restored on DSW1 Gi1/0/24, with the effect explained
- [ ] Interface descriptions updated to match the corrected documentation

### 8.10 Chapter 8 summary notes (exam revision)

- **CDP:** Cisco proprietary, on by default, every 60 s, holdtime 180 s; CDPv2 adds native VLAN and duplex. `cdp run` (global), `cdp enable` (interface).
- **LLDP:** IEEE 802.1AB, often off by default on Cisco IOS (`lldp run`), every 30 s, holdtime 120 s, reinit 2 s; per-interface `lldp transmit` / `lldp receive`. LLDP-MED gives phones their voice VLAN and PoE information.
- **Scope:** directly connected neighbors only; advertisements are never forwarded. PCs and printers usually run neither protocol.
- **Reading output:** **Local Intrfce / Local Intf = my port; Port ID = the neighbor's port.** Each EtherChannel member is listed separately.
- **`detail` / `show cdp entry`** adds management IP, full platform, software version, native VLAN and duplex.
- **Validation:** compare hostname, both ports, model, IP (and VLAN/duplex) for every link; correct whichever side breaks the approved design; verify hosts with MAC, ARP and DHCP tables; publish dated documentation.
- **Security:** disable on untrusted or Internet-facing ports; keep on infrastructure links and phone ports.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "Port ID is my port" | Port ID is the neighbor's port |
| "CDP shows the whole network" | Only directly connected neighbors |
| "LLDP works as soon as it is configured on one side" | It must run on both ends (transmit on one, receive on the other) |
| "A mismatch means the cabling is wrong" | Often the documentation is wrong; check the approved design |
| "CDP can find the printer" | Printers and PCs rarely run CDP; use the MAC address table |

### 8.11 Practice questions — Chapter 8

**1. (Output interpretation)**

```
Device ID   Local Intrfce   Holdtme   Capability   Platform    Port ID
CORE-2      Gig 1/0/48      132       R S I        C9300-48P   Gig 1/1/1
```

The documentation says "DSW1 Gi1/1/1 connects to CORE-2 Gi1/0/48". Is it correct?

**2. (Recall)** Which protocol is the IEEE standard for neighbor discovery?

A. CDP  B. LLDP  C. DTP  D. VTP

**3. (Configuration)** LLDP neighbors are empty on a Cisco switch connected to a non-Cisco switch that runs LLDP. What is the most likely missing command?

**4. (Concept)** R1 connects to DSW1, which connects to SW1. Will R1's `show cdp neighbors` list SW1? Why?

**5. (Recall)** What are the default CDP advertisement timer and holdtime?

A. 30 s / 120 s  B. 60 s / 180 s  C. 10 s / 40 s  D. 2 s / 6 s

**6. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which pieces of information does `show cdp neighbors detail` provide that `show cdp neighbors` does not?

A. Management IP address  B. Device ID  C. Software version  D. Local interface  E. Capability

**7. (Security)** On which interface should CDP be disabled first?

A. A trunk to another campus switch  B. An IP phone port  C. The router interface facing the ISP  D. An EtherChannel member between distribution switches

**8. (Concept)** What does LLDP-MED add for an IP phone?

**9. (Validation)** CDP shows that DSW1 Gi1/0/2 connects to SW2 Gi0/1, but the documentation says SW2 Gi0/2. The network works and Gi0/1 is in the approved cabling plan. What do you change?

**10. (Validation)** How would you verify the documented port of a printer that does not run CDP or LLDP?

**11. (Troubleshooting)** You re-cable SW1 from DSW1 Gi1/0/1 to Gi1/0/5. For a few minutes `show cdp neighbors` shows SW1 on both ports. Why?

**12. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A network assistant analyses `show cdp neighbors` on DSW1 and reports: *"R1 is connected to port G0/0/0 of DSW1."* The line reads `R1  Gig 1/0/24  ...  ISR4331  Gig 0/0/0`. Is the assistant right?

### 8.12 Answer key with explanations

1. **No — the ports are swapped.** The local port (DSW1's) is Gi1/0/48 and CORE-2's port is Gi1/1/1. Correct documentation: *DSW1 Gi1/0/48 connects to CORE-2 Gi1/1/1.*
2. **B.** LLDP is IEEE 802.1AB; CDP is Cisco proprietary; DTP and VTP are not discovery protocols.
3. **`lldp run`** in global configuration. LLDP is often disabled by default on Cisco IOS.
4. **No.** CDP advertisements are not forwarded, so each device sees only directly connected neighbors. You must log in to DSW1 to see SW1.
5. **B.** CDP sends every 60 seconds and keeps entries for 180 seconds. A matches LLDP.
6. **A and C.** The summary already shows device ID, local interface, capability, platform and port ID; the detail view adds addresses, version, native VLAN and duplex.
7. **C.** Advertising device details to the ISP or the Internet helps attackers and serves no purpose. Phone ports need CDP/LLDP for the voice VLAN; internal infrastructure links benefit from it.
8. It lets the switch advertise the **voice VLAN** (and QoS) in a network-policy TLV, and lets the phone report its **PoE** requirements and inventory — the multi-vendor equivalent of what CDP does for Cisco phones.
9. **The documentation.** The network is working and matches the approved design, so update the port table (and interface descriptions).
10. Use **`show mac address-table interface <port>`** (and the ARP or DHCP table to match the printer's MAC to its IP), because the printer is invisible to CDP and LLDP.
11. The old entry on Gi1/0/1 stays until its **holdtime (180 s)** expires; the new entry on Gi1/0/5 appears immediately. `clear cdp table` removes it sooner.
12. **No.** Gig 1/0/24 is **DSW1's** port (Local Intrfce) and Gig 0/0/0 is **R1's** port (Port ID). The correct reading is: DSW1 Gi1/0/24 connects to R1 G0/0/0. Check every assistant summary of neighbor output for this column swap.

All 3 figures were rendered, embedded beside the text they explain, and checked visually after rendering. Figure 8.2's first render had two arrows that implied the wrong path through the workflow; they were corrected and re-checked.

| Figure | Title | Section | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 8.1 | Discovery scope: directly connected neighbors only | 8.2 | Rendered, embedded, checked |
| 8.2 | Documentation validation workflow | 8.6 | Rendered, embedded, checked after one fix |
| 8.3 | Documented vs discovered neighbors of DSW1 | 8.7 | Rendered, embedded, checked |
