### 8.14 Chapter 8 progress record

**Objective completed:** **2.3** — CDP and LLDP operation, configuration, output interpretation, documentation validation, and security.

**Related objectives that reuse this chapter:** 2.2.c voice VLAN delivery (Chapter 7), 2.4 troubleshooting with show commands (Chapter 15), 4.7 Layer 2 security (Chapter 23).

**Next:** Chapter 9 — **2.5 Configure Rapid PVST+**: root bridge, port roles and states, PortFast, root guard, loop guard and BPDU guard.

## Chapter 9 — Rapid PVST+ and STP protection

**Objective covered:** **2.5 Configure operations of the Rapid Per VLAN Spanning Tree Protocol (Rapid PVST+)**: **2.5.a** root port, root bridge (primary/secondary) and other port names; **2.5.b** port states and roles; **2.5.c** PortFast; **2.5.d** root guard, loop guard and BPDU guard. In v1.1 this was "interpret"; v2.0 says **configure**.

### 9.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Explain why redundant Layer 2 links create loops and what a loop does to a network.
2. Describe the bridge ID and predict the root bridge, root ports, designated ports and alternate ports for any VLAN.
3. Name the RSTP port roles and states and explain rapid convergence.
4. Configure Rapid PVST+, root primary/secondary, priorities and costs, and share load across VLANs.
5. Configure PortFast, BPDU guard, root guard and loop guard on the right ports.
6. Read `show spanning-tree` output and troubleshoot STP problems.

### 9.2 Why loops are deadly

Redundant links between switches are good: if one fails, another carries traffic. But Ethernet frames have **no TTL field**, so a frame caught in a Layer 2 loop circulates forever (Figure 9.1). Three things happen within seconds:

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch09/fig-09-01-layer2-loop.svg" alt="Figure 9.1 — A Layer 2 loop without STP: one broadcast becomes an endless storm" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 9.1</b> — A Layer 2 loop without STP: one broadcast becomes an endless storm</sub></p>

In Figure 9.1 the red arrows follow one copy of PC1's broadcast around the triangle. Each switch floods it out every port except the one it arrived on, so it never stops and every lap adds more copies. The numbered effects below happen in parallel.

1. **Broadcast storm** — every switch floods each broadcast out every other port, so copies multiply until links and CPUs are saturated.
2. **MAC address table instability** — the same source MAC arrives on different ports, so the switch keeps relearning it (`%SW_MATM-4-MACFLAP_NOTIF`).
3. **Duplicate frames** — hosts receive multiple copies of the same unicast frame.

**Spanning Tree Protocol (STP)** prevents this by choosing one loop-free tree through the switches and **blocking** the redundant ports. If an active link fails, STP unblocks a backup path.

### 9.3 The STP family

| Protocol | Standard | Instances | Convergence | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| STP | IEEE 802.1D | One for all VLANs | 30–50 s | Original; obsolete |
| PVST+ | Cisco | One per VLAN | 30–50 s | 802.1D per VLAN |
| RSTP | IEEE 802.1w | One for all VLANs | Typically under a few seconds | Rapid convergence |
| **Rapid PVST+** | Cisco | **One RSTP instance per VLAN** | Rapid | **The v2.0 objective; default on many current Cisco switches** |
| MSTP | IEEE 802.1s | Groups VLANs into a few instances | Rapid | Large networks; not a v2.0 objective |

"Per VLAN" means each VLAN can have a different root bridge and different blocked ports, which lets you use all links across different VLANs (9.9).

### 9.4 Bridge ID and BPDUs

Switches running STP exchange **BPDUs** (Bridge Protocol Data Units) to build the tree. In RSTP every switch sends a BPDU on its designated ports every **hello time (2 s)**; a neighbor is considered lost after three missed hellos (6 s). Each BPDU carries the sender's view of who the root is, its cost to reach the root, and its own bridge ID.

The **bridge ID (BID)** is 8 bytes (Figure 9.2):

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch09/fig-09-02-bridge-id.svg" alt="Figure 9.2 — Bridge ID structure with a VLAN 10 example" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 9.2</b> — Bridge ID structure with a VLAN 10 example</sub></p>

Figure 9.2 shows why priorities jump in steps of 4096: the priority has only 4 bits, sitting above the 12-bit VLAN field. IOS prints the two together as one number, which is why VLAN 10's default appears as 32778.

| Part | Bits | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bridge priority | 4 | Configurable in steps of **4096**: 0, 4096, 8192 … 61440. Default **32768** |
| Extended system ID | 12 | The **VLAN number**, added to the priority |
| MAC address | 48 | A MAC address of the switch (the tie-breaker) |

So in VLAN 10 a switch with default priority shows **32778** (32768 + 10). **Lower BID is better.** The priority is compared first; the MAC address decides only when priorities are equal.

### 9.5 Port cost

Each port has a **cost** based on its speed; a switch's **root path cost** is the sum of the costs of the ports a BPDU enters on the way from the root. Cisco switches commonly use the short method by default; some platforms use the long method (`spanning-tree pathcost method long`).

| Link speed | Short (802.1D-1998) cost | Long (802.1t) cost |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 10 Mb/s | 100 | 2,000,000 |
| 100 Mb/s | 19 | 200,000 |
| 1 Gb/s | 4 | 20,000 |
| 10 Gb/s | 2 | 2,000 |
| 2 × 1 Gb/s EtherChannel | 3 | 10,000 |

### 9.6 The election in four steps

Worked example (Figure 9.3): SW-A, SW-B and SW-C in a triangle, all 1 Gb/s links (cost 4), all at default priority, VLAN 10. MACs: SW-A 0011.1111.1111, SW-B 0022.2222.2222, SW-C 0033.3333.3333.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch09/fig-09-03-stp-election.svg" alt="Figure 9.3 — STP election result for VLAN 10: root bridge, root ports, designated ports and the alternate port" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 9.3</b> — STP election result for VLAN 10: root bridge, root ports, designated ports and the alternate port</sub></p>

Figure 9.3 is the end state of the four steps below. Every link has exactly one designated port, every non-root switch exactly one root port, and the single leftover port (red) discards.

1. **Elect one root bridge per VLAN — lowest BID.** All priorities are 32778, so the lowest MAC wins: **SW-A is root**. Every port on the root bridge is a **designated port**.
2. **Each non-root switch picks one root port — lowest root path cost.** SW-B reaches SW-A directly for 4 (via C would be 8): **SW-B Gi0/1 is its root port**. Likewise **SW-C Gi0/1**.
3. **Each link (segment) picks one designated port** — the side advertising the lowest root path cost. On the SW-B–SW-C link both advertise cost 4, a tie.
4. **Break ties in this order:** lowest root path cost → lowest **sender bridge ID** → lowest sender port priority → lowest sender port number. SW-B's BID is lower than SW-C's, so **SW-B Gi0/2 is designated**. SW-C Gi0/2 is neither root nor designated, so it becomes an **alternate** port and **discards** — the loop is broken.

**Exam method:** find the root first; then on each non-root switch find its cheapest path to the root (root port); then on each link the end closer to the root is designated; every port left over is alternate (or backup) and blocks.

**Common traps:** the root bridge is chosen by **BID, not by speed or position**, so an old access switch can become root by accident if nobody sets priorities. Cost is added for the ports a BPDU **enters**, not exits.

### 9.7 RSTP port roles (2.5.a)

| Role | Abbreviation in IOS | Meaning | State in a stable network |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Root port | Root | The one port on a non-root switch with the best path to the root bridge | Forwarding |
| Designated port | Desg | The one port on each link that forwards toward that link (all ports of the root bridge are designated) | Forwarding |
| Alternate port | Altn | A backup path to the root through a **different** switch; ready to become the root port instantly | Discarding |
| Backup port | Back | A backup for a designated port on the **same** shared segment (rare: needs a hub) | Discarding |
| Disabled | — | Shut down or not participating | — |

**Root bridge "primary" and "secondary"** are not port roles; they are the switches you choose to be root and backup root for a VLAN (9.9).

### 9.8 RSTP port states (2.5.b) and rapid convergence

RSTP reduces 802.1D's five states to three:

| 802.1D state | RSTP state | Learns MACs? | Forwards frames? |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Disabled | Discarding | No | No |
| Blocking | Discarding | No | No |
| Listening | Discarding | No | No |
| Learning | Learning | Yes | No |
| Forwarding | Forwarding | Yes | Yes |

**Why RSTP is fast:**

1. **Alternate ports are pre-computed.** If the root port fails, the alternate port becomes the root port and starts forwarding immediately.
2. **Proposal/agreement handshake.** On a point-to-point link a new designated port sends a *proposal*; the neighbor syncs its own ports and replies with an *agreement*, and the port forwards at once instead of waiting through timers.
3. **Edge ports** (PortFast) go straight to forwarding because nothing can loop through an end host.
4. **Faster failure detection:** three missed hellos (6 s) instead of the 20 s max age.

**Link types** decide whether the handshake can be used: **point-to-point** (full-duplex links between switches, the normal case), **shared** (half duplex, old hubs — falls back to slower timers) and **edge** (PortFast ports). `show spanning-tree` prints them in the Type column (`P2p`, `Shr`, `P2p Edge`).

**Timers (defaults):** hello 2 s, max age 20 s, forward delay 15 s. RSTP rarely relies on max age and forward delay; you should not need to change any of them for the exam.

### 9.9 Configuring Rapid PVST+

**Step 1 — run Rapid PVST+ on every switch.** Some platforms default to PVST+ (802.1D); mixing works but loses the rapid behaviour on those links.

```
DSW1(config)# spanning-tree mode rapid-pvst
```

**Step 2 — choose the root bridges deliberately (2.5.a).** Put the root on a distribution switch, never leave it to chance:

```
DSW1(config)# spanning-tree vlan 10,30 root primary       ! DSW1 = root for VLANs 10 and 30
DSW1(config)# spanning-tree vlan 40,99 root secondary     ! backup root for 40 and 99
DSW2(config)# spanning-tree vlan 40,99 root primary
DSW2(config)# spanning-tree vlan 10,30 root secondary
```

`root primary` is a **macro**: it sets the priority to 24576, or lower if needed to beat the current root. `root secondary` sets 28672, so the switch takes over only if the primary fails. Both are one-time calculations written into the configuration as `spanning-tree vlan X priority N`.

You can also set the value directly (multiples of 4096):

```
DSW1(config)# spanning-tree vlan 10 priority 4096
```

**Step 3 (optional) — influence the path with cost or port priority** on an interface:

```
SW1(config)# interface fastethernet0/24
SW1(config-if)# spanning-tree vlan 40 cost 2                ! make this path cheaper for VLAN 40
SW1(config-if)# spanning-tree vlan 40 port-priority 64      ! 0-240 in steps of 16; default 128; lower wins
```

Remember: **cost** changes the decision on the switch where you configure it (its root port); **port priority** affects the *neighbor's* choice, because the neighbor compares the sender's port ID.

| Command | Mode | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `spanning-tree mode rapid-pvst` | Global | Enable Rapid PVST+ |
| `spanning-tree vlan <list> root primary` / `secondary` | Global | Make this switch root / backup root |
| `spanning-tree vlan <list> priority <0–61440>` | Global | Set bridge priority (multiple of 4096) |
| `spanning-tree vlan <list> cost <n>` | Interface | Override the port cost for those VLANs |
| `spanning-tree vlan <list> port-priority <0–240>` | Interface | Override port priority (multiple of 16) |
| `spanning-tree pathcost method {short \| long}` | Global | Choose the cost scale |

**Per-VLAN load sharing.** With DSW1 root for VLANs 10 and 30 and DSW2 root for 40 and 99, each VLAN builds its own tree, so different VLANs block different links and both distribution switches carry traffic. Align the STP root with the VLAN's default gateway (the SVI or FHRP active router, Chapter 13) so traffic does not take an extra hop.

### 9.10 PortFast (2.5.c)

Without PortFast, a newly connected access port must negotiate or wait before forwarding; a PC may time out on DHCP. **PortFast** marks a port as an **edge port** that moves straight to forwarding, because an end host cannot create a loop.

```
SW1(config)# interface fastethernet0/1
SW1(config-if)# spanning-tree portfast                 ! one access port
SW1(config)# spanning-tree portfast default            ! global: all access (non-trunk) ports
SW1(config-if)# spanning-tree portfast trunk           ! a trunk to a server/hypervisor (Chapter 7)
```

Newer IOS releases use `spanning-tree portfast edge`, `spanning-tree portfast edge default` and `spanning-tree portfast edge trunk`. IOS prints a warning when you enable PortFast, reminding you to use it only on host ports. A PortFast port still sends BPDUs; if it *receives* one it loses edge status and runs normal STP. **Never enable PortFast on a link to another switch.**

### 9.11 BPDU guard, root guard and loop guard (2.5.d)

These three features protect the tree from mistakes and attacks. Figure 9.4 shows where each belongs.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch09/fig-09-04-stp-protection-placement.svg" alt="Figure 9.4 — Placement of root guard, loop guard, PortFast and BPDU guard in the campus" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 9.4</b> — Placement of root guard, loop guard, PortFast and BPDU guard in the campus</sub></p>

In Figure 9.4 each coloured dot marks the port end where a feature is configured, and the text beside each row names it. Root guard sits on the distribution side of the access links, loop guard on the access side of the same links, and PortFast with BPDU guard on every host port.

**BPDU guard** — for edge ports. If any BPDU arrives (someone plugged in a switch), the port is **err-disabled** immediately.

```
SW1(config-if)# spanning-tree bpduguard enable                 ! per port
SW1(config)# spanning-tree portfast bpduguard default          ! global: all PortFast ports
%SPANTREE-2-BLOCK_BPDUGUARD: Received BPDU on port Fa0/1 with BPDU Guard enabled. Disabling port.
%PM-4-ERR_DISABLE: bpduguard error detected on Fa0/1, putting Fa0/1 in err-disable state
```

Recover by removing the cause and then `shutdown` / `no shutdown`, or automatically with `errdisable recovery cause bpduguard` and `errdisable recovery interval 300` (seconds).

**Root guard** — for designated ports that should never lead toward the root, typically distribution-switch downlinks to access switches. If a **superior BPDU** (one claiming a better root) arrives, the port is placed in **root-inconsistent** state (discarding). It recovers **automatically** when the superior BPDUs stop.

```
DSW1(config)# interface port-channel 1
DSW1(config-if)# spanning-tree guard root
%SPANTREE-2-ROOTGUARD_BLOCK: Root guard blocking port Port-channel1 on VLAN0010.
```

**Loop guard** — for root and alternate ports (the uplinks of access switches). If BPDUs **stop arriving** on such a port — for example because one fibre strand failed and the link is now unidirectional — normal STP would wrongly turn it into a forwarding designated port and create a loop. Loop guard instead puts it in **loop-inconsistent** state (discarding) until BPDUs return.

```
SW1(config)# spanning-tree loopguard default        ! global: all point-to-point ports
SW1(config-if)# spanning-tree guard loop            ! or per port
```

| Feature | Put it on | Triggered by | Action | Recovery |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PortFast | Edge (host) ports | — | Port forwards immediately | — |
| BPDU guard | Edge ports | **Any** BPDU received | **err-disabled** | Manual bounce or errdisable recovery |
| Root guard | Distribution downlinks (designated ports) | **Superior** BPDU received | root-inconsistent (discarding) | Automatic when superior BPDUs stop |
| Loop guard | Access uplinks (root and alternate ports) | **Missing** BPDUs | loop-inconsistent (discarding) | Automatic when BPDUs resume |

Root guard and loop guard cannot be enabled on the same port. **BPDU filter** (stops sending and receiving BPDUs) also exists, but it disables protection and is not a v2.0 objective; avoid it unless a design specifically requires it.

`show spanning-tree inconsistentports` lists ports blocked by root guard or loop guard; `show interfaces status err-disabled` lists ports shut by BPDU guard.

### 9.12 Reading `show spanning-tree`

On SW1 in the Lab 9.1 topology (VLAN 10, DSW1 is root). Port numbers and MAC addresses are examples.

```
SW1# show spanning-tree vlan 10

VLAN0010
  Spanning tree enabled protocol rstp
  Root ID    Priority    24586
             Address     0002.4a11.1111
             Cost        3
             Port        65 (Port-channel1)
             Hello Time   2 sec  Max Age 20 sec  Forward Delay 15 sec

  Bridge ID  Priority    32778  (priority 32768 sys-id-ext 10)
             Address     0003.e422.2222
             Hello Time   2 sec  Max Age 20 sec  Forward Delay 15 sec
             Aging Time  300 sec

Interface           Role Sts Cost      Prio.Nbr Type
------------------- ---- --- --------- -------- --------------------------------
Fa0/1               Desg FWD 19        128.1    P2p Edge
Fa0/2               Desg FWD 19        128.2    P2p Edge
Fa0/24              Desg FWD 19        128.24   P2p
Po1                 Root FWD 3         128.65   P2p
```

| Part | What it tells you |
| --- | --- |
| `protocol rstp` | Rapid PVST+ is running (`ieee` would mean legacy PVST+) |
| **Root ID** | Who the root is: priority 24586 = 24576 (root primary) + VLAN 10 |
| Root ID **Cost 3, Port Po1** | SW1's root path cost and its root port. On the root bridge itself this block says **"This bridge is the root"** |
| **Bridge ID** | This switch's own BID; higher than the root's, so it is not root |
| Role / Sts | Root FWD, Desg FWD, Altn BLK (shown as BLK for discarding) |
| Cost | The port's cost (19 = 100 Mb/s, 3 = 2 × 1 Gb/s bundle) |
| Prio.Nbr | Port priority and port number (the port ID) |
| Type | P2p, Shr, and **Edge** for PortFast ports |

**Useful commands:**

| Command | Use it to check |
| --- | --- |
| `show spanning-tree` | Every VLAN on the switch |
| `show spanning-tree vlan 10` | One VLAN in detail |
| `show spanning-tree root` | The root bridge, cost and root port for every VLAN, one line each |
| `show spanning-tree summary` | Mode, which global features (PortFast default, BPDU guard default, loop guard) are on, port counts per state |
| `show spanning-tree interface fa0/24 detail` | One port's role, state, and BPDUs sent/received |
| `show spanning-tree inconsistentports` | Ports blocked by root guard or loop guard |
| `show interfaces status err-disabled` | Ports shut by BPDU guard |

### 9.13 Troubleshooting Rapid PVST+

| Symptom | Likely cause | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Traffic takes a strange path; an access switch is root | Root left to default priorities; oldest switch (lowest MAC) won | `root primary` / `secondary` on the distribution switches |
| A new switch plugged into an access port takes over as root | No root guard on distribution downlinks, no BPDU guard on edge ports | Root guard on downlinks; BPDU guard on edge ports |
| Host port err-disabled | BPDU guard saw a BPDU (a switch or a bridging device was connected) | Remove the device, bounce the port, or enable errdisable recovery |
| PCs wait \~30 s for a link and DHCP fails | PortFast missing on access ports | `spanning-tree portfast` (or `portfast default`) |
| Broadcast storm, MAC flapping, high CPU | A loop: PortFast + BPDU filter on a switch link, a unidirectional link, or STP disabled | Find the loop, re-enable STP protection, add loop guard |
| Port stuck root-inconsistent | A downstream switch claims a better root | Fix that switch's priority; port recovers by itself |
| Port stuck loop-inconsistent | BPDUs not arriving (unidirectional fibre, faulty transceiver) | Fix the physical link (Chapter 1); port recovers when BPDUs resume |
| Slow convergence on some links | Neighbor runs legacy PVST+, or link type shared (half duplex) | `spanning-tree mode rapid-pvst` everywhere; fix duplex |

### 9.14 Packet Tracer Lab 9.1 — Rapid PVST+ on a looped campus (Intermediate–Advanced)

**Objectives:** 2.5.a–d. **Start from:** your Lab 6.1 file. **You will:** add redundant Layer 2 links so STP has loops to break, choose root bridges per VLAN, predict and verify every port role, then add PortFast, BPDU guard, root guard and loop guard.

**New cables** (all copper crossover):

| Link | From | To | Speed | STP cost (short) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Distribution trunk | DSW1 Gi1/0/23 | DSW2 Gi1/0/23 | 1 Gb/s | 4 |
| SW1 second uplink | SW1 Fa0/24 | DSW2 Gi1/0/5 | 100 Mb/s | 19 |
| SW2 second uplink | SW2 Fa0/24 | DSW2 Gi1/0/6 | 100 Mb/s | 19 |
| Existing Po1 / Po2 | DSW1 to SW1 / SW2 | — | 2 × 1 Gb/s | 3 |

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch09/fig-09-05-lab-9-1-topology.svg" alt="Figure 9.5 — Lab 9.1 topology with the expected Rapid PVST+ port roles for VLAN 10" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 9.5</b> — Lab 9.1 topology with the expected Rapid PVST+ port roles for VLAN 10</sub></p>

Figure 9.5 combines the Lab 6.1 campus with the three new links from the table above. Line styles show each link's cost; the labels give every port's role in VLAN 10. Try to derive them yourself before reading them off the figure.

**DSW1 additions**

```
configure terminal
spanning-tree mode rapid-pvst
spanning-tree vlan 10,30 root primary
spanning-tree vlan 40,99 root secondary
interface gigabitethernet1/0/23
 description Trunk to DSW2 Gi1/0/23
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
interface range port-channel 1 - 2
 spanning-tree guard root
end
```

**DSW2 additions** (VLANs first; DSW2 has no SVIs in this lab)

```
configure terminal
vlan 10
 name STAFF
vlan 30
 name WIRELESS
vlan 40
 name PRINTERS
vlan 99
 name MGMT
vlan 999
 name NATIVE
exit
spanning-tree mode rapid-pvst
spanning-tree vlan 40,99 root primary
spanning-tree vlan 10,30 root secondary
interface range gigabitethernet1/0/5 - 6 , gigabitethernet1/0/23
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
end
```

**SW1 and SW2 additions**

```
configure terminal
spanning-tree mode rapid-pvst
spanning-tree portfast default
spanning-tree portfast bpduguard default
spanning-tree loopguard default
interface fastethernet0/24
 description Uplink to DSW2
 switchport mode trunk
 switchport trunk native vlan 999
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,30,40,99
 no shutdown
end
```

If IOS rejects `switchport mode trunk` on the 3650, add `switchport trunk encapsulation dot1q` first. **PT note:** Packet Tracer supports Rapid PVST+, PortFast, BPDU guard and root guard; loop guard commands are accepted on recent versions but a unidirectional link cannot be simulated, so treat loop guard as configuration practice.

**Why no root guard on DSW2's downlinks here?** Root guard forces a port to stay designated. In VLANs 10 and 30, DSW2 Gi1/0/5 and Gi1/0/6 correctly hear a *better* path to the root (DSW1) from the access switches, so root guard would show them as **root-inconsistent** instead of alternate, and would stop DSW2 using them as a backup path. Many production designs accept that trade-off and protect every downlink, as Figure 9.4 shows; this lab leaves them unprotected so the predicted roles stay simple, and Lab 9.2 shows the risk.

**Predict first, then verify.** Work out the roles yourself using 9.6 before running the commands.

| Device and port | VLAN 10 (root DSW1) | VLAN 40 (root DSW2) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DSW1 Gi1/0/23 | Desg FWD | **Root** FWD (cost 4) |
| DSW1 Po1, Po2 | Desg FWD | Desg FWD |
| DSW2 Gi1/0/23 | **Root** FWD (cost 4) | Desg FWD |
| DSW2 Gi1/0/5, Gi1/0/6 | **Altn BLK** | Desg FWD |
| SW1 Po1 / SW2 Po2 | **Root** FWD (cost 3) | **Root** FWD (cost 3 + 4 = 7) |
| SW1 Fa0/24 / SW2 Fa0/24 | Desg FWD | **Altn BLK** (direct cost 19 > 7) |
| SW1 Fa0/1–2 / SW2 Fa0/1–2 | Desg FWD, P2p Edge | Desg FWD, P2p Edge |

Notice VLAN 40: even though DSW2 is root, SW1 still reaches it through DSW1 because the 100 Mb/s link costs 19 while the path via Po1 and Gi1/0/23 costs only 7. **Cost decides, not distance.**

**Verification:** `show spanning-tree root` on every switch (root bridge and root port per VLAN); `show spanning-tree vlan 10` and `vlan 40` on DSW2 and SW1; `show spanning-tree summary` on SW1 (PortFast, BPDU guard and loop guard defaults enabled).

**Experiments**

1. **Failover:** start a long ping from PC1 to PC3, then shut DSW1 Gi1/0/23. In VLAN 10, DSW2 loses its root port and one of its alternate ports takes over within about a second. Restore the link.
2. **Root guard:** on SW2 shut Fa0/24 (so Po2 is its only uplink), then enter `spanning-tree vlan 10 priority 0`. SW2's superior BPDUs make DSW1 Po2 **root-inconsistent** (`show spanning-tree inconsistentports`); DSW1 stays root. Remove the command, watch Po2 recover on its own, then re-enable SW2 Fa0/24.
3. **BPDU guard:** `no shutdown` SW1 Fa0/3 and connect a spare 2960 to it. Fa0/3 becomes **err-disabled** (`show interfaces status err-disabled`). Remove the switch, then `shutdown` / `no shutdown`.

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] Predicted role table completed before verifying, and every prediction checked
- [ ] DSW1 root for VLANs 10/30, DSW2 root for 40/99
- [ ] PortFast and BPDU guard on all host ports; root guard on distribution downlinks; loop guard default on access switches
- [ ] All three experiments reproduced and explained
- [ ] File saved (Chapter 13 adds HSRP to this topology)

### 9.15 Lab 9.2 — STP challenge

Apply to a copy of Lab 9.1, then diagnose using show commands only: (1) on SW2, `spanning-tree vlan 10,30 priority 4096`; (2) on SW1, `no spanning-tree portfast default` and `no spanning-tree portfast bpduguard default`; (3) on DSW2, `spanning-tree mode pvst`.

**Answer key (hide until done):** (1) Root guard puts DSW1 Po2 into root-inconsistent state, but SW2's superior BPDUs still reach DSW2 through the unprotected Fa0/24 link, so SW2 becomes root for VLANs 10 and 30 anyway and traffic is pulled across the slow 100 Mb/s link — the lesson is that root guard must cover every path toward the access layer. Fix SW2's priority. (2) SW1's host ports lose Edge status (Type no longer shows Edge) and BPDU protection; PCs take longer to come up — restore both defaults. (3) DSW2 runs legacy PVST+ (`protocol ieee`), so its links converge slowly — set `rapid-pvst` again.

### 9.16 Chapter 9 summary notes (exam revision)

- **Loops** cause broadcast storms, MAC flapping and duplicate frames because Ethernet has no TTL. STP blocks redundant ports.
- **Rapid PVST+** = one RSTP instance per VLAN. `spanning-tree mode rapid-pvst`.
- **BID** = priority (multiple of 4096, default 32768) + VLAN number + MAC. Lowest wins. VLAN 10 default shows 32778.
- **Election:** lowest BID = root (all its ports designated) → each non-root switch's lowest-cost path = root port → each link's lowest-cost side = designated → the rest are alternate (discarding). Ties: root cost → sender BID → sender port priority → sender port number.
- **Costs (short):** 10M 100, 100M 19, 1G 4, 10G 2, 2×1G bundle 3.
- **Roles:** root, designated, alternate, backup. **States:** discarding, learning, forwarding.
- **`root primary`** = priority 24576 (or lower); **`root secondary`** = 28672. Put roots on distribution switches; share VLANs between them.
- **PortFast** on host ports only; **BPDU guard** err-disables on any BPDU; **root guard** blocks on a superior BPDU (root-inconsistent, auto-recovers); **loop guard** blocks when BPDUs stop (loop-inconsistent, auto-recovers).

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "The fastest or newest switch becomes root" | The lowest BID does; set priorities deliberately |
| "The root bridge has a root port" | It has none; all its ports are designated |
| "Port priority on my switch changes my root port" | Port priority influences the *neighbor's* choice; use cost to change your own |
| "Root guard and BPDU guard do the same thing" | BPDU guard err-disables on any BPDU; root guard blocks only on a superior BPDU and recovers by itself |
| "PortFast is fine on any port" | Only on host-facing ports; on a switch link it risks a loop |

### 9.17 Practice questions — Chapter 9

**1. (Election)** In VLAN 1, SW-X has priority 32769 and MAC 0000.1111.2222; SW-Y has priority 28673 and MAC 00ff.ffff.ffff. Which is root, and why?

**2. (Recall)** With default settings, what bridge priority does `show spanning-tree vlan 20` display?

A. 32768  B. 32788  C. 32748  D. 20

**3. (Configuration)** Which is a valid command?

A. `spanning-tree vlan 10 priority 1000`  B. `spanning-tree vlan 10 priority 8192`  C. `spanning-tree vlan 10 priority 65536`  D. `spanning-tree priority 8192 vlan 10`

**4. (Output interpretation)** Under Root ID, `show spanning-tree vlan 30` prints "This bridge is the root". What else must be true of this switch's ports in VLAN 30?

**5. (Recall)** What priority does `spanning-tree vlan 10 root primary` set if the current root uses the default priority?

**6. (Concept)** Which port roles forward traffic in a stable RSTP network? (Choose two.)

A. Root  B. Alternate  C. Designated  D. Backup  E. Disabled

**7. (Recall)** How many port states does RSTP define, and what are they?

**8. (Calculation)** A switch can reach the root over a 100 Mb/s link directly, or over two 1 Gb/s links through another switch. Using short costs, which path wins?

**9. (Election)** SW2 has two 1 Gb/s links to the root bridge SW1: SW2 Gi0/1 connects to SW1 Gi0/2, and SW2 Gi0/2 connects to SW1 Gi0/1. Port priorities are default. Which SW2 port becomes the root port?

**10. (Concept)** What happens when a port with BPDU guard receives a BPDU, and what happens when a port with root guard receives a superior BPDU?

**11. (Design)** A technician enables `spanning-tree portfast trunk` on the trunk between two access switches to speed up convergence. Is this acceptable?

**12. (Concept)** What problem does loop guard solve?

**13. (Log interpretation)** `%PM-4-ERR_DISABLE: bpduguard error detected on Gi1/0/7, putting Gi1/0/7 in err-disable state`. What happened and how do you restore the port?

**14. (Multiple response — choose two)** Where should root guard normally be configured?

A. Access-switch host ports  B. Distribution-switch ports facing access switches  C. The access switch's root port  D. Ports where an unauthorized root bridge could appear below the distribution layer  E. The root bridge's loopback interface

**15. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A user plugged a small unmanaged-looking switch into their desk port and it went err-disabled. A network assistant recommends: *"Run `no spanning-tree portfast bpduguard default` so this does not happen again."* Is this good advice?

### 9.18 Answer key with explanations

1. **SW-Y.** Priority is compared first and 28673 < 32769; the MAC address only breaks ties when priorities are equal.
2. **B.** 32768 + VLAN 20 = 32788 (the extended system ID).
3. **B.** Priorities must be multiples of 4096 between 0 and 61440. 1000 is not a multiple; 65536 is too large; D has the wrong syntax.
4. It has **no root port**; every one of its ports in VLAN 30 that is up is a **designated** (forwarding) port.
5. **24576** (it sets 24576, or lower if needed to beat the existing root). With a default root at 32768 + VLAN, 24576 wins.
6. **A and C.** Alternate and backup ports discard; disabled ports do not participate.
7. **Three:** discarding, learning, forwarding.
8. Direct 100 Mb/s costs **19**; two 1 Gb/s links cost 4 + 4 = **8**. The path through the other switch wins (lower total cost).
9. Costs and sender BID are equal (both links go to SW1), and port priority is 128 on both, so the **lowest sender port number** decides: the link to SW1 **Gi0/1**, which is SW2's **Gi0/2**. The neighbor's port ID matters, not your own.
10. **BPDU guard:** the port is **err-disabled** immediately and stays down until bounced or recovered. **Root guard:** the port goes **root-inconsistent** (discarding) and recovers automatically when the superior BPDUs stop.
11. **No.** PortFast tells STP the port cannot form a loop; between two switches it can. Use PortFast only toward hosts (and host trunks such as hypervisors).
12. If BPDUs stop arriving on a root or alternate port (for example on a unidirectional link), normal STP would make that port designated and forwarding, creating a loop. Loop guard puts it in **loop-inconsistent** (blocked) state instead until BPDUs return.
13. A **BPDU arrived on Gi1/0/7**, which has BPDU guard (usually because a switch was plugged in), so the port was err-disabled. Remove the device, then `shutdown` and `no shutdown` (or let `errdisable recovery cause bpduguard` restore it).
14. **B and D.** Root guard protects the root position at the distribution layer by blocking superior BPDUs coming from below. It is not used on host ports (BPDU guard is) or on root ports.
15. **No.** That removes protection from every PortFast port and allows a user's switch to join the STP domain, risking loops and a rogue root. The correct response is to remove the switch (or provide a properly configured one), then recover the port.

## Chapter 10 — Interpreting the routing table

**Objective covered:** **3.1 Interpret a routing table to identify the next hop for a packet** (routing protocol, prefix/mask, administrative distance, metric, and default route). This is Domain 3's foundation: static routing (Chapter 11), OSPF (Chapter 12) and FHRP (Chapter 13) all end up as lines in this table.

### 10.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Describe step by step how a router forwards a packet.
2. Read every field of an IPv4 and IPv6 routing table entry.
3. Apply the three route-selection rules in the right order: longest prefix match, administrative distance, metric.
4. Explain the gateway of last resort and the default route.
5. Find the next hop and exit interface for any destination from `show ip route` output.

### 10.2 How a router forwards a packet

A router makes the same decision for every packet, independently (Figure 10.1):

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch10/fig-10-01-router-forwarding-process.svg" alt="Figure 10.1 — The router's per-packet forwarding process" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 10.1</b> — The router's per-packet forwarding process</sub></p>

Figure 10.1 follows one packet from arrival to departure. The two red boxes are the only routing reasons to drop it: the TTL ran out, or no route matched. Everything this chapter teaches happens in steps 3 to 5.

1. **Receive the frame.** Check the FCS; accept it only if the destination MAC is the router's own interface MAC (or broadcast/multicast it listens to).
2. **De-encapsulate** to the IP packet and **decrement the TTL**. If TTL reaches 0, drop it and send an ICMP Time Exceeded back to the source (this is how traceroute works).
3. **Look up the destination IP** in the routing table and choose the **longest matching prefix** (10.4).
4. **No match and no default route?** Drop the packet and send ICMP Destination Unreachable.
5. **Match found:** read the **next-hop address** and/or **exit interface**. If the next hop is not directly connected, look it up again (a *recursive* lookup).
6. **Resolve the next hop's MAC** with ARP (IPv4) or ND (IPv6). If the destination is on a directly connected network, ARP for the destination itself.
7. **Re-encapsulate** in a new frame for the exit interface (new source and destination MAC, new FCS) and send it.

The routing table (the **RIB**) is built by the control plane from connected interfaces, static routes and routing protocols. For speed, the forwarding plane uses a copy optimized for lookups (Cisco Express Forwarding, CEF), but its decisions follow the same table.

### 10.3 Anatomy of `show ip route`

This example is R1, the edge router of the campus, once OSPF (Chapter 12) runs inside the campus and R1 has a default route to an ISP. R1's G0/0/1 connects to the ISP on 198.51.100.0/30.

```
R1# show ip route
Codes: L - local, C - connected, S - static, R - RIP, M - mobile, B - BGP
       D - EIGRP, EX - EIGRP external, O - OSPF, IA - OSPF inter area
       N1 - OSPF NSSA external type 1, N2 - OSPF NSSA external type 2
       E1 - OSPF external type 1, E2 - OSPF external type 2
       i - IS-IS, * - candidate default, + - replicated route ...

Gateway of last resort is 198.51.100.1 to network 0.0.0.0

S*    0.0.0.0/0 [1/0] via 198.51.100.1
      10.0.0.0/8 is variably subnetted, 9 subnets, 4 masks
C        10.0.0.0/30 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
L        10.0.0.1/32 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
O        10.0.0.4/30 [110/2] via 10.0.0.2, 00:14:02, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
S        10.10.0.0/16 [1/0] via 10.0.0.2
O        10.10.10.0/24 [110/2] via 10.0.0.2, 00:14:02, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
O        10.10.30.0/24 [110/2] via 10.0.0.2, 00:14:02, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
O        10.10.40.0/24 [110/2] via 10.0.0.2, 00:14:02, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
O        10.10.99.0/24 [110/2] via 10.0.0.2, 00:14:02, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
O        10.255.0.2/32 [110/3] via 10.0.0.2, 00:14:02, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
      198.51.100.0/24 is variably subnetted, 2 subnets, 2 masks
C        198.51.100.0/30 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet0/0/1
L        198.51.100.2/32 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet0/0/1
```

**Reading it, block by block:**

- **Codes legend** — the first column of every route tells you its source. The ones to know: **C** connected network, **L** local (the router's own address, /32 or /128), **S** static, **S\*** static default (candidate default), **O** OSPF intra-area, **O IA** OSPF inter-area, **O E1/E2** OSPF external, **D** EIGRP, **D EX** EIGRP external, **R** RIP, **B** BGP.
- **Gateway of last resort** — the default route's next hop, used when nothing more specific matches. "Gateway of last resort is not set" means unmatched packets are dropped.
- **Header lines** such as `10.0.0.0/8 is variably subnetted, 9 subnets, 4 masks` are **not routes**. They group the entries that fall inside the classful network 10.0.0.0/8 and say how many different masks are in use. A packet to 10.200.1.1 does **not** match "10.0.0.0/8" here.
- **Route lines** (Figure 10.2 dissects one): code, destination prefix and length, **\[administrative distance/metric\]**, **via** next-hop address, age, exit interface.
- **Connected and local routes** have no brackets and no next hop: the network is directly attached.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch10/fig-10-02-routing-entry-anatomy.svg" alt="Figure 10.2 — Anatomy of one routing table entry from R1" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 10.2</b> — Anatomy of one routing table entry from R1</sub></p>

In Figure 10.2 each underlined part of the route line has a leader down to its meaning. Read any route the same way, left to right: how it was learned, where it goes, how trusted and how costly it is, and which neighbor and interface to use.

| Field | Example | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Code | O | How the route was learned |
| Prefix / length | 10.10.40.0/24 | Destination network and mask |
| Administrative distance | 110 | How trustworthy the source is; lower wins between sources |
| Metric | 2 | Cost within that protocol; lower wins between routes from the same protocol |
| Next hop | via 10.0.0.2 | The next router's address on a connected subnet |
| Age | 00:14:02 | Time since learned (dynamic routes only) |
| Exit interface | GigabitEthernet0/0/0 | Where the packet leaves this router |

**Administrative distance values to memorise (Cisco defaults):**

| Source | AD |
| --- | --- |
| Connected | 0 |
| Static | 1 |
| eBGP | 20 |
| EIGRP (internal) | 90 |
| OSPF | 110 |
| IS-IS | 115 |
| RIP | 120 |
| EIGRP external | 170 |
| iBGP | 200 |
| Default route learned from DHCP | 254 |
| Unknown / unusable | 255 (never installed) |

**Metrics are protocol-specific** and cannot be compared between protocols: OSPF uses **cost** (based on bandwidth), RIP uses **hop count**, EIGRP a **composite** of bandwidth and delay. That is exactly why AD exists.

### 10.4 The three decisions, and their order

Three different rules are often confused. They answer different questions, at different times (Figure 10.3):

| Rule | Question it answers | When | Compares |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Administrative distance** | Several sources offer the **same prefix**; which one goes into the table? | When building the table | Lowest AD |
| **Metric** | One protocol has several paths to the **same prefix**; which go in? | When building the table | Lowest metric; equal metrics all go in (ECMP) |
| **Longest prefix match** | A packet arrives; which installed route does it use? | Every packet | Most specific (longest) prefix that contains the destination |

**The trap:** AD and metric never compete across *different* prefixes. A static route to 10.10.0.0/16 (AD 1) does **not** beat an OSPF route to 10.10.40.0/24 (AD 110) for traffic to 10.10.40.25. Both are installed, because they are different prefixes, and the packet uses the /24 because it is longer.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch10/fig-10-03-route-selection.svg" alt="Figure 10.3 — Route selection: AD and metric when building the table, longest match when forwarding" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 10.3</b> — Route selection: AD and metric when building the table, longest match when forwarding</sub></p>

Read Figure 10.3 as two separate jobs. The left column runs whenever routing information changes and decides which routes are installed for each prefix. The right column runs for every packet and only ever looks at installed routes, choosing by prefix length alone.

### 10.5 Longest prefix match

A route **contains** a destination if the destination's first *n* bits equal the route's first *n* bits, where *n* is the prefix length. Among all routes that contain the destination, the router uses the one with the **largest n**.

**Worked example on R1's table — destination 10.10.40.25** (Figure 10.4):

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch10/fig-10-04-longest-prefix-match.svg" alt="Figure 10.4 — Longest prefix match for 10.10.40.25 against five of R1's routes" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 10.4</b> — Longest prefix match for 10.10.40.25 against five of R1's routes</sub></p>

In Figure 10.4 the three filled bars are routes that contain 10.10.40.25; the highlighted one is longest, so it wins. The /30 is longer still but does not contain the address, so length alone is never enough: the route must match first.

1. 0.0.0.0/0 contains everything (0 bits to compare).
2. 10.10.0.0/16: the first 16 bits (10.10) match. Contains it.
3. 10.10.40.0/24: the first 24 bits (10.10.40) match. Contains it.
4. 10.10.30.0/24: the third octet differs (30 ≠ 40). Does **not** contain it.
5. 10.0.0.4/30: covers only 10.0.0.4–10.0.0.7. Does not contain it.
6. The longest containing prefix is **10.10.40.0/24 → next hop 10.0.0.2, exit G0/0/0**.

**Quick method:** for each candidate, apply the candidate's mask to the destination (Chapter 2 block-size method) and see whether you get the candidate's network address. 10.10.40.25 with /24 → 10.10.40.0 ✓; with /16 → 10.10.0.0 ✓; the longer one wins.

### 10.6 Administrative distance and metric in action

**AD decides between sources.** If R1 also had `ip route 10.10.40.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.0.2`, the static route (AD 1) would replace the OSPF route (AD 110) for that same /24 in the table; the OSPF route stays in the OSPF database as a backup. If the static route were configured with AD 130 (a **floating static**, Chapter 11), OSPF would stay installed and the static would appear only if OSPF lost the route.

**Metric decides within one protocol.** If OSPF knew two paths to 10.20.0.0/24 with costs 3 and 5, only cost 3 is installed. With two paths of equal cost, **both** are installed and traffic is shared (equal-cost multipath, ECMP):

```
O        10.20.0.0/24 [110/3] via 10.0.0.6, 00:02:11, Port-channel3
                      [110/3] via 10.0.0.10, 00:02:11, GigabitEthernet1/0/24
```

OSPF installs up to four equal-cost paths by default (`maximum-paths` changes it).

### 10.7 Default routes and the gateway of last resort

A **default route** is a route to **0.0.0.0/0** (IPv4) or **::/0** (IPv6). It contains every address but is the **least specific** possible, so it is used only when nothing longer matches. Typical use: the edge router points the default at the ISP, and internal routers learn or point a default toward the edge.

- Configured statically: `ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 198.51.100.1` (shows as `S*`).
- Learned from a routing protocol (for example `O*E2` from OSPF's `default-information originate`, Chapter 12).
- Learned from DHCP on an Internet-facing interface (AD 254).

`Gateway of last resort is 198.51.100.1 to network 0.0.0.0` confirms which default is active. If it says **not set**, packets with no matching route are dropped with ICMP Unreachable.

### 10.8 Reading an IPv6 routing table

The same rules apply to IPv6; only the layout differs. Each route takes two lines, and connected and local routes show `[0/0]`.

```
R1# show ipv6 route
IPv6 Routing Table - default - 8 entries
Codes: C - Connected, L - Local, S - Static, U - Per-user Static route
       O - OSPF Intra, OI - OSPF Inter, OE1 - OSPF ext 1, OE2 - OSPF ext 2
       ND - ND Default, NDp - ND Prefix ...
S   ::/0 [1/0]
     via 2001:DB8:FFFF::1
C   2001:DB8:ACAD:10::/64 [0/0]
     via GigabitEthernet0/0/0, directly connected
L   2001:DB8:ACAD:10::1/128 [0/0]
     via GigabitEthernet0/0/0, receive
O   2001:DB8:ACAD:40::/64 [110/2]
     via FE80::2, GigabitEthernet0/0/1
C   2001:DB8:FFFF::/64 [0/0]
     via GigabitEthernet0/0/2, directly connected
L   2001:DB8:FFFF::2/128 [0/0]
     via GigabitEthernet0/0/2, receive
L   FF00::/8 [0/0]
     via Null0, receive
```

**Differences to notice:** local routes are /128; OSPFv3 and many routing protocols use the neighbor's **link-local** address (FE80::2) as the next hop, so the exit interface is always shown with it; `FF00::/8` via Null0 is the router's internal multicast entry; `::/0` is the default route; `ND` marks a default learned from a Router Advertisement.

### 10.9 Special cases you must recognise

| What you see | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `S 10.50.0.0/16 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet0/0/1` | A static route with only an **exit interface** (no next-hop IP). Fine on point-to-point links; on Ethernet the router must ARP for every destination (Chapter 11) |
| `S 10.60.0.0/16 [1/0] via 10.0.0.2` with no interface | A next-hop-only static: the router performs a **recursive lookup** for 10.0.0.2 to find the exit interface |
| Two or more `via` lines under one prefix | Equal-cost load sharing (ECMP) |
| `O*E2 0.0.0.0/0` | A default route advertised into OSPF (external type 2) |
| `D EX`, `O E2`, `O IA` | External EIGRP, external OSPF, OSPF inter-area routes |
| `[110/2]` on a static route line | Impossible: static routes show `[1/0]` unless their AD was changed (for example `[130/0]` for a floating static) |
| `Gateway of last resort is not set` | No default route; unmatched destinations are dropped |
| A /32 `L` route | The router's own interface address; packets to it are received, not forwarded |

### 10.10 Worked lookups on R1's table (10.3)

For each destination, find every route that contains it and take the longest.

| Destination | Containing routes | Longest | Next hop / exit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 10.10.40.25 | /0, 10.10.0.0/16, 10.10.40.0/24 | 10.10.40.0/24 (O) | 10.0.0.2, G0/0/0 |
| 10.10.77.9 | /0, 10.10.0.0/16 | 10.10.0.0/16 (S) | 10.0.0.2, G0/0/0 (recursive via 10.0.0.0/30) |
| 10.0.0.6 | /0, 10.0.0.4/30 | 10.0.0.4/30 (O) | 10.0.0.2, G0/0/0 |
| 10.0.0.2 | /0, 10.0.0.0/30 | 10.0.0.0/30 (C) | Directly connected, G0/0/0 (ARP for 10.0.0.2) |
| 10.200.1.1 | /0 only | 0.0.0.0/0 (S\*) | 198.51.100.1, G0/0/1 — the "10.0.0.0/8 is variably subnetted" line is a header, not a route |
| 8.8.8.8 | /0 only | 0.0.0.0/0 (S\*) | 198.51.100.1, G0/0/1 |
| 198.51.100.2 | /0, 198.51.100.0/30, 198.51.100.2/32 | 198.51.100.2/32 (L) | Received by R1 itself |

**Exam habit:** write the destination in the same octet style as each candidate and check the octets covered by the mask. Do not stop at the first match you see; scan the whole table for a longer one.

### 10.11 Packet Tracer Lab 10.1 — Reading the campus routing tables (Intermediate)

**Objective:** 3.1. **Start from:** your Lab 9.1 file (topology in Figure 9.5; routed links from Figures 5.6 and 6.4). No new devices. **You will:** predict, read and explain the routing tables of R1, DSW1 and DSW2, find next hops for given destinations, and prove a longest-match decision.

**Part 1 — Predict before you look.** Using the configurations from Labs 5.1 and 6.1, write down every route you expect on each router. Then compare with the outputs below (your timers and order may differ slightly).

```
DSW1# show ip route
Gateway of last resort is 10.0.0.1 to network 0.0.0.0

S*    0.0.0.0/0 [1/0] via 10.0.0.1
      10.0.0.0/8 is variably subnetted, 13 subnets, 3 masks
C        10.0.0.0/30 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet1/0/24
L        10.0.0.2/32 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet1/0/24
C        10.0.0.4/30 is directly connected, Port-channel3
L        10.0.0.5/32 is directly connected, Port-channel3
C        10.10.10.0/24 is directly connected, Vlan10
L        10.10.10.1/32 is directly connected, Vlan10
C        10.10.30.0/24 is directly connected, Vlan30
L        10.10.30.1/32 is directly connected, Vlan30
C        10.10.40.0/24 is directly connected, Vlan40
L        10.10.40.1/32 is directly connected, Vlan40
C        10.10.99.0/24 is directly connected, Vlan99
L        10.10.99.1/32 is directly connected, Vlan99
S        10.255.0.2/32 [1/0] via 10.0.0.6

R1# show ip route
Gateway of last resort is not set

      10.0.0.0/8 is variably subnetted, 3 subnets, 3 masks
C        10.0.0.0/30 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
L        10.0.0.1/32 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
S        10.10.0.0/16 [1/0] via 10.0.0.2
      203.0.113.0/32 is subnetted, 1 subnets
C        203.0.113.1 is directly connected, Loopback0

DSW2# show ip route
Gateway of last resort is 10.0.0.5 to network 0.0.0.0

S*    0.0.0.0/0 [1/0] via 10.0.0.5
      10.0.0.0/8 is variably subnetted, 3 subnets, 2 masks
C        10.0.0.4/30 is directly connected, Port-channel3
L        10.0.0.6/32 is directly connected, Port-channel3
C        10.255.0.2/32 is directly connected, Loopback0
```

**Part 2 — Questions on the outputs**

1. How many routes on DSW1 are connected, local and static? Why does every SVI produce both a C and an L route?
2. Which DSW1 route would a packet to 10.255.0.2 use, and what is its AD and metric?
3. **Ping 10.255.0.2 from R1.** It fails. Use R1's table to explain why, even though PC1 can reach it. (Hint: does 10.10.0.0/16 contain 10.255.0.2? Is there a default?)
4. Add a fix on R1 without touching any other device, and verify with `show ip route` and a ping. (One answer: `ip route 10.255.0.0 255.255.0.0 10.0.0.2`; another: a default route to 10.0.0.2.)
5. On DSW2, which route does a packet to 10.10.40.11 use? Why does the response come back successfully?

**Part 3 — Prove longest match.** On R1 add `ip route 10.10.40.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.0.2`. `show ip route` now lists both S 10.10.0.0/16 and S 10.10.40.0/24. Run `show ip route 10.10.40.11` and `show ip route 10.10.30.11`: each command prints which entry is used. Remove the /24 afterwards.

**Part 4 — Watch TTL in Simulation mode.** Switch Packet Tracer to Simulation, send a ping from PC1 to 203.0.113.1, and open the packet at each hop: the TTL drops by one at DSW1 (an SVI hop) and is received by R1. The Ethernet source and destination MACs change at DSW1; the IP addresses do not.

**Answers (hide until done):** (1) Six connected, six local (one pair per routed interface, Po3 and SVI), one static and one static default; the C route is the subnet, the L route is the router's own /32 on it. (2) S 10.255.0.2/32, AD 1, metric 0, next hop 10.0.0.6. (3) R1 has no route containing 10.255.0.2 and no gateway of last resort, so it drops the packet; PC1 works because DSW1 has the static /32. (5) DSW2 uses its default route 0.0.0.0/0 via 10.0.0.5 (DSW1), which has a connected route to VLAN 40.

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] Predicted tables written before viewing the real ones
- [ ] All Part 2 questions answered and R1's missing route fixed
- [ ] `show ip route <address>` used to prove a longest-match decision
- [ ] TTL and MAC changes observed in Simulation mode

### 10.12 Chapter 10 summary notes (exam revision)

- **Per packet:** check frame → decrement TTL (0 = drop, ICMP Time Exceeded) → longest-prefix lookup → no route and no default = drop, ICMP Unreachable → ARP/ND for next hop → new frame.
- **Route line:** code, prefix/length, **\[AD/metric\]**, via next hop, age, exit interface. C and L lines have no brackets; L = the router's own /32 (or /128).
- **Codes:** C, L, S, S\* (static default), O, O IA, O E1/E2, D, D EX, R, B. "is variably subnetted" lines are headers, not routes.
- **AD:** connected 0, static 1, eBGP 20, EIGRP 90, OSPF 110, IS-IS 115, RIP 120, EIGRP external 170, iBGP 200, DHCP default 254, 255 never used.
- **Order of rules:** AD (same prefix, different sources) and metric (same prefix, same protocol) decide what is **installed**; **longest prefix match** decides what each packet **uses**. AD never compares different prefixes.
- **Equal metrics** → several `via` lines (ECMP).
- **Default route** 0.0.0.0/0 or ::/0; shown as the gateway of last resort; "not set" = no default.
- **IPv6:** two-line entries, /128 local routes, link-local next hops with an exit interface.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "The static route wins because its AD is lower" | Only if it is the **same prefix**; otherwise the longer prefix wins |
| "10.0.0.0/8 is variably subnetted means there is a /8 route" | It is a header grouping subnets, not a route |
| "The first matching line is used" | The longest matching line is used, wherever it is in the table |
| "Metric 30 from OSPF is worse than metric 2 from RIP" | Metrics from different protocols cannot be compared; AD decides |
| "\[1/0\] means metric 1" | 1 is the AD, 0 the metric |

### 10.13 Practice questions — Chapter 10

Questions 1–5 use this table from router RX:

```
Gateway of last resort is 192.0.2.1 to network 0.0.0.0

S*    0.0.0.0/0 [1/0] via 192.0.2.1
      172.16.0.0/16 is variably subnetted, 4 subnets, 3 masks
O        172.16.0.0/20 [110/20] via 10.1.1.2, 00:05:12, GigabitEthernet0/1
O        172.16.4.0/22 [110/30] via 10.1.1.6, 00:05:12, GigabitEthernet0/2
S        172.16.5.0/24 [1/0] via 10.1.1.10
C        172.16.8.0/24 is directly connected, GigabitEthernet0/3
```

**1.** What is the next hop for a packet to 172.16.5.9?

**2.** What is the next hop for 172.16.6.200?

**3.** What is the next hop for 172.16.12.1?

**4.** What is the next hop for 172.16.20.1?

**5.** How does RX deliver a packet to 172.16.8.77?

**6. (Recall)** In `[110/30]`, which number is the administrative distance and which is the metric?

**7. (AD)** OSPF and RIP both offer 10.50.0.0/16. Which route is installed?

A. RIP, because hop count is simpler  B. OSPF, because AD 110 is lower than 120  C. Both, load-shared  D. Whichever was learned first

**8. (AD)** EIGRP (internal) and OSPF both offer 10.60.0.0/24. Which is installed and why?

**9. (Output interpretation)** What does `Gateway of last resort is not set` mean for a packet to 8.8.8.8 if no other route matches?

**10. (Output interpretation)** A prefix shows two `via` lines with identical `[110/3]`. What does that mean?

**11. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which statements about administrative distance are true?

A. Lower AD is preferred  B. AD compares routes to different prefixes  C. A route with AD 255 is never installed  D. AD is the same as the metric  E. Connected routes have AD 1

**12. (IPv6)** An IPv6 route reads `O 2001:DB8:ACAD:40::/64 [110/2] via FE80::2, GigabitEthernet0/0/1`. Why is the exit interface always shown with this next hop?

**13. (Concept)** OSPF installs 10.70.0.0/24. An administrator adds `ip route 10.70.0.0 255.255.255.0 10.9.9.9 130`. Which route is in the table now?

**14. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** Looking at RX's table, a network assistant says: *"Traffic to 172.16.5.9 uses the OSPF route 172.16.4.0/22 because dynamic routes are preferred over static ones."* Is that correct?

### 10.14 Answer key with explanations

1. **10.1.1.10.** 172.16.5.9 is contained by /20 (0–15), /22 (4–7) and /24 (5); the static /24 is longest. Its next hop 10.1.1.10 is then resolved recursively.
2. **10.1.1.6 via Gi0/2.** The third octet 6 falls in 172.16.4.0/22 (4–7) and in /20; it is not in 5.0/24. The /22 is longest.
3. **10.1.1.2 via Gi0/1.** 12 is inside 172.16.0.0/20 (third octets 0–15) but not in the /22 or /24 blocks.
4. **192.0.2.1 (default route).** 20 is outside 0–15, so no 172.16 route contains it. The 172.16.0.0/16 header is not a route.
5. **Directly** out Gi0/3: 172.16.8.0/24 (C) is longer than the /20 that also contains it. RX ARPs for 172.16.8.77 itself.
6. **110 is the AD, 30 is the metric** (OSPF cost).
7. **B.** Same prefix, different sources: lower AD wins. Metrics of different protocols are never compared.
8. **EIGRP (AD 90)** beats OSPF (AD 110) for the same prefix.
9. There is **no default route**, so the packet is dropped and an ICMP Destination Unreachable is returned.
10. **Equal-cost multipath**: two paths with the same metric are both installed, and traffic is shared between the next hops.
11. **A and C.** AD compares sources offering the same prefix (B is wrong); it is not the metric (D); connected routes have AD 0 (E).
12. Link-local addresses are valid only on one link, and every link uses FE80::/10, so FE80::2 is ambiguous without naming the interface it is on.
13. **The OSPF route stays installed** (AD 110 < 130). The static route is a **floating static**: it appears only if OSPF loses 10.70.0.0/24.
14. **No.** Longest prefix match comes first: the static 172.16.5.0/24 is more specific than the /22, so it is used. (Static routes also have a lower AD than OSPF, but AD is irrelevant here because the prefixes differ.)

## Chapter 11 — IPv4 and IPv6 static routing

**Objective covered:** **3.2 Troubleshoot IPv4 and IPv6 static routing** — **3.2.a** default route, **3.2.b** network route, **3.2.c** host route, **3.2.d** floating static. The verb is *troubleshoot*: you must configure all four types and also find what is wrong with someone else's.

### 11.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Decide when static routes are the right tool.
2. Configure IPv4 static routes with a next hop, an exit interface, or both, and explain recursive lookup.
3. Configure and recognise default, network, host and floating static routes.
4. Configure IPv6 static routes, including link-local next hops.
5. Verify static routes and troubleshoot missing, wrong, one-way and never-failing routes.

### 11.2 When static routes make sense

A **static route** is a route you type in by hand. It never changes unless you change it, uses no bandwidth or CPU to maintain, and reveals nothing to neighbors.

| Use static routes for | Avoid static routes when |
| --- | --- |
| A **stub** network with one way out (a branch, a home router) | Many routers and paths: every change must be made by hand everywhere |
| A **default route** toward the ISP | Paths must change automatically after a failure far away |
| A **backup** path behind a dynamic protocol (floating static) | The network grows often |
| Forcing one specific destination along a chosen path (host route) | You cannot document and maintain them reliably |

**The four types in objective 3.2** all use the same command; they differ only in the destination and the AD (Figure 11.1):

| Type | Destination | Example | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Default route** (3.2.a) | 0.0.0.0/0 or ::/0 | `ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 198.51.100.1` | Anything not otherwise known |
| **Network route** (3.2.b) | A subnet or summary | `ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.12.2` | A whole remote network |
| **Host route** (3.2.c) | One address: /32 or /128 | `ip route 10.20.20.10 255.255.255.255 10.0.21.2` | One server, a loopback, a special path for one device |
| **Floating static** (3.2.d) | Any prefix, with a **higher AD** | `ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.21.2 200` | Backup that appears only when the preferred route disappears |

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch11/fig-11-01-static-route-types.svg" alt="Figure 11.1 — Default, network, host and floating static routes on R1 (the Lab 11.1 network)" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 11.1</b> — Default, network, host and floating static routes on R1 (the Lab 11.1 network)</sub></p>

Figure 11.1 places all four route types from the table on one network, which you build in Lab 11.1. Each blue arrow is one of R1's static routes, pointing toward the destination it describes; the legend gives the exact route.

### 11.3 IPv4 static route syntax

```
ip route <network> <mask> { <next-hop-ip> | <exit-interface> | <exit-interface> <next-hop-ip> } [<AD>] [name <text>] [permanent]
```

| Parameter | Meaning | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `network` | The destination network (all host bits 0) | Typing a host address with a subnet mask: IOS rejects it with `%Inconsistent address and mask` |
| `mask` | Dotted-decimal mask (not `/24`) | Wrong mask covers the wrong range |
| `next-hop-ip` | The neighbor's address on a directly connected subnet | Using the router's own address, or a far-away address that has no route |
| `exit-interface` | The local interface to send out of | Using it alone on Ethernet (see below) |
| `AD` | 1–255; default 1 | Making a backup lower than the route it should back up |
| `name` | A label shown in the configuration | — |
| `permanent` | Keeps the route even if the exit interface goes down | Black-holes traffic during an outage; rarely wanted |

All static routes are entered in **global configuration mode** and appear in the running configuration as `ip route ...` lines.

**Three ways to say where to go (Figure 11.2):**

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch11/fig-11-02-next-hop-vs-exit-interface.svg" alt="Figure 11.2 — Next-hop, exit-interface and fully specified static routes compared" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 11.2</b> — Next-hop, exit-interface and fully specified static routes compared</sub></p>

Each row of Figure 11.2 shows the command, the routing table line it produces, and what R1 has to ARP for. The dashed red arrow marks the form to avoid on Ethernet; the highlighted row is the safest form.

1. **Next hop only** — `ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.12.2`. The router must perform a **recursive lookup**: it looks up 10.0.12.2 in its own table, finds `C 10.0.12.0/30 ... GigabitEthernet0/0/1`, and uses that interface. Shown as `S 10.20.20.0/24 [1/0] via 10.0.12.2`.
2. **Exit interface only** — `ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 GigabitEthernet0/0/1`. Shown as *directly connected*. On a point-to-point serial link this is fine. On **Ethernet** the router treats every destination as if it were on the wire and ARPs for each one; it works only if the neighbor answers with **proxy ARP**, and it fills the ARP cache. Avoid it on Ethernet.
3. **Fully specified** — `ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 GigabitEthernet0/0/1 10.0.12.2`. No recursive lookup, and ARP only for the next hop. Recommended on Ethernet, and **required** in IPv6 when the next hop is a link-local address.

**When is a static route installed?** Only when its next hop is reachable (recursion succeeds) or its exit interface is up. If the exit interface goes down, the route is removed (unless `permanent`). This is what makes a floating static take over (11.5).

### 11.4 Default route (3.2.a)

```
R1(config)# ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 198.51.100.1          ! HQ edge: everything unknown to the ISP
R2(config)# ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 10.0.12.1             ! branch: everything to HQ
```

The route appears as `S* 0.0.0.0/0 [1/0] via ...` and sets the **gateway of last resort**. A stub site such as R2 often needs **only** a default route: every destination it does not own is "somewhere toward HQ".

### 11.5 Network route (3.2.b)

A network route reaches one remote subnet, or a **summary** of several:

```
R1(config)# ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.12.2    ! branch LAN
ISP(config)# ip route 10.0.0.0 255.0.0.0 198.51.100.2      ! one summary for all of the company
```

Every network route needs a **matching route back** on the far side. A ping fails if only one direction exists: the echo request arrives but the reply has no route home. That one-way mistake is the most common static-routing fault.

### 11.6 Host route (3.2.c)

A host route has a /32 mask (IPv4) or /128 (IPv6) and so beats any other route to that address by longest match:

```
R1(config)# ip route 10.20.20.10 255.255.255.255 10.0.21.2 ! a branch server
```

Typical uses: reaching a router's **loopback** for management or as a routing protocol ID, sending one server's traffic over a different path (Figure 11.1, route c), or pointing at a single DNS or syslog server. Remember that connected interfaces already create /32 **L** routes for the router's own addresses; host routes you type are **S** routes.

### 11.7 Floating static route (3.2.d)

A **floating static** is a static route configured with an AD **higher than** the route it backs up. Because the preferred route has a lower AD, the floating route stays out of the routing table — it "floats" above it — until the preferred route disappears (Figure 11.3).

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch11/fig-11-03-floating-static-failover.svg" alt="Figure 11.3 — Floating static failover on R1: before and after the primary link fails" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 11.3</b> — Floating static failover on R1: before and after the primary link fails</sub></p>

In Figure 11.3 the blue arrow is the route R1 is actually using. On the left the AD 1 route wins and the AD 200 route exists only in the configuration. On the right the primary interface is down, its route is removed, and the floating route is installed with \[200/0\].

```
R1(config)# ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.12.2          ! primary, AD 1
R1(config)# ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.21.2 200      ! backup, AD 200
R2(config)# ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 10.0.12.1                   ! primary default
R2(config)# ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 10.0.21.1 200               ! backup default
```

**Choosing the AD:**

| Backing up | Floating AD must be higher than | Typical choice |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Another static route | 1 | 5–254 |
| An OSPF-learned route | 110 | 111–254 (often 120–200) |
| An EIGRP-learned route | 90 | 91–254 |

If the floating route's AD is **equal** to the primary's, both are installed and traffic is load-shared; if it is **lower**, the "backup" becomes the primary. Both are classic exam faults.

**What a floating static can and cannot detect.** It takes over when the preferred route is **removed**: the primary static's exit interface went down (link failure detected locally), or the dynamic protocol withdrew the route. A static route **cannot** detect a failure further away — for example the ISP's router behind a working link — so it keeps sending traffic into a dead path. Detecting remote failures needs tracking (IP SLA) or a routing protocol, which are beyond objective 3.2.

**Viewing a floating route.** It does not appear in `show ip route` while unused. Check the configuration (`show running-config | include ip route`) and, after a failure, look for its AD in brackets: `S 10.20.20.0/24 [200/0] via 10.0.21.2`.

### 11.8 IPv6 static routes

IPv6 static routes follow the same ideas with three differences: the prefix is written with a slash, IPv6 routing must be enabled, and link-local next hops need an interface.

```
ipv6 route <prefix>/<length> { <next-hop> | <exit-interface> | <exit-interface> <next-hop> } [<AD>]
```

**The four types on R1 and R2 (Lab 11.1 addressing):**

```
R1(config)# ipv6 unicast-routing                                             ! required for any IPv6 routing
R1(config)# ipv6 route ::/0 2001:db8:ffff::1                                 ! (a) default to the ISP
R1(config)# ipv6 route 2001:db8:acad:20::/64 2001:db8:acad:f012::2          ! (b) network route, primary
R1(config)# ipv6 route 2001:db8:acad:20::10/128 2001:db8:acad:f021::2       ! (c) host route
R1(config)# ipv6 route 2001:db8:acad:20::/64 2001:db8:acad:f021::2 200      ! (d) floating static

R2(config)# ipv6 unicast-routing
R2(config)# ipv6 route ::/0 GigabitEthernet0/0/1 FE80::1                     ! default via R1's link-local
R2(config)# ipv6 route ::/0 GigabitEthernet0/0/2 FE80::1 200                 ! floating default
```

| Next-hop form | Example | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Global unicast next hop | `ipv6 route 2001:db8:acad:20::/64 2001:db8:acad:f012::2` | Recursive lookup, as in IPv4 |
| Exit interface only | `ipv6 route 2001:db8:acad:20::/64 GigabitEthernet0/0/1` | Works on point-to-point links; on Ethernet relies on the neighbor answering ND for every destination |
| Link-local next hop | `ipv6 route ::/0 GigabitEthernet0/0/1 FE80::1` | **Interface is mandatory** — every link uses FE80::/10. Without it IOS replies `% Interface has to be specified for a link-local nexthop` |

Using the neighbor's link-local address is common because it does not change if global addressing is renumbered; that is why Lab 3.1 set `ipv6 address fe80::1 link-local` on R1.

```
R1# show ipv6 route static
S   ::/0 [1/0]
     via 2001:DB8:FFFF::1
S   2001:DB8:ACAD:20::/64 [1/0]
     via 2001:DB8:ACAD:F012::2
S   2001:DB8:ACAD:20::10/128 [1/0]
     via 2001:DB8:ACAD:F021::2
```

The floating route is again invisible until the primary route is removed.

### 11.9 Verifying static routes

| Command | Use it to check |
| --- | --- |
| `show ip route static` / `show ipv6 route static` | Which static routes are **installed** |
| `show ip route 10.20.20.10` | Which entry a specific destination uses, with AD, metric and next hop |
| `show running-config \| include ip route` | Every configured static route, including floating ones not installed |
| `show ip interface brief` | Whether exit interfaces and next-hop subnets are up |
| `ping 10.20.20.1 source 10.10.10.1` | End-to-end reachability **from the LAN address**, which tests the return route too |
| `traceroute 10.20.20.10` | Which path (primary or backup) packets actually take |

**Why `source` matters.** A plain `ping` from R1 uses the exit interface's address (10.0.12.1). That can succeed even when the branch has no route back to the HQ LAN. Pinging from the LAN address (or from a PC) proves both directions. This is an **extended ping**, covered fully in Chapter 15.

### 11.10 Troubleshooting static routes

Work through these checks in order:

1. **Is the route configured correctly?** Right network address, right mask, right next hop (`show running-config | include ip route`).
2. **Is it installed?** If not, the next hop is unreachable or the exit interface is down, or a lower-AD route to the same prefix already exists.
3. **Is it the route actually used?** A longer prefix elsewhere (a host route, a more specific network) may win (`show ip route <destination>`).
4. **Is there a route back?** Check the return path on every router, from the far end toward the source.
5. **Does the path work end to end?** `ping ... source`, `traceroute`.

| Symptom | Likely cause | Evidence | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Route missing from the table | Next hop not on a connected subnet, or exit interface down | `show ip route static` empty for it; next hop absent from C routes | Correct the next hop; bring the interface up |
| IOS rejects the command | Host bits set in the network (`%Inconsistent address and mask`) | Error at config time | Use the network address (10.20.20.0, not 10.20.20.1) |
| Ping from the router works, from the LAN fails | No return route on the far router | `ping ... source <LAN IP>` fails; far router lacks the route | Add the return route |
| Traffic takes the backup link all the time | Floating AD equal to or lower than the primary, or primary next hop wrong | Both routes or only the backup installed | Fix the AD (backup higher) or the primary next hop |
| Load sharing that was not intended | Primary and backup configured with the same AD | Two `via` lines | Raise the backup AD |
| Backup never takes over during an ISP outage | Primary static stays installed because the local link is still up | Primary route still present; traffic black-holed | Expected static behaviour; needs tracking or a routing protocol |
| One server goes a different way from its subnet | A host route (/32) overrides the network route | `show ip route 10.20.20.10` shows the /32 | Intended (Figure 11.1, route c) or remove it |
| Exit-interface static on Ethernet works inconsistently | Relies on proxy ARP; ARP table full of remote hosts | `show ip arp` lists many remote addresses on one interface | Use a next hop or fully specified route |
| IPv6 route rejected | Link-local next hop without an interface | `% Interface has to be specified for a link-local nexthop` | Add the exit interface |
| IPv6 static routes ignored | `ipv6 unicast-routing` missing | No forwarding; no RAs | Enable it |

**Objectives:** 3.2.a–d (IPv4 and IPv6). **New file.** **Devices:** 3 × ISR4331 (R1 HQ, R2 Branch, ISP), connected with copper **crossover** cables (router to router). LANs are simulated with loopback interfaces so the lab needs no switches; R2's Lo20 address (10.20.20.1) stands in for the branch server of Figure 11.1.
