| Device | Interface | IPv4 | IPv6 | Link-local |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| R1 | G0/0/0 (to ISP) | 198.51.100.2/30 | 2001:db8:ffff::2/64 | auto |
| R1 | G0/0/1 (primary to R2) | 10.0.12.1/30 | 2001:db8:acad:f012::1/64 | fe80::1 |
| R1 | G0/0/2 (backup to R2) | 10.0.21.1/30 | 2001:db8:acad:f021::1/64 | fe80::1 |
| R1 | Loopback10 (HQ LAN) | 10.10.10.1/24 | 2001:db8:acad:10::1/64 | — |
| R2 | G0/0/1 | 10.0.12.2/30 | 2001:db8:acad:f012::2/64 | fe80::2 |
| R2 | G0/0/2 | 10.0.21.2/30 | 2001:db8:acad:f021::2/64 | fe80::2 |
| R2 | Loopback20 (Branch LAN) | 10.20.20.1/24 | 2001:db8:acad:20::1/64 | — |
| ISP | G0/0/0 | 198.51.100.1/30 | 2001:db8:ffff::1/64 | auto |
| ISP | Loopback0 (an Internet host) | 203.0.113.8/32 | 2001:db8:9999::8/128 | — |

**R1**

```
enable
configure terminal
hostname R1
ipv6 unicast-routing
interface gigabitethernet0/0/0
 ip address 198.51.100.2 255.255.255.252
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:ffff::2/64
 no shutdown
interface gigabitethernet0/0/1
 description Primary link to R2
 ip address 10.0.12.1 255.255.255.252
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:acad:f012::1/64
 ipv6 address fe80::1 link-local
 no shutdown
interface gigabitethernet0/0/2
 description Backup link to R2
 ip address 10.0.21.1 255.255.255.252
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:acad:f021::1/64
 ipv6 address fe80::1 link-local
 no shutdown
interface loopback10
 ip address 10.10.10.1 255.255.255.0
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:acad:10::1/64
exit
! (a) default
ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 198.51.100.1
ipv6 route ::/0 2001:db8:ffff::1
! (b) network route via the primary link
ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.12.2
ipv6 route 2001:db8:acad:20::/64 2001:db8:acad:f012::2
! (c) host route via the backup link
ip route 10.20.20.1 255.255.255.255 10.0.21.2
ipv6 route 2001:db8:acad:20::1/128 2001:db8:acad:f021::2
! (d) floating static via the backup link
ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.21.2 200
ipv6 route 2001:db8:acad:20::/64 2001:db8:acad:f021::2 200
end
copy running-config startup-config
```

**R2**

```
enable
configure terminal
hostname R2
ipv6 unicast-routing
interface gigabitethernet0/0/1
 ip address 10.0.12.2 255.255.255.252
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:acad:f012::2/64
 ipv6 address fe80::2 link-local
 no shutdown
interface gigabitethernet0/0/2
 ip address 10.0.21.2 255.255.255.252
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:acad:f021::2/64
 ipv6 address fe80::2 link-local
 no shutdown
interface loopback20
 ip address 10.20.20.1 255.255.255.0
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:acad:20::1/64
exit
ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 10.0.12.1
ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 10.0.21.1 200
ipv6 route ::/0 GigabitEthernet0/0/1 FE80::1
ipv6 route ::/0 GigabitEthernet0/0/2 FE80::1 200
end
copy running-config startup-config
```

**ISP** — addresses from the table, plus return routes for the whole company (in real life NAT would hide private addresses; Chapter 21):

```
ip route 10.0.0.0 255.0.0.0 198.51.100.2
ipv6 unicast-routing
ipv6 route 2001:db8:acad::/48 2001:db8:ffff::2
```

**Verification and expected results**

| Test | Command (device) | Expected |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Installed routes | `show ip route static` (R1) | S\* 0.0.0.0/0, S 10.20.20.0/24 \[1/0\] via 10.0.12.2, S 10.20.20.1/32 via 10.0.21.2 — the floating route absent |
| Host route wins | `show ip route 10.20.20.1` vs `show ip route 10.20.20.99` (R1) | /32 via 10.0.21.2 vs /24 via 10.0.12.2 |
| Both directions | `ping 10.10.10.1 source 10.20.20.1` (R2) | Success |
| Internet via defaults | `ping 203.0.113.8 source 10.20.20.1` (R2) | Success: R2 default → R1 default → ISP, return via the ISP's 10.0.0.0/8 |
| IPv6 | `ping 2001:db8:9999::8 source 2001:db8:acad:20::1` (R2) | Success |
| Failover | `shutdown` R1 G0/0/1, then `show ip route static` on R1 and R2 | R1: S 10.20.20.0/24 **\[200/0\]** via 10.0.21.2; R2: S\* 0.0.0.0/0 **\[200/0\]** via 10.0.21.1; pings still succeed |
| Recovery | `no shutdown` R1 G0/0/1 | AD 1 routes return; floating routes disappear |

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] All four route types configured for IPv4 and IPv6
- [ ] Host route proven with `show ip route <address>`
- [ ] Pings succeed from LAN to LAN and LAN to Internet using `source`
- [ ] Failover and recovery observed on both routers

### 11.12 Lab 11.2 — Static routing challenge

Apply to a copy of Lab 11.1 and diagnose with show commands, pings with `source`, and traceroute only.

```
! R1
no ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.12.2
ip route 10.20.20.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.12.3
! R2
no ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 10.0.21.1 200
ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 10.0.21.1
! ISP
no ip route 10.0.0.0 255.0.0.0 198.51.100.2
! R2
no ipv6 unicast-routing
```

**Answer key (hide until done)**

| Fault | Symptom | Evidence | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| R1 next hop 10.0.12.3 does not exist | HQ cannot reach the branch LAN (except 10.20.20.1, which uses the host route) | Route installed (10.0.12.3 is in a connected subnet) but `show ip arp` has no entry for it | Correct next hop 10.0.12.2 |
| R2 backup default at AD 1 | Traffic load-shared over both links | Two `via` lines under S\* 0.0.0.0/0 | Restore AD 200 |
| ISP has no return route | LAN-to-Internet pings with `source` fail; R1's own ping to the ISP works | ISP `show ip route` lacks 10.0.0.0/8 | Add the return route |
| R2 IPv6 routing disabled | All IPv6 beyond R2's links fails | `show running-config` lacks `ipv6 unicast-routing` | Re-enable it |

### 11.13 Chapter 11 summary notes (exam revision)

- **IPv4:** `ip route <network> <mask> {next-hop | exit-int | exit-int next-hop} [AD]`. **IPv6:** `ipv6 route <prefix/len> ... [AD]` with `ipv6 unicast-routing` enabled.
- **Next hop only** = recursive lookup. **Exit interface only** = "directly connected"; on Ethernet relies on proxy ARP — avoid. **Fully specified** = best on Ethernet; **mandatory for IPv6 link-local next hops**.
- **Default** 0.0.0.0/0 or ::/0 (S\*, gateway of last resort). **Network** route to a subnet or summary. **Host** route /32 or /128 wins by longest match. **Floating** static = higher AD than the route it backs up; installed only when that route is removed.
- Floating AD must exceed: static 1, EIGRP 90, OSPF 110. Equal AD = load sharing; lower = it becomes primary.
- A static route is installed only if its next hop is reachable / exit interface up; it **cannot detect remote failures**.
- **Every route needs a return route.** Test with `ping <dest> source <LAN-IP>` and `traceroute`.
- Network address must have host bits 0, or IOS says `%Inconsistent address and mask`.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "A floating static shows in `show ip route` all the time" | Only after the preferred route disappears; otherwise check the configuration |
| "Backup route AD 1 makes failover faster" | AD 1 equals the primary: both are installed and traffic is load-shared |
| "A ping from the router proves the route works" | It may use the router's link address; test from the LAN address |
| "A static route fails over when the ISP fails" | Only if the local next hop or interface fails |
| "IPv6 link-local next hops work like global ones" | They need the exit interface specified |

### 11.14 Practice questions — Chapter 11

**1. (Configuration)** Write the IPv4 default route that sends everything to 203.0.113.1.

**2. (Configuration)** Write a host route to 192.168.5.20 through next hop 10.1.1.2.

**3. (Floating static)** OSPF already provides a route to 10.40.0.0/16. Which AD makes a static route to the same prefix a floating backup?

A. 1  B. 100  C. 110  D. 115

**4. (Troubleshooting)** `ip route 10.30.0.0 255.255.0.0 10.5.5.5` is configured, but the route never appears in `show ip route`. R1 has no route to 10.5.5.0. Why?

**5. (Troubleshooting)** IOS rejects `ip route 172.16.10.5 255.255.255.0 10.1.1.2` with `%Inconsistent address and mask`. Why, and what is the correct command?

**6. (Troubleshooting)** From R1, `ping 10.20.20.1` succeeds, but `ping 10.20.20.1 source 10.10.10.1` fails. What is the most likely cause?

**7. (Output interpretation)** R1 shows `S 10.20.20.0/24 [200/0] via 10.0.21.2`. What does this tell you about the network right now?

**8. (Output interpretation)** R2's default route shows two lines: `[1/0] via 10.0.12.1` and `[1/0] via 10.0.21.1`. The second link was meant to be a backup. What went wrong?

**9. (IPv6)** Why does IOS reject `ipv6 route ::/0 FE80::1`, and how do you fix it?

**10. (Longest match)** R1 has `S 10.20.20.0/24 via 10.0.12.2` and `S 10.20.20.1/32 via 10.0.21.2`. Which path do packets to 10.20.20.1 take, and to 10.20.20.50?

**11. (Multiple response — choose two)** What are disadvantages of an exit-interface-only static route on an Ethernet link?

A. It relies on the neighbor answering proxy ARP  B. It requires a recursive lookup  C. The ARP cache fills with remote destinations  D. It cannot be used for a default route  E. It has a higher AD

**12. (Concept)** R1's primary static default points to the ISP's router across a working link. The ISP's upstream connection fails. A floating default via a second ISP does not take over. Why?

**13. (Configuration)** Write the IPv6 default route on R2 that uses R1's link-local address FE80::1 on G0/0/1.

**14. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A network assistant suggests: *"Configure the backup static route with AD 1 so it takes over instantly when the primary fails."* Is this good advice?

### 11.15 Answer key with explanations

1. `ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 203.0.113.1`.
2. `ip route 192.168.5.20 255.255.255.255 10.1.1.2`.
3. **D.** Any AD above 110 makes it float behind OSPF. A and B would replace the OSPF route; C equals it.
4. The next hop 10.5.5.5 is **not reachable** (no connected or other route to it), so the recursive lookup fails and the route is not installed.
5. The "network" 172.16.10.5 has host bits set for a /24 mask. Use the network address: `ip route 172.16.10.0 255.255.255.0 10.1.1.2`.
6. The far end has **no return route** to 10.10.10.0/24. The plain ping used R1's link address, which R2 can reach as a connected network.
7. The **floating static is active**: the preferred AD 1 route to 10.20.20.0/24 has been removed (for example the primary link is down), so traffic uses the backup next hop 10.0.21.2.
8. The backup was configured **without a higher AD** (both are AD 1), so both are installed and traffic is load-shared. Reconfigure the backup with AD 200.
9. A link-local next hop is ambiguous without an interface (every link uses FE80::/10). Use `ipv6 route ::/0 GigabitEthernet0/0/1 FE80::1`.
10. 10.20.20.1 matches the **/32** → via 10.0.21.2 (backup link). 10.20.20.50 matches only the **/24** → via 10.0.12.2 (primary).
11. **A and C.** No recursive lookup is needed (B is false); it can be used for defaults (D); its AD is still 1 (E).
12. The primary static route is still installed because its local next hop is still reachable; static routes cannot see failures beyond the next hop. Tracking (IP SLA) or a routing protocol is needed.
13. `ipv6 route ::/0 GigabitEthernet0/0/1 FE80::1`.
14. **No.** With AD 1 the backup equals the primary, so both are used all the time and traffic is split across both links. A backup needs a **higher** AD; it takes over as soon as the primary route is removed.

### 11.17 Chapter 11 progress record

**Objectives completed:** **3.2** and **3.2.a–d** for IPv4 and IPv6: default, network, host and floating static routes, syntax forms, verification and troubleshooting.

## Chapter 12 — Single-area OSPFv2 and OSPFv3

**Objective covered:** **3.3 Configure single-area OSPFv2 for IPv4 and OSPFv3 for IPv6** — **3.3.a** neighbor adjacencies (excluding authentication), **3.3.b** point-to-point, **3.3.c** broadcast (DR/BDR selection), **3.3.d** router ID. OSPF is the only dynamic routing protocol named in v2.0, so expect configuration, `show` output and troubleshooting questions.

### 12.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Explain how a link-state protocol builds its routing table and why OSPF uses areas.
2. Describe OSPF packets, neighbor states and the conditions for an adjacency.
3. Predict the router ID and the DR/BDR on a broadcast network.
4. Configure OSPFv2 with `network` statements or per interface, passive interfaces, cost, priority, network type and a default route.
5. Configure OSPFv3 for IPv6.
6. Read `show ip ospf neighbor`, `show ip ospf interface` and `show ip route ospf`, and troubleshoot adjacencies that do not form.

### 12.2 Introduction: why a routing protocol

Static routes do not scale and cannot react to failures beyond the next hop (Chapter 11). A **dynamic routing protocol** lets routers tell each other which networks they can reach and recalculate automatically when something changes. **OSPF (Open Shortest Path First)** is an open-standard **link-state** protocol: every router learns the full map of its area, then each runs the **SPF (Dijkstra)** algorithm to compute the best (lowest-cost) path to every network.

| Property | OSPF |
| --- | --- |
| Type | Link-state, interior gateway protocol |
| Versions | OSPFv2 for IPv4; OSPFv3 for IPv6 |
| Administrative distance | 110 |
| Metric | **Cost** = reference bandwidth ÷ interface bandwidth |
| Transport | IP protocol **89** |
| Multicast | **224.0.0.5** all OSPF routers, **224.0.0.6** DR/BDR (IPv6: FF02::5, FF02::6) |
| Hello / dead timers | 10 s / 40 s on broadcast and point-to-point networks |
| Areas | Area 0 is the backbone; **v2.0 tests single-area OSPF (everything in area 0)** |

### 12.3 How OSPF works internally

1. **Discover neighbors** with Hello packets on every OSPF-enabled interface.
2. **Form adjacencies** and exchange the link-state database (LSDB).
3. Each router floods **LSAs** (link-state advertisements) describing its links; all routers in the area end up with an **identical LSDB**.
4. Each router runs **SPF** with itself as the root and installs the best paths as `O` routes.
5. When a link changes, only the affected LSA is flooded and SPF reruns — fast convergence without periodic full updates.

**The five OSPF packet types:**

| Type | Name | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Hello | Discover and keep neighbors; carry the parameters that must match |
| 2 | DBD (Database Description) | Summary list of the LSAs in the sender's database |
| 3 | LSR (Link-State Request) | Ask for LSAs you are missing |
| 4 | LSU (Link-State Update) | Carry full LSAs |
| 5 | LSAck | Acknowledge LSUs |

### 12.4 Neighbor states and adjacencies (3.3.a)

Figure 12.1 shows the states two routers pass through.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch12/fig-12-01-ospf-neighbor-states.svg" alt="Figure 12.1 — OSPF neighbor states and the packets that move two routers from Down to Full" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 12.1</b> — OSPF neighbor states and the packets that move two routers from Down to Full</sub></p>

Read Figure 12.1 top to bottom: the arrows are packets, the left column is the state they cause. A pair reaches **FULL** only after both databases match.

| State | Meaning | Stuck here usually means |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Down | No Hellos received | Link down, OSPF not enabled on the interface, passive interface, ACL blocking 224.0.0.5 |
| Init | Hello received, but my RID is not in it | One-way communication; Hellos not reaching the other side |
| 2-Way | Each sees itself in the other's Hello | **Normal** between two DROthers; otherwise DR/BDR election issue |
| ExStart / Exchange | Negotiating and exchanging DBDs | **MTU mismatch** |
| Loading | Requesting missing LSAs | Corrupt or lost LSUs (rare) |
| Full | Synchronized | Healthy |

**What must match for two routers to become neighbors** (the Hello packet carries these):

1. **Area ID** (both interfaces in area 0).
2. **Subnet and mask** on the shared link.
3. **Hello and dead intervals**.
4. **Authentication** settings (excluded from the objective, but they must match).
5. **Stub area flag** (not relevant in a single backbone area).
6. **Unique router IDs** — two routers with the same RID will not form a proper adjacency.
7. **MTU** — a mismatch lets them reach ExStart/Exchange but not Full.
8. The interface must **not be passive**.

### 12.5 Router ID (3.3.d)

Every OSPF router needs a 32-bit **router ID (RID)**, written like an IPv4 address. It is chosen once, when the OSPF process starts, in this order:

1. The `router-id` command (best practice: set it).
2. Otherwise the **highest IPv4 address on an up loopback** interface.
3. Otherwise the **highest IPv4 address on an up physical** interface.

Changing the RID later requires `clear ip ospf process` (or a reload) to take effect. OSPFv3 uses the same 32-bit RID; on a router with no IPv4 addresses you **must** configure `router-id` manually.

### 12.6 Network types: broadcast and point-to-point (3.3.b, 3.3.c)

| Network type | Default on | DR/BDR? | Neighbors reach FULL with |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Broadcast** | Ethernet | Yes | The DR and the BDR only |
| **Point-to-point** | Serial (HDLC/PPP); configurable on Ethernet | No | The single neighbor |

On a link with only two routers (a /30 or /31 between them) a DR is pointless. Setting `ip ospf network point-to-point` on both ends skips the election and the wait, so the adjacency forms faster. Both ends must use the same network type; mismatched types can form an adjacency yet produce missing routes.

### 12.7 DR and BDR election (3.3.c)

On a broadcast network every router would otherwise need a full adjacency with every other router — n(n−1)/2 of them. Instead one **Designated Router (DR)** and a **Backup DR (BDR)** are elected, and everyone else (**DROther**) forms FULL adjacencies only with those two (Figure 12.2).

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch12/fig-12-02-dr-bdr-election.svg" alt="Figure 12.2 — DR/BDR election on a broadcast LAN: FULL and 2-WAY adjacencies" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 12.2</b> — DR/BDR election on a broadcast LAN: FULL and 2-WAY adjacencies</sub></p>

In Figure 12.2 the blue lines are FULL adjacencies; the dashed line between the two DROthers is 2-WAY, which is correct and expected.

**Election rules:**

1. Highest **interface OSPF priority** wins (0–255, default **1**; `ip ospf priority <n>`).
2. Tie: highest **router ID**.
3. Priority **0** = never DR or BDR.
4. **No preemption**: a better router that joins later does not take over until the DR fails or the OSPF process is reset on the segment. When the DR fails, the BDR becomes DR and a new BDR is elected.

The election happens per segment (per interface), so a router can be DR on one LAN and DROther on another.

### 12.8 OSPF cost and the metric

**Cost = reference bandwidth ÷ interface bandwidth**, with a default reference of **100 Mb/s**. Costs below 1 are rounded up to 1, so FastEthernet, Gigabit and 10-Gigabit links all cost **1** by default — OSPF cannot tell them apart. Fix it on **every** router with the same value:

```
R1(config-router)# auto-cost reference-bandwidth 10000      ! in Mb/s: now 10G = 1, 1G = 10, 100M = 100
```

You can also set a cost directly on an interface: `ip ospf cost 20`. The route's metric is the sum of the outgoing interface costs along the path, from the router to the destination.

### 12.9 Configuring OSPFv2

**Method 1 — `network` statements** in router configuration mode. Each statement uses a **wildcard mask** (the inverse of a subnet mask: 0 = must match, 1 = ignore) to select interfaces; matching interfaces join the area.

```
R1(config)# router ospf 1                                  ! process ID: local only, need not match
R1(config-router)# router-id 1.1.1.1
R1(config-router)# auto-cost reference-bandwidth 10000
R1(config-router)# network 10.0.123.0 0.0.0.255 area 0     ! the LAN
R1(config-router)# network 10.0.12.0 0.0.0.3 area 0        ! the /30 point-to-point link
R1(config-router)# network 10.10.10.0 0.0.0.255 area 0     ! the loopback LAN
R1(config-router)# passive-interface loopback10            ! advertise it, but send no Hellos
R1(config-router)# default-information originate always    ! advertise a default route into OSPF
```

**Method 2 — per interface** (clearer, and the only method in OSPFv3):

```
R1(config)# interface gigabitethernet0/0/0
R1(config-if)# ip ospf 1 area 0
```

| Command | Mode | Purpose / notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `router ospf <pid>` | Global | Starts OSPF process; the process ID is locally significant |
| `router-id <a.b.c.d>` | Router | Sets the RID; needs `clear ip ospf process` if OSPF already runs |
| `network <addr> <wildcard> area <id>` | Router | Enables OSPF on matching interfaces. `network 10.0.123.1 0.0.0.0 area 0` matches exactly one interface |
| `ip ospf <pid> area <id>` | Interface | Enables OSPF on this interface |
| `passive-interface <int>` / `passive-interface default` | Router | No Hellos (no neighbors) on that interface, but its network is still advertised. Use on LAN and loopback interfaces with no routers |
| `ip ospf network point-to-point` | Interface | Point-to-point type: no DR/BDR (3.3.b). On a loopback it advertises the real mask instead of /32 |
| `ip ospf priority <0-255>` | Interface | DR election priority (3.3.c) |
| `ip ospf cost <n>` | Interface | Override the computed cost |
| `ip ospf hello-interval` / `dead-interval` | Interface | Timers; must match neighbors |
| `auto-cost reference-bandwidth <Mb/s>` | Router | Same value on every router |
| `default-information originate [always]` | Router | Advertise a default route; `always` even if this router has no default itself |

**Loopbacks and /32.** OSPF advertises a loopback as a /32 host route regardless of its mask. To advertise Lo10 as 10.10.10.0/24 (as if it were a LAN), add `ip ospf network point-to-point` on the loopback.

### 12.10 Configuring OSPFv3 for IPv6

OSPFv3 works the same way (same states, DR/BDR rules, cost and AD) but is enabled per interface, forms neighbors over **link-local** addresses, and needs IPv6 routing on:

```
R1(config)# ipv6 unicast-routing
R1(config)# ipv6 router ospf 1
R1(config-rtr)# router-id 1.1.1.1                         ! mandatory if the router has no IPv4 address
R1(config-rtr)# auto-cost reference-bandwidth 10000
R1(config-rtr)# passive-interface loopback10
R1(config-rtr)# exit
R1(config)# interface gigabitethernet0/0/0
R1(config-if)# ipv6 ospf 1 area 0
R1(config-if)# interface gigabitethernet0/0/1
R1(config-if)# ipv6 ospf 1 area 0
R1(config-if)# ipv6 ospf network point-to-point
```

Newer IOS XE releases also offer the address-family syntax (`router ospfv3 1`, `address-family ipv6 unicast`, interface `ospfv3 1 ipv6 area 0`); both produce the same result. OSPFv2 and OSPFv3 are **separate processes** with separate neighbor tables and separate DR elections.

### 12.11 Verification

```
R1# show ip ospf neighbor
Neighbor ID     Pri   State           Dead Time   Address         Interface
2.2.2.2           0   FULL/  -        00:00:36    10.0.12.2       GigabitEthernet0/0/1
2.2.2.2          50   FULL/BDR        00:00:33    10.0.123.2      GigabitEthernet0/0/0
3.3.3.3         100   FULL/DR         00:00:39    10.0.123.3      GigabitEthernet0/0/0
```

Read it as: R1 has **two** adjacencies with R2 (one per link) and one with R3. On the point-to-point link the role is `-` (no DR) and priority shows 0. On the LAN, R3 is DR and R2 BDR; R1 itself is DROther (shown in the next command).

```
R1# show ip ospf interface brief
Interface    PID   Area     IP Address/Mask    Cost  State Nbrs F/C
Lo10         1     0        10.10.10.1/24      1     P2P   0/0
Gi0/0/1      1     0        10.0.12.1/30       10    P2P   1/1
Gi0/0/0      1     0        10.0.123.1/24      10    DROTH 2/2

R1# show ip route ospf
O     10.20.20.0/24 [110/11] via 10.0.12.2, 00:05:10, GigabitEthernet0/0/1
                    [110/11] via 10.0.123.2, 00:05:10, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
O     10.30.30.0/24 [110/11] via 10.0.123.3, 00:05:10, GigabitEthernet0/0/0
```

The cost of 11 is 10 (1 Gb/s link with reference 10000) + 1 (the remote loopback). R1 has two equal-cost paths to R2's LAN, so both are installed.

| Command | Shows |
| --- | --- |
| `show ip ospf neighbor` | Neighbor RID, priority, state/role, dead timer, address, interface |
| `show ip ospf interface [int]` | Area, cost, network type, DR/BDR, Hello/dead timers, priority, neighbor count |
| `show ip ospf interface brief` | One line per OSPF interface |
| `show ip protocols` | RID, networks configured, passive interfaces, reference bandwidth, routing sources |
| `show ip ospf` | RID, SPF statistics, reference bandwidth |
| `show ip route ospf` | Installed O routes |
| `show ipv6 ospf neighbor`, `show ipv6 ospf interface brief`, `show ipv6 route ospf` | The OSPFv3 equivalents |

### 12.12 Troubleshooting OSPF

| Symptom | Likely cause | Evidence / fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| No neighbor at all | Interface not in OSPF (wrong `network` wildcard), passive interface, area mismatch, subnet mismatch, ACL blocking 224.0.0.5 | `show ip ospf interface brief` (interface missing?), `show ip protocols` (passive?) |
| Neighbor never appears; timers differ | Hello/dead mismatch | `show ip ospf interface` on both ends; match them |
| Stuck in ExStart/Exchange | MTU mismatch | Match `ip mtu` on both ends |
| `%OSPF-4-DUP_RTRID_NBR` | Two routers with the same RID | Set unique `router-id`; `clear ip ospf process` |
| Two routers show only 2-WAY | Both are DROthers on a broadcast LAN | Normal |
| Unexpected DR | Elections are not preemptive; priorities changed after the election | `clear ip ospf process` on the segment after setting priorities |
| Routes for a loopback show as /32 | Loopback network type | `ip ospf network point-to-point` on the loopback |
| Slow path chosen over a fast one | Reference bandwidth left at 100 (all fast links cost 1) or different on routers | Same `auto-cost reference-bandwidth` everywhere |
| Adjacency up, but routes odd on a two-router link | Network type mismatch (one end broadcast, one point-to-point) | Make both ends the same |
| No default route learned | `default-information originate` missing (or no default without `always`) | Add it on the edge router |
| OSPFv3 will not start | No router ID on an IPv6-only router, or `ipv6 unicast-routing` missing | Configure both |

**Order of checks:** interface up and addressed → interface in OSPF and not passive → Hello parameters match (area, subnet, timers) → unique RID → MTU → routes and costs.

### 12.13 Packet Tracer Lab 12.1 — Single-area OSPFv2 and OSPFv3 (Intermediate–Advanced)

**Objectives:** 3.3.a–d. **New file.** **Devices:** 3 × ISR4331 (R1, R2, R3), 1 × 2960 (SW-LAN). Each router's G0/0/0 connects to SW-LAN (straight-through); R1 G0/0/1 connects to R2 G0/0/1 (crossover).

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch12/fig-12-03-lab-12-1-topology.svg" alt="Figure 12.3 — Lab 12.1 topology: broadcast LAN with DR/BDR and a point-to-point link" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 12.3</b> — Lab 12.1 topology: broadcast LAN with DR/BDR and a point-to-point link</sub></p>

Figure 12.3 shows the two network types side by side: the LAN needs a DR election, the R1–R2 link does not. Loopbacks stand in for each router's user LAN.

| Router | G0/0/0 (LAN) | G0/0/1 (p2p) | Loopback LAN | RID / LAN priority |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| R1 | 10.0.123.1/24, 2001:db8:acad:123::1/64 | 10.0.12.1/30, 2001:db8:acad:12::1/64 | Lo10 10.10.10.1/24, 2001:db8:acad:10::1/64 | 1.1.1.1 / 1 |
| R2 | 10.0.123.2/24, 2001:db8:acad:123::2/64 | 10.0.12.2/30, 2001:db8:acad:12::2/64 | Lo20 10.20.20.1/24, 2001:db8:acad:20::1/64 | 2.2.2.2 / 50 |
| R3 | 10.0.123.3/24, 2001:db8:acad:123::3/64 | — | Lo30 10.30.30.1/24, 2001:db8:acad:30::1/64 | 3.3.3.3 / 100 |

**R1**

```
enable
configure terminal
hostname R1
ipv6 unicast-routing
interface gigabitethernet0/0/0
 ip address 10.0.123.1 255.255.255.0
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:acad:123::1/64
 ipv6 ospf 1 area 0
 no shutdown
interface gigabitethernet0/0/1
 ip address 10.0.12.1 255.255.255.252
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:acad:12::1/64
 ip ospf network point-to-point
 ipv6 ospf network point-to-point
 ipv6 ospf 1 area 0
 no shutdown
interface loopback10
 ip address 10.10.10.1 255.255.255.0
 ipv6 address 2001:db8:acad:10::1/64
 ip ospf network point-to-point
 ipv6 ospf network point-to-point
 ipv6 ospf 1 area 0
exit
router ospf 1
 router-id 1.1.1.1
 auto-cost reference-bandwidth 10000
 network 10.0.123.0 0.0.0.255 area 0
 network 10.0.12.0 0.0.0.3 area 0
 network 10.10.10.0 0.0.0.255 area 0
 passive-interface loopback10
 default-information originate always
exit
ipv6 router ospf 1
 router-id 1.1.1.1
 auto-cost reference-bandwidth 10000
 passive-interface loopback10
end
copy running-config startup-config
```

**R2** — the same pattern with its own addresses, RID 2.2.2.2, Lo20, **no** `default-information originate`, and on G0/0/0:

```
interface gigabitethernet0/0/0
 ip ospf priority 50
 ipv6 ospf priority 50
```

**R3** — RID 3.3.3.3, only G0/0/0 and Lo30, and `ip ospf priority 100` / `ipv6 ospf priority 100` on G0/0/0.

**Because the DR election is not preemptive**, the router that boots first may become DR. After all three are configured, run `clear ip ospf process` and `clear ipv6 ospf process` (answer `yes`) on all three routers so the priorities decide.

**Verification and expected results**

| Check | Command (device) | Expected |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Neighbors | `show ip ospf neighbor` (R1) | R2 FULL/- on G0/0/1; R2 FULL/BDR and R3 FULL/DR on G0/0/0 |
| Interface roles | `show ip ospf interface brief` (R1, R2, R3) | R1 DROTH, R2 BDR, R3 DR on the LAN; P2P on R1/R2 G0/0/1 |
| RIDs and passive | `show ip protocols` (R1) | Router ID 1.1.1.1; Lo10 passive; three networks |
| Routes | `show ip route ospf` (R3) | O 10.10.10.0/24 and 10.20.20.0/24 \[110/11\]; O 10.0.12.0/30 \[110/20\] via both R1 and R2; **O\*E2 0.0.0.0/0** via 10.0.123.1 |
| IPv6 neighbors | `show ipv6 ospf neighbor` (R1) | Same roles; neighbors identified by RID, reached over link-local addresses |
| IPv6 routes | `show ipv6 route ospf` (R3) | O 2001:DB8:ACAD:10::/64 and :20::/64 via FE80:: next hops |
| End to end | `ping 10.10.10.1 source 10.30.30.1` and `ping 2001:db8:acad:10::1 source 2001:db8:acad:30::1` (R3) | Success |

**Experiments**

1. **Timer mismatch:** on R3 G0/0/0 enter `ip ospf hello-interval 5`. Within 40 s R1 and R2 drop R3 (dead timers no longer match). Remove the command.
2. **Point-to-point failover:** shut R1 G0/0/1. R1's route to 10.20.20.0/24 drops to a single path via the LAN. Restore it.
3. **Non-preemption:** set `ip ospf priority 255` on R1 G0/0/0. Nothing changes until you clear the OSPF process on all three routers; then R1 becomes DR. Set it back and clear again.
4. **Passive mistake:** on R2 enter `router ospf 1` then `passive-interface gigabitethernet0/0/0`. R2 loses both LAN neighbors but still advertises 10.0.123.0/24. Remove it.

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] OSPFv2 and OSPFv3 neighbors FULL with the expected roles
- [ ] R3 is DR and R2 BDR on both protocols
- [ ] Default route O\*E2 learned on R2 and R3
- [ ] Loopback LANs advertised as /24 and /64, not /32 and /128
- [ ] All four experiments reproduced and explained

### 12.14 Lab 12.2 — OSPF challenge

On a copy of Lab 12.1 apply: on R2 `router ospf 1` → `no network 10.0.123.0 0.0.0.255 area 0` → `network 10.0.123.0 0.0.0.255 area 1`; on R3 `router ospf 1` → `router-id 1.1.1.1` and `clear ip ospf process`; on R1 `router ospf 1` → `passive-interface gigabitethernet0/0/1`; on R2 `router ospf 1` → `no auto-cost reference-bandwidth 10000`. Diagnose with show commands and logs only.

**Answer key (hide until done):** (1) **Area mismatch** on R2's LAN interface: no LAN adjacencies for R2; `show ip ospf interface` shows area 1. (2) **Duplicate router ID** between R1 and R3: `%OSPF-4-DUP_RTRID_NBR` messages, unstable adjacencies. (3) **Passive** point-to-point interface: the R1–R2 link loses its adjacency, though 10.0.12.0/30 is still advertised. (4) **Reference bandwidth mismatch**: adjacencies stay up, but R2 calculates all gigabit links as cost 1 while the others use 10, so paths and metrics differ between routers.

### 12.15 Chapter 12 summary notes (exam revision)

- OSPF: link-state, AD 110, metric cost = reference bandwidth (default 100 Mb/s) ÷ interface bandwidth; protocol 89; 224.0.0.5 / 224.0.0.6 (FF02::5 / FF02::6).
- Packets: Hello, DBD, LSR, LSU, LSAck. States: Down, Init, 2-Way, ExStart, Exchange, Loading, Full.
- Neighbors need matching area, subnet/mask, Hello/dead, authentication, stub flag, MTU (for Full); unique RIDs; non-passive interfaces.
- **RID:** `router-id` → highest loopback IP → highest active interface IP; change needs `clear ip ospf process`.
- **Broadcast:** DR/BDR by highest priority (default 1, 0 = never), then highest RID, **non-preemptive**; DROthers FULL with DR/BDR, 2-WAY with each other. **Point-to-point:** no DR.
- Config: `router ospf 1`, `network ... wildcard area 0` or `ip ospf 1 area 0`, `passive-interface`, `auto-cost reference-bandwidth`, `default-information originate`. OSPFv3: `ipv6 router ospf 1`, `router-id`, interface `ipv6 ospf 1 area 0`.
- Verify: `show ip ospf neighbor`, `show ip ospf interface [brief]`, `show ip protocols`, `show ip route ospf` (and `ipv6` versions).

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "Process IDs must match between neighbors" | They are local; areas must match |
| "2-WAY means the adjacency failed" | Between DROthers it is the correct final state |
| "Raising a priority immediately makes that router DR" | No preemption; reset the process |
| "Passive interfaces are not advertised" | They are advertised; they just send no Hellos |
| "1 G and 10 G links have different OSPF costs by default" | Both cost 1 until the reference bandwidth is raised |

### 12.16 Practice questions — Chapter 12

**1. (RID)** A router has no `router-id` command, Loopback0 10.255.0.9, Loopback1 10.255.0.4, and G0/0/0 192.168.1.1. What is its RID?

**2. (DR election)** Four routers on a LAN: R1 priority 1 RID 4.4.4.4; R2 priority 1 RID 2.2.2.2; R3 priority 10 RID 1.1.1.1; R4 priority 0 RID 9.9.9.9. All start together. Which is DR and which BDR?

**3. (States)** Two routers are stuck in EXSTART. What is the most likely cause?

A. Area mismatch  B. MTU mismatch  C. Hello timer mismatch  D. Passive interface

**4. (Output interpretation)** `show ip ospf neighbor` on a DROther lists another DROther as `2WAY/DROTHER`. Is anything wrong?

**5. (Configuration)** Write one `network` statement that enables OSPF area 0 on only the interface with address 172.16.5.1.

**6. (Cost)** With the default reference bandwidth, what is the OSPF cost of a 1 Gb/s interface and of a 10 Gb/s interface? What problem does this cause?

**7. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which must match for two OSPF routers to become neighbors?

A. Process ID  B. Area ID  C. Hello and dead intervals  D. Router ID  E. Interface description

**8. (Network type)** What changes when you configure `ip ospf network point-to-point` on both ends of a /30 Ethernet link?

**9. (Passive)** What does `passive-interface gigabitethernet0/0/2` do to that interface?

**10. (Routing table)** R3 shows `O*E2 0.0.0.0/0 [110/1] via 10.0.123.1`. What configured it, and on which router?

**11. (OSPFv3)** An IPv6-only router refuses to start OSPFv3. What is missing?

**12. (Troubleshooting)** R2 sees R1 as a neighbor on the LAN but R3 does not, and `show ip ospf interface` on R3 shows Hello 5, Dead 20. What is wrong?

**13. (Loopbacks)** Other routers see `10.10.10.1/32` instead of `10.10.10.0/24` for R1's Loopback10. Why, and how is it fixed?

**14. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A network assistant reviews `show ip ospf neighbor` showing two neighbors in `2WAY/DROTHER` and one `FULL/DR` and recommends: *"Change all OSPF priorities to 1 so every router reaches FULL."* Is that right?

### 12.17 Answer key with explanations

1. **10.255.0.9** — the highest loopback address wins over any physical interface, even a numerically larger one.
2. **DR R3** (highest priority 10). **BDR R1** (priority 1 tie with R2, broken by higher RID 4.4.4.4 over 2.2.2.2). R4 (priority 0) can never be DR or BDR.
3. **B.** Mismatched MTU stops the DBD exchange. Area or Hello mismatches prevent the neighbor from appearing at all.
4. **No.** DROthers on a broadcast segment remain 2-WAY with each other and are FULL only with the DR and BDR.
5. `network 172.16.5.1 0.0.0.0 area 0`.
6. Both are **1** (100 ÷ 1000 and 100 ÷ 10000 round up to 1), so OSPF cannot prefer the faster link. Fix with the same `auto-cost reference-bandwidth` on all routers.
7. **B and C.** Process IDs are local; RIDs must be **different**, not the same; descriptions are irrelevant.
8. No DR/BDR election takes place, the adjacency forms faster, and `show ip ospf neighbor` shows `FULL/ -` instead of DR/BDR roles.
9. It stops sending and processing Hellos there (no neighbors can form), but the interface's network is **still advertised** into OSPF.
10. `default-information originate` (here with `always`) on **R1**, the router at 10.0.123.1. It appears as an external type 2 route with metric 1.
11. A manual **`router-id`** (no IPv4 address exists to choose one from), and `ipv6 unicast-routing` must be enabled.
12. **Hello/dead timer mismatch**: R3 uses 5/20 while R1 uses the default 10/40, so R3 never becomes a neighbor of R1 (or of R2, which also uses the defaults). Make the timers identical on every router on the LAN.
13. OSPF advertises loopbacks as **/32 host routes** by default. Add `ip ospf network point-to-point` on Loopback10 so the configured /24 is advertised.
14. **No.** 2-WAY between DROthers is normal and efficient; forcing everyone to the same priority changes nothing about that (a DR and BDR are still elected) and could move the DR to a weaker router. No change is needed.

## Chapter 13 — First hop redundancy protocols

**Objective covered:** **3.4 Interpret the operational status of First Hop Redundancy Protocols (HSRP and VRRP)** — the exam asks you to read and explain HSRP and VRRP status output (13.8). Configuration is included because building a group once makes that output easy to read.

### 13.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Explain why hosts need a redundant default gateway and what an FHRP provides.
2. Describe virtual IP and virtual MAC addresses, active/standby (HSRP) and master/backup (VRRP) roles.
3. Explain priority, preemption, timers, tracking and failover.
4. Compare HSRP, VRRP and GLBP.
5. Configure and verify HSRP on a pair of Layer 3 switches.

### 13.2 The problem: one gateway per host

A host has **one** default gateway address. In Lab 5.1 every PC in VLAN 10 uses DSW1's SVI, 10.10.10.1. If DSW1 fails, the hosts keep sending off-subnet traffic to 10.10.10.1 and nothing answers — even though DSW2 could route for them. Changing the gateway on every host is not practical, and hosts do not run routing protocols.

A **first hop redundancy protocol (FHRP)** makes two or more routers share a **virtual IP address** and a **virtual MAC address**. Hosts use the virtual IP as their gateway. One router actively forwards for it; another stands by and takes over the same addresses if the first fails (Figure 13.1).

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch13/fig-13-01-fhrp-group.svg" alt="Figure 13.1 — A first hop redundancy group: one virtual gateway shared by an active and a standby router" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 13.1</b> — A first hop redundancy group: one virtual gateway shared by an active and a standby router</sub></p>

In Figure 13.1 the dashed box is the virtual router that hosts believe they are talking to. The highlighted DSW1 owns it while active; the Hellos let DSW2 notice when DSW1 stops.

### 13.3 How an FHRP works

1. Routers in the same **group** exchange Hello (advertisement) messages on the shared subnet.
2. The router with the **highest priority** becomes active (HSRP) or master (VRRP); a tie is broken by the highest real IP address.
3. The active router answers ARP requests for the virtual IP with the **virtual MAC**, and forwards frames sent to that MAC.
4. The standby router listens. If Hellos stop for the **hold time**, it becomes active and sends a **gratuitous ARP** so switches relearn where the virtual MAC now lives.
5. Hosts notice nothing: the gateway's IP and MAC have not changed.

**Preemption.** If a higher-priority router comes back after a failure, should it take over again? With **preempt** enabled it does; without it, the current active router keeps the role. HSRP preemption is **off by default**; VRRP preemption is **on by default**.

**Tracking.** A router can lower its own priority when something else fails — for example its uplink toward the core. If DSW1 tracks its uplink and decrements its priority from 110 to 90, DSW2 (100) takes over (with preempt), avoiding a gateway that has no way out.

### 13.4 Failover timing

Figure 13.2 shows a failure with HSRP's default timers.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch13/fig-13-02-hsrp-failover-timeline.svg" alt="Figure 13.2 — HSRP failover timeline with default timers" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 13.2</b> — HSRP failover timeline with default timers</sub></p>

In Figure 13.2 hosts lose their gateway from the moment DSW1 fails until DSW2's hold timer expires. Faster timers shrink that gap, at the cost of more Hello traffic.

### 13.5 HSRP, VRRP and GLBP compared

| Feature | HSRP | VRRP | GLBP |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Standard | Cisco proprietary | Open standard (RFC 5798, VRRPv3) | Cisco proprietary |
| Roles | **Active** / **Standby** (others Listen) | **Master** / **Backup** | AVG (gateway) and up to 4 AVFs (forwarders) |
| Load sharing | Per group (different VLANs, different active routers) | Per group | Within one group, across up to 4 routers |
| Virtual MAC | v1: 0000.0c07.acXX; v2: 0000.0c9f.fXXX | 0000.5e00.01XX | 0007.b400.XXYY |
| Multicast / transport | v1 224.0.0.2, v2 224.0.0.102; UDP 1985 | 224.0.0.18; IP protocol 112 | 224.0.0.102; UDP 3222 |
| Default timers | Hello 3 s, hold 10 s | Advertisement 1 s, down ≈ 3 s | Hello 3 s, hold 10 s |
| Default priority | 100 (higher wins) | 100; **255 for the address owner** | 100 |
| Preemption default | **Off** | **On** | Off |
| Virtual IP may equal a real interface IP | No | **Yes** (that router is the owner) | No |
| IPv6 | HSRPv2 | VRRPv3 | Yes |

**HSRP versions.** Version 1 supports groups 0–255 and IPv4 only; version 2 supports groups 0–4095, IPv6 and millisecond timers. The two versions are not compatible, so use the same version on all group members. Matching the group number to the VLAN ID (group 10 on VLAN 10) is a common, readable convention.

**HSRP states:** Initial → Learn → Listen → Speak → Standby or Active. Seeing routers stuck in Speak or both Active usually means they cannot hear each other's Hellos.

### 13.6 Configuring HSRP

Objective 3.4 asks you to *interpret HSRP and VRRP statu*s, so configuration is not strictly required; configuring HSRP once makes every concept concrete. All commands are in **interface configuration mode** on the SVI (or routed interface) that faces the hosts. Each router keeps its own **real** IP; the virtual IP is the one hosts use.

```
DSW1(config)# interface vlan 10
DSW1(config-if)# ip address 10.10.10.2 255.255.255.0      ! real address; .1 becomes the virtual IP
DSW1(config-if)# standby version 2
DSW1(config-if)# standby 10 ip 10.10.10.1                  ! group 10, virtual IP
DSW1(config-if)# standby 10 priority 110                   ! default 100; higher wins
DSW1(config-if)# standby 10 preempt                        ! take the role back after recovering

DSW2(config)# interface vlan 10
DSW2(config-if)# ip address 10.10.10.3 255.255.255.0
DSW2(config-if)# standby version 2
DSW2(config-if)# standby 10 ip 10.10.10.1
DSW2(config-if)# standby 10 preempt                        ! priority stays 100
```

| Command | Purpose | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `standby version 2` | HSRPv2 (groups 0–4095, IPv6, ms timers) | Same version on every member |
| `standby <grp> ip <virtual-ip>` | Join group, set the virtual IP | Same group number and virtual IP on all members; the virtual IP is in the subnet but on no interface |
| `standby <grp> priority <0-255>` | Election priority | Default 100 |
| `standby <grp> preempt` | Reclaim the Active role when priority is higher | Off by default |
| `standby <grp> timers <hello> <hold>` | Faster failover, e.g. `timers 1 3` | Match on all members (the Active router's timers are learned) |
| `track 1 interface <int> line-protocol` + `standby <grp> track 1 decrement 20` | Lower priority when the uplink fails | Global `track` object, then reference it in the group; needs `preempt` on the peer to matter |
| `standby <grp> ipv6 autoconfig` | IPv6 virtual gateway (link-local, learned from the virtual MAC) | HSRPv2 only; use a different group number from the IPv4 group on the same interface |

**Load sharing across VLANs.** HSRP elects one Active router per group, so make DSW1 Active for VLANs 10 and 30 and DSW2 Active for VLANs 40 and 99. Align each VLAN's HSRP Active router with its **STP root** (Chapter 9) so frames reach the gateway without crossing an extra switch.

### 13.7 Configuring VRRP (for comparison)

VRRP is configured the same way in principle; the syntax depends on the IOS release.

```
! Classic IOS (VRRPv2)
DSW1(config-if)# vrrp 10 ip 10.10.10.1
DSW1(config-if)# vrrp 10 priority 110                       ! preempt is already on

! IOS XE (VRRPv3)
DSW1(config)# fhrp version vrrp v3
DSW1(config)# interface vlan 10
DSW1(config-if)# vrrp 10 address-family ipv4
DSW1(config-if-vrrp)# address 10.10.10.1 primary
DSW1(config-if-vrrp)# priority 110
```

VRRP may use a router's **own** interface address as the virtual IP; that router is the **address owner**, gets priority 255 automatically and is always Master while it is up. **Packet Tracer limitation:** VRRP is not available in most Packet Tracer versions (check with `vrrp ?` in interface mode). If yours lacks it, practise VRRP on paper using this section and do Lab 13.1 with HSRP.

### 13.8 Verifying HSRP

```
DSW1# show standby brief
                     P indicates configured to preempt.
                     |
Interface   Grp  Pri P State   Active          Standby         Virtual IP
Vl10        10   110 P Active  local           10.10.10.3      10.10.10.1
Vl30        30   110 P Active  local           10.10.30.3      10.10.30.1
Vl40        40   100 P Standby 10.10.40.3      local           10.10.40.1
Vl99        99   100 P Standby 10.10.99.3      local           10.10.99.1
```

Read each line: interface, group, this router's priority, **P** = preempt, this router's state, who is Active and Standby (`local` = me), and the virtual IP. DSW1 is Active for VLANs 10 and 30 and Standby for 40 and 99, exactly the planned split.

```
DSW1# show standby vlan 10
Vlan10 - Group 10 (version 2)
  State is Active
    2 state changes, last state change 00:04:11
  Virtual IP address is 10.10.10.1
  Active virtual MAC address is 0000.0c9f.f00a (MAC In Use)
  Hello time 3 sec, hold time 10 sec
  Preemption enabled
  Active router is local
  Standby router is 10.10.10.3, priority 100 (expires in 9.296 sec)
  Priority 110 (configured 110)
```

The virtual MAC **0000.0c9f.f00a** is HSRPv2's prefix plus the group number in hexadecimal (10 = 0x00A). On a PC, `arp -a` after pinging the gateway shows 10.10.10.1 mapped to that MAC; it does not change after a failover. (Output layout varies slightly by platform.)

**Interpreting VRRP status.** The same reading skills apply to VRRP; only the words differ (Master/Backup instead of Active/Standby). Example from a router running VRRPv3 on IOS XE (column layout varies by release):

```
DSW2# show vrrp brief
  Interface   Grp  A-F Pri  Time Own Pre State   Master addr/Group addr
  Vl10         10 IPv4 100     0  N   Y  BACKUP  10.10.10.2(r)/10.10.10.1
  Vl40         40 IPv4 110     0  N   Y  MASTER  10.10.40.3(r)/10.10.40.1
```

Read it as: in group 10 this router (priority 100) is **Backup** and the Master is 10.10.10.2; in group 40 it is **Master**. **Own N** means its own interface address is not the virtual IP (an owner would show Y and priority 255); **Pre Y** means preemption is on, VRRP's default. The virtual IP is the address after the slash.

| You see | It means |
| --- | --- |
| HSRP `Active` / VRRP `MASTER` | This router forwards for the virtual IP |
| HSRP `Standby` / VRRP `BACKUP` | Ready to take over (HSRP has exactly one Standby; VRRP may have several Backups) |
| HSRP `Listen` | A third router that is neither Active nor Standby |
| HSRP `Speak` or `Init` for a long time | Hellos not exchanged, or the interface is down |
| `P` (HSRP) / `Pre Y` (VRRP) | Preemption configured |
| Both routers Active / Master | They cannot hear each other: an error, not load sharing |

| Command | Shows |
| --- | --- |
| `show standby brief` | One line per group: state, priority, preempt, Active/Standby, virtual IP |
| `show standby [interface]` | Full detail: virtual MAC, timers, state changes, tracking |
| `show vrrp brief` / `show vrrp` | The VRRP equivalents |
| `show track` | Tracked objects and their state |
| PC: `ipconfig` and `arp -a` | Gateway is the virtual IP; gateway MAC is the virtual MAC |

### 13.9 Troubleshooting FHRPs

| Symptom | Likely cause | Evidence / fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Both routers Active for the same group | They cannot hear each other's Hellos: VLAN missing from the trunk between them, different group numbers, different HSRP versions, an ACL | `show standby brief` on both shows `local` as Active; fix the trunk, group or version |
| Wrong router stays Active after recovery | `preempt` missing on the higher-priority router | Add `standby <grp> preempt` |
| Wrong router Active from the start | Priorities as default (tie broken by highest real IP) | Set priorities deliberately |
| `%HSRP-4-DIFFVIP1` log, inconsistent gateway | Virtual IP typed differently on the members | Make the virtual IP identical |
| Failover works but hosts still lose access | Hosts' gateway set to a router's **real** IP | Point hosts (or the DHCP `default-router`) at the virtual IP |
| Gateway stays on a router whose uplink failed | No tracking | `track` the uplink and decrement the priority below the peer's |
| Traffic crosses an extra switch | HSRP Active router and STP root differ for that VLAN | Align them (Chapter 9) |
| Failover takes \~10 s | Default timers | `standby <grp> timers 1 3` on all members |

No new devices. **You will:** move DSW1's SVIs to real addresses .2, give DSW2 SVIs at .3, and make .1 the HSRP virtual gateway in every VLAN — so no host or access switch needs a change. DSW1 is Active for VLANs 10 and 30 and DSW2 for VLANs 40 and 99, matching the STP roots from Lab 9.1.

| VLAN | Virtual IP (hosts' gateway) | DSW1 real IP / priority | DSW2 real IP / priority | Active |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 10 | 10.10.10.1 | .2 / 110 | .3 / 100 | DSW1 |
| 30 | 10.10.30.1 | .2 / 110 | .3 / 100 | DSW1 |
| 40 | 10.10.40.1 | .2 / 100 | .3 / 110 | DSW2 |
| 99 | 10.10.99.1 | .2 / 100 | .3 / 110 | DSW2 |

**DSW1** (changing the SVI address briefly interrupts each VLAN; that is expected)

```
configure terminal
interface vlan 10
 ip address 10.10.10.2 255.255.255.0
 standby version 2
 standby 10 ip 10.10.10.1
 standby 10 priority 110
 standby 10 preempt
interface vlan 30

interface vlan 40

end
copy running-config startup-config
```

**DSW2** (it already has `ip routing`, the VLANs and a default route to DSW1 from Labs 6.1 and 9.1)

```
configure terminal
interface vlan 10
 ip address 10.10.10.3 255.255.255.0
 standby version 2
 standby 10 ip 10.10.10.1

interface vlan 30

end

```

**PT notes:** HSRP (`standby` commands) works on the 3650 and ISR4331 in current Packet Tracer. If `standby version 2` is rejected, leave it out (version 1 behaves the same for this lab, with virtual MAC 0000.0c07.acXX). Wait about 30 seconds after configuring for the elections to settle.

**Verification and expected results**

| Check | Command (device) | Expected |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Roles | `show standby brief` (DSW1, DSW2) | DSW1 Active for 10 and 30, Standby for 40 and 99; DSW2 the opposite; all virtual IPs .1 |
| Virtual MAC | PC1: `ping 10.10.10.1`, then `arp -a` | 10.10.10.1 → 0000.0c9f.f00a |
| Routing between VLANs | PC1 `ping 10.10.40.11` (Printer1) | Success: PC1's packets routed by DSW1, Printer1's replies by DSW2 |
| Management | SW1 `ping 10.10.99.1` | Success: the switches' default gateway is now the VLAN 99 virtual IP |
| Internet path | PC1 `ping 203.0.113.1` | Success via DSW1 and R1 |

**Experiments**

1. **Failover:** start a long ping from PC1 to Printer1 (`ping -t 10.10.40.11`), then on DSW1 `interface vlan 10` → `shutdown`. Expect a few lost replies (up to the 10 s hold time) and DSW2 becoming Active for VLAN 10. Run `arp -a` on PC1: the gateway MAC is unchanged.
2. **Preemption:** `no shutdown` the SVI. DSW1 (priority 110, preempt) takes VLAN 10 back. Repeat with `no standby 10 preempt` on DSW1: this time DSW2 stays Active.
3. **Faster timers:** add `standby 10 timers 1 3` on both switches and repeat experiment 1; fewer replies are lost.
4. **Tracking (optional; needs `track` support in your version):** on DSW1 `track 1 interface gigabitethernet1/0/24 line-protocol` and on VLAN 10 `standby 10 track 1 decrement 20`. Shut Gi1/0/24: DSW1's priority drops to 90 and DSW2 (100, preempt) takes over. In this lab R1 has a single link, so the Internet is unreachable either way; the point is to watch the priority change in `show standby`.

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] Hosts unchanged; every VLAN's gateway .1 is now a virtual IP
- [ ] DSW1 Active for 10/30, DSW2 Active for 40/99, matching the STP roots
- [ ] Failover and preemption reproduced and explained
- [ ] Virtual MAC seen in a PC's ARP cache
- [ ] File saved (Chapter 14 adds DHCP to this campus)

### 13.11 Lab 13.2 — FHRP challenge

On a copy of Lab 13.1 apply: (1) on DSW2 `interface vlan 30` → `standby 30 ip 10.10.30.254`; (2) on DSW2 `interface vlan 40` → `no standby version 2` (so it runs version 1); (3) on DSW1 `interface vlan 10` → `no standby 10 preempt`, then `shutdown` and `no shutdown` that SVI. Diagnose with `show standby brief`, logs and pings.

**Answer key (hide until done):** (1) **Virtual IP mismatch** — a `%HSRP-4-DIFFVIP1` message; hosts using 10.10.30.1 may lose their gateway if DSW2 becomes Active. (2) **Version mismatch** — the versions cannot hear each other, so **both** switches show Active for VLAN 40 (and both answer for the virtual IP with different MACs). (3) **No preemption** — after the bounce DSW2 stays Active for VLAN 10 although DSW1 has the higher priority, so VLAN 10 traffic no longer follows the STP root. Fix each and re-check.

### 13.12 Chapter 13 summary notes (exam revision)

- Hosts have **one** default gateway; an **FHRP** gives a group of routers a shared **virtual IP and virtual MAC** so the gateway survives a router failure without host changes.
- One router forwards (HSRP **Active**, VRRP **Master**); another waits (HSRP **Standby**, VRRP **Backup**). Highest priority wins (default 100), tie → highest real IP.
- **Preemption:** HSRP off by default, VRRP on. **Tracking** lowers priority when an uplink fails.
- Failover = Hellos stop for the hold time → standby takes over → gratuitous ARP updates switch MAC tables.
- **HSRP:** Cisco, Hello 3 s / hold 10 s, UDP 1985, v1 224.0.0.2 MAC 0000.0c07.acXX, v2 224.0.0.102 MAC 0000.0c9f.fXXX (IPv6 in v2). **VRRP:** open standard, 224.0.0.18, protocol 112, MAC 0000.5e00.01XX, virtual IP may be a real address (owner, priority 255). **GLBP:** Cisco, load balances within one group (AVG + AVFs).
- Load share by making different routers Active for different VLANs; align with the STP root.
- Verify: `show standby brief`, `show standby`, `show vrrp brief`; on hosts, gateway = virtual IP, `arp -a` shows the virtual MAC.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "Hosts are configured with both routers' addresses" | Hosts use only the virtual IP |
| "The standby router forwards half the traffic" | Within one HSRP group only the Active router forwards; share load with multiple groups |
| "A recovered higher-priority router always takes over" | Only with `preempt` (off by default in HSRP) |
| "The virtual MAC changes after a failover" | It stays the same; only the switch port that owns it changes |
| "HSRP and VRRP can be mixed in one group" | They are different protocols and never form one group |

### 13.13 Practice questions — Chapter 13

**1. (Concept)** What problem does a first hop redundancy protocol solve?

**2. (Recall)** Which FHRP is an open standard?

A. HSRP  B. GLBP  C. VRRP  D. CDP

**3. (Election)** In HSRP group 10, R1 has priority 100 and real IP 10.10.10.3; R2 has priority 100 and real IP 10.10.10.2. Neither preempts and both start together. Which is Active?

**4. (Preemption)** DSW1 (priority 110) fails, DSW2 (100) becomes Active, then DSW1 recovers. DSW1 has no `preempt`. Who is Active now?

**5. (Recall)** What is the HSRPv2 virtual MAC for group 30?

A. 0000.0c07.ac1e  B. 0000.0c9f.f01e  C. 0000.5e00.011e  D. 0000.0c9f.f030

**6. (Output interpretation)** `show standby brief` on DSW1 shows `Vl40  40  100 P Standby 10.10.40.3  local  10.10.40.1`. What do the fields tell you?

**7. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which statements about VRRP are true?

A. Preemption is enabled by default  B. It uses the Active/Standby terminology  C. The virtual IP can be a real interface address  D. It is Cisco proprietary  E. It uses UDP port 1985

**8. (Design)** How can two distribution switches both forward traffic using HSRP?

**9. (Troubleshooting)** Both switches show themselves as Active for VLAN 20. Give two likely causes.

**10. (Concept)** Why does the Standby router send a gratuitous ARP when it becomes Active?

**11. (Tracking)** DSW1 (priority 110, preempt) tracks its uplink with `decrement 20`; DSW2 has priority 100 and preempt. The uplink fails. What happens?

**12. (Troubleshooting)** After an FHRP failover some PCs still cannot reach other subnets. Their gateway is 10.10.10.2. What is wrong?

**13. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A network assistant suggests: *"Configure the same HSRP group on VLAN 10 with DSW1 using HSRP and DSW2 using VRRP so the network works with both vendors."* Is that workable?

### 13.14 Answer key with explanations

1. A host has a single default gateway. If that router fails, off-subnet traffic stops even though another router could forward it. An FHRP lets several routers share one virtual gateway so a backup takes over transparently.
2. **C.** HSRP and GLBP are Cisco proprietary; CDP is a discovery protocol.
3. **R1** — equal priority, so the higher real IP (10.10.10.3) wins.
4. **DSW2.** Without preempt, DSW1 does not take the role back even though its priority is higher.
5. **B.** HSRPv2 uses 0000.0c9f.fXXX with the group in hexadecimal: 30 = 0x01E. A is the version 1 format; C is VRRP's format; D writes 30 in decimal.
6. VLAN 40, group 40, DSW1's priority 100, preempt configured, DSW1 is **Standby**, the Active router is 10.10.40.3 (DSW2), DSW1 is the Standby itself (`local`), virtual IP 10.10.40.1.
7. **A and C.** VRRP uses Master/Backup terms, is an open standard and uses IP protocol 112 (UDP 1985 is HSRP).
8. Use **several groups**: DSW1 Active for some VLANs and DSW2 Active for others (Lab 13.1). Within one group only one router forwards.
9. The two cannot hear each other's Hellos: VLAN 20 missing on the link between them, different group numbers, different HSRP versions, or an ACL blocking the Hellos.
10. So that switches update their MAC address tables: the virtual MAC now lives behind a different port, and the gratuitous ARP teaches them the new location immediately.
11. DSW1's priority drops to 90, lower than DSW2's 100. Because DSW2 preempts, DSW2 becomes Active. When the uplink returns, DSW1 goes back to 110 and preempts.
12. They point at a router's **real** address (DSW1's 10.10.10.2), not the virtual IP 10.10.10.1, so when that router fails they lose their gateway. Fix the host or DHCP `default-router` setting.
13. **No.** HSRP and VRRP are different protocols with different packets, addresses and MACs; they never form one group, so both switches would act as gateway independently. Use VRRP on both for multi-vendor gateways.
