## Chapter 14 — DHCPv4 client, server and relay

**Objective covered:** **1.7 Troubleshoot DHCPv4 client, server, and relay on IOS devices.** You configure an IOS router as a DHCP server, an IOS Layer 3 switch as a relay agent, and an IOS interface as a DHCP client, then find out why a host did not get the right address.

### 14.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Explain the DHCP DORA exchange, leases, renewal and the most important options.
2. Configure an IOS DHCP server with pools, exclusions and reservations.
3. Explain why relay is needed, configure `ip helper-address`, and describe the giaddr field.
4. Configure an IOS interface as a DHCP client.
5. Verify DHCP with bindings, pool statistics and conflict tables.
6. Troubleshoot DHCP methodically from the client to the server and back.

### 14.2 Why DHCP

Typing an address, mask, gateway and DNS server into every PC, phone and laptop is slow and error-prone, and mistakes cause duplicates and wrong gateways (Chapter 2). **DHCP (Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol)** lets a host ask the network for its settings. A **DHCP server** keeps pools of addresses and **leases** them for a set time.

| Fact | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Transport | UDP: **server port 67**, **client port 68** |
| First messages | Broadcast (the client has no address yet) |
| What a client receives | Address, mask, default gateway, DNS servers, domain name, lease time, plus optional extras |
| Typical server | A Windows/Linux server in larger networks; an IOS router or switch in small sites and labs |
| IPv6 equivalent | DHCPv6 and SLAAC (Chapter 3); this chapter is IPv4 only |

### 14.3 The DORA exchange

A new client gets its address in four messages, remembered as **DORA** (Figure 14.1):

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch14/fig-14-01-dhcp-dora.svg" alt="Figure 14.1 — The DHCP DORA exchange between a client and a server on the same subnet" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 14.1</b> — The DHCP DORA exchange between a client and a server on the same subnet</sub></p>

In Figure 14.1 time runs downward. The two messages from the client are broadcasts because it has no address yet; the highlighted Acknowledgment is the moment the lease begins.

1. **Discover** — client → broadcast. Source 0.0.0.0:68, destination 255.255.255.255:67. "Is there a DHCP server?" It carries the client's MAC address.
2. **Offer** — server → client. Proposes an address (10.10.10.11), mask, gateway, DNS and lease time. Before offering, an IOS server pings the address to make sure no one is using it.
3. **Request** — client → **broadcast**. "I accept the offer from server X." It is broadcast so that every server that made an offer learns which one was chosen; the others return their addresses to the pool.
4. **Acknowledgment (ACK)** — server → client. Confirms the lease. The client then checks the address with ARP (a duplicate triggers a DHCPDECLINE) and starts using it.

**Other messages:** **NAK** (server refuses a request, for example the client moved to another subnet), **DECLINE** (client found the offered address already in use), **RELEASE** (client gives the address back: `ipconfig /release`), **INFORM** (client already has an address and wants only options).

**Lease timers.** At **T1 = 50 %** of the lease the client **renews** by unicast with its own server. If that fails, at **T2 = 87.5 %** it **rebinds** by broadcast to any server. If the lease expires, it stops using the address and starts again with a Discover. A Windows client that gets no answer at all assigns itself an **APIPA** address, 169.254.x.x (Chapter 2) — the classic sign of a DHCP failure.

**Options you should recognise:**

| Option | Meaning | IOS pool command |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Subnet mask | `network <net> <mask>` |
| 3 | Default gateway (router) | `default-router` |
| 6 | DNS servers | `dns-server` |
| 15 | Domain name | `domain-name` |
| 51 | Lease time | `lease <days> [hours] [minutes]` |
| 43 | Vendor-specific, e.g. WLC address for lightweight APs (Chapter 7) | `option 43 hex …` |
| 82 | Relay agent information (added by relays and snooping switches, Chapter 23) | — |
| 150 (Cisco) / 66 | TFTP server for IP phones | `option 150 ip …` |

### 14.4 Configuring an IOS DHCP server

An IOS router or Layer 3 switch can serve DHCP. All commands below are in **global configuration** mode, except the pool's own options in **DHCP pool configuration** mode (`R1(dhcp-config)#`).

```
R1(config)# ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.10.1 10.10.10.10      ! never lease gateways, servers, printers
R1(config)# ip dhcp pool STAFF
R1(dhcp-config)# network 10.10.10.0 255.255.255.0                ! the pool's subnet and mask (option 1)
R1(dhcp-config)# default-router 10.10.10.1                       ! option 3: the HSRP virtual IP, not a real one
R1(dhcp-config)# dns-server 10.10.99.53                          ! option 6 (the DNS server built in Chapter 22)
R1(dhcp-config)# domain-name campus.example                      ! option 15
R1(dhcp-config)# lease 2                                         ! 2 days (default 1 day)
```

| Command | Purpose | Common mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ip dhcp excluded-address <first> [<last>]` | Addresses the server must never hand out | Forgetting the gateway and its HSRP real addresses (.1, .2, .3), so the server offers them to PCs |
| `ip dhcp pool <name>` | Creates a pool; the name is local | — |
| `network <net> <mask>` | Subnet the pool serves; with relay, it must contain the relay's giaddr (14.5) | A mask or network that does not match the client VLAN |
| `default-router <ip>` | Gateway given to clients | A router's real IP instead of the FHRP virtual IP |
| `dns-server <ip> [<ip>]` | DNS servers | Leaving it out: names do not resolve (Chapter 22) |
| `lease {days [hours [min]] \| infinite}` | Lease time | Very short leases cause constant renewals |
| `host <ip> <mask>` + `client-identifier <01+MAC>` or `hardware-address <MAC>` | A **reservation**: one pool per reserved host | Packet Tracer support varies; give printers static addresses if it is missing |
| `service dhcp` | DHCP server and relay service (on by default) | `no service dhcp` silently disables both |

**Conflict detection.** Before an Offer, IOS pings the address. If something answers, the address goes into the **conflict table** (`show ip dhcp conflict`) and is not leased until you clear it with `clear ip dhcp conflict *`.

### 14.5 DHCP relay: crossing a router

Discover messages are broadcasts, and **routers do not forward broadcasts**. If the DHCP server sits in another subnet — usual in any real network — the router facing the clients must **relay** the request (Figure 14.2):

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch14/fig-14-02-dhcp-relay-giaddr.svg" alt="Figure 14.2 — DHCP relay: the client's broadcast becomes a unicast to the server, stamped with giaddr" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 14.2</b> — DHCP relay: the client's broadcast becomes a unicast to the server, stamped with giaddr</sub></p>

In Figure 14.2 the grey legs are on the client's VLAN and the highlighted legs cross the routed network. The three cards show what is in the packet at each stage; the giaddr value is what lets one central server serve many VLANs.

1. The client broadcasts its Discover in VLAN 10.
2. The relay agent (the SVI or router interface with `ip helper-address`) receives it.
3. The relay writes its **own interface address** in the client VLAN into the **giaddr** (gateway IP address) field, for example 10.10.10.2, and **unicasts** the message to the server.
4. The server uses **giaddr** to choose the pool: the pool whose `network` contains 10.10.10.2 is STAFF.
5. The server unicasts the Offer back to giaddr; the relay sends it on to the client in VLAN 10. Request and Ack follow the same path.

```
DSW1(config)# interface vlan 10
DSW1(config-if)# ip helper-address 10.0.0.1          ! the DHCP server's address; one line per server
```

**Where it goes:** on the **interface that receives the client's broadcast** — the client VLAN's SVI (or router subinterface) — never on the interface toward the server. With HSRP (Chapter 13), configure it on **both** distribution switches; each relays, the server answers both, and the client takes one Offer.

**Requirements:** the server needs a **route back** to every giaddr subnet, and a pool whose network contains each giaddr. `ip helper-address` also forwards a few other UDP broadcast services by default (such as TFTP, DNS and NetBIOS), which matters only when you use them.

### 14.6 IOS as a DHCP client

A router's Internet-facing interface often gets its address from the ISP by DHCP:

```
R1(config)# interface gigabitethernet0/0/1
R1(config-if)# ip address dhcp                      ! interface configuration mode
R1(config-if)# no shutdown

R1# show dhcp lease
Temp IP addr: 198.51.100.6  for peer on Interface: GigabitEthernet0/0/1
Temp  sub net mask: 255.255.255.248
   DHCP Lease server: 198.51.100.1, state: 5 Bound
   DHCP transaction id: 1A2B
   Lease: 86400 secs,  Renewal: 43200 secs,  Rebind: 75600 secs
Temp default-gateway addr: 198.51.100.1
```

The router installs the received gateway as a **default route with AD 254** (`S* 0.0.0.0/0 [254/0]`), so any static default you configure (AD 1) wins (Chapter 10). Renewal at 43,200 s and rebind at 75,600 s are the 50 % and 87.5 % points of the 86,400 s lease. (Exact `show dhcp lease` layout varies by release.)

### 14.7 Verifying DHCP

```
R1# show ip dhcp binding
Bindings from all pools not associated with VRF:
IP address      Client-ID/              Lease expiration        Type
                Hardware address
10.10.10.11     0100.5079.6668.01       Oct 17 2026 09:14 AM    Automatic
10.10.30.11     0100.d0ff.3a21.9c       Oct 17 2026 09:15 AM    Automatic

R1# show ip dhcp pool STAFF
Pool STAFF :
 Utilization mark (high/low)    : 100 / 0
 Subnet size (first/next)       : 0 / 0
 Total addresses                : 254
 Leased addresses               : 1
 Pending event                  : none
 1 subnet is currently in the pool :
 Current index        IP address range                    Leased addresses
 10.10.10.12          10.10.10.1       - 10.10.10.254      1

R1# show ip dhcp conflict
IP address        Detection method   Detection time
10.10.10.3        Ping               Oct 15 2026 10:02 AM
```

Read them as: PC1's MAC (shown with a leading `01` = Ethernet) holds 10.10.10.11; the STAFF pool has leased one of 254 addresses; and 10.10.10.3 answered a ping before it could be offered — it is DSW2's real SVI address, which should have been excluded. (Output layout varies slightly between IOS releases and Packet Tracer.)

| Command | Device | Use it to check |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `show ip dhcp binding` | Server | Which client (MAC) has which address, until when |
| `show ip dhcp pool [name]` | Server | Pool size, leased count, next address |
| `show ip dhcp conflict` | Server | Addresses withheld because something already used them |
| `show ip dhcp server statistics` | Server | Discovers, Offers, Requests, ACKs counted — are requests arriving at all? |
| `show running-config \| section dhcp` | Server | Pools and exclusions |
| `show ip interface vlan 10` | Relay | `Helper address is 10.0.0.1` line |
| `show dhcp lease` | IOS client | Address, server, lease and gateway learned |
| `debug ip dhcp server events` / `packet` | Server | Each message as it arrives (use briefly; `undebug all` to stop) |
| `ipconfig /all`, `ipconfig /release`, `ipconfig /renew` | Windows client | Address, gateway, DNS, DHCP server, lease times (Chapter 17 adds macOS and Linux) |

### 14.8 Troubleshooting DHCP

Follow the request from the client toward the server, then follow the reply back (Figure 14.3).

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch14/fig-14-03-dhcp-troubleshooting.svg" alt="Figure 14.3 — DHCP troubleshooting flowchart from client to server" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 14.3</b> — DHCP troubleshooting flowchart from client to server</sub></p>

Figure 14.3 asks one question per hop, in the order the Discover travels. The first answer that leads right is the fault to fix; then start again from the top to confirm the client now gets an address.

| Symptom | Likely cause | Evidence / fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Client has 169.254.x.x | No Offer received: client port down or in the wrong VLAN, no relay, server unreachable, pool missing or exhausted | Work through Figure 14.3 |
| Clients in one VLAN only fail | `ip helper-address` missing on that SVI, or no pool whose network contains that giaddr | `show ip interface vlan X`; compare pools with SVI addresses |
| Relay configured, still nothing | Server has no route back to the giaddr subnet, or the helper points at the wrong address | Ping the server **from** the SVI address: `ping 10.0.0.1 source vlan 10` |
| Address fine, but no access off the subnet | Wrong `default-router`, or a real IP instead of the virtual IP | `ipconfig /all` gateway; fix the pool |
| Names do not resolve | `dns-server` missing in the pool | Add it (Chapter 22) |
| Clients get addresses from an unknown subnet | A **rogue DHCP server** (for example a home router plugged in) | Find it with the binding/MAC tables; prevent with DHCP snooping (Chapter 23) |
| New clients fail after weeks of working | Pool exhausted | `show ip dhcp pool`: leased = total; enlarge the subnet or shorten leases |
| Gateway or printer addresses handed out | Exclusions missing | Add `excluded-address`; check `show ip dhcp conflict`, then clear it |
| Client gives up before an Offer arrives | Access port without PortFast waits \~30 s for STP | `spanning-tree portfast` (Chapter 9) |
| Server never answers anyone | `no service dhcp`, or an ACL blocking UDP 67/68 | Re-enable the service; check ACLs (Chapter 20) |

**Server statistics tell you where to look.** If `show ip dhcp server statistics` shows Discovers arriving but no Offers, the server has no matching pool (or it is exhausted). If no Discovers arrive at all, the problem is between the client and the server: the relay, the routing or the client's VLAN.

### 14.9 Packet Tracer Lab 14.1 — Central DHCP server, redundant relays and an IOS client (Intermediate–Advanced)

**Objective:** 1.7 (server, relay and client). **Start from:** your Lab 13.1 file. **You will:** make R1 the campus DHCP server for VLANs 10 and 30, relay from both distribution switches, switch PC1, PC2 and PC3 to DHCP, then add an ISP router that gives R1 its outside address by DHCP. Printers and switches keep static addresses.

**New devices:** 1 × ISR4331 (ISP), one copper crossover cable R1 G0/0/1 ↔ ISP G0/0/0.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch14/fig-14-04-lab-14-1-topology.svg" alt="Figure 14.4 — Lab 14.1 topology: DHCP server R1, relays DSW1 and DSW2, ISP serving R1's outside interface" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 14.4</b> — Lab 14.1 topology: DHCP server R1, relays DSW1 and DSW2, ISP serving R1's outside interface</sub></p>

Figure 14.4 shows the three DHCP roles in one network: R1 is a server for the campus, both distribution switches relay, and R1's outside interface is itself a DHCP client of the ISP.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| DHCP server | R1, 10.0.0.1 (G0/0/0 toward DSW1) |
| Pool STAFF | 10.10.10.0/24, gateway 10.10.10.1 (HSRP virtual), excluded .1–.10 |
| Pool WIRELESS | 10.10.30.0/24, gateway 10.10.30.1 (HSRP virtual), excluded .1–.10 |
| DNS / domain (both pools) | 10.10.99.53 (built in Chapter 22) / campus.example |
| Relays | DSW1 and DSW2, `interface vlan 10` and `vlan 30` → `ip helper-address 10.0.0.1` |
| ISP | G0/0/0 198.51.100.1/29, pool OUTSIDE 198.51.100.0/29, Lo0 192.0.2.8/32 (an Internet host) |
| R1 G0/0/1 | DHCP client; expected address 198.51.100.2/29 |

**R1 — DHCP server**

```
configure terminal
ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.10.1 10.10.10.10
ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.30.1 10.10.30.10
ip dhcp pool STAFF
 network 10.10.10.0 255.255.255.0
 default-router 10.10.10.1
 dns-server 10.10.99.53
 domain-name campus.example
ip dhcp pool WIRELESS
 network 10.10.30.0 255.255.255.0
 default-router 10.10.30.1
 dns-server 10.10.99.53
 domain-name campus.example
end
```

R1 already has `ip route 10.10.0.0 255.255.0.0 10.0.0.2` from Lab 5.1 — that is the **route back** to every giaddr.

**DSW1 and DSW2 — relays** (identical on both)

```
configure terminal
interface vlan 10
 ip helper-address 10.0.0.1
interface vlan 30
 ip helper-address 10.0.0.1
end
copy running-config startup-config
```

DSW2 reaches 10.0.0.1 through its default route to DSW1 (Lab 6.1).

**Clients:** set PC1, PC2 (VLAN 30) and PC3 to **DHCP** in Desktop → IP Configuration. Within a few seconds each shows an address from its pool, gateway .1 and DNS 10.10.99.53.

**ISP and R1 — DHCP client**

```
! ISP
enable
configure terminal
hostname ISP
interface gigabitethernet0/0/0
 ip address 198.51.100.1 255.255.255.248
 no shutdown
interface loopback0
 ip address 192.0.2.8 255.255.255.255
exit
ip dhcp excluded-address 198.51.100.1
ip dhcp pool OUTSIDE
 network 198.51.100.0 255.255.255.248
 default-router 198.51.100.1
exit
ip route 10.0.0.0 255.0.0.0 198.51.100.2      ! return route (NAT replaces this in Chapter 21)
end

! R1
configure terminal
interface gigabitethernet0/0/1
 ip address dhcp
 no shutdown
end
```

**Verification and expected results**

| Check | Command (device) | Expected |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Leases | `show ip dhcp binding` (R1) | PC1, PC3 in 10.10.10.11+, PC2 in 10.10.30.11+ |
| Relay configured | `show ip interface vlan 10` (DSW1, DSW2) | `Helper address is 10.0.0.1` |
| Client settings | PC1 `ipconfig /all` | Gateway 10.10.10.1, DNS 10.10.99.53, DHCP server 10.0.0.1 |
| Pool use | `show ip dhcp pool` (R1) | Leased counts match the clients |
| Conflicts | `show ip dhcp conflict` (R1) | Empty (the exclusions cover .1–.3) |
| IOS client | `show ip interface brief` and `show dhcp lease` (R1) | G0/0/1 198.51.100.2; lease from 198.51.100.1 |
| Default from DHCP | `show ip route` (R1) | `S* 0.0.0.0/0 [254/0] via 198.51.100.1` |
| End to end | PC1 `ping 192.0.2.8` | Success |

**PT notes:** `show dhcp lease` and `show ip dhcp server statistics` are missing in some Packet Tracer versions; `show ip interface brief`, `show ip route` and `show ip dhcp binding` are enough to finish the lab. Use Simulation mode with a DHCP filter to watch the Discover reach DSW1 as a broadcast and leave as a unicast to 10.0.0.1.

**Experiments**

1. **Relay redundancy:** shut DSW1's `interface vlan 10`, then on PC1 click DHCP again (or `ipconfig /renew`). PC1 still gets an address, relayed by DSW2 with giaddr 10.10.10.3.
2. **Missing exclusion:** remove `ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.10.1 10.10.10.10`, renew PC3, and run `show ip dhcp conflict`: .1, .2 and .3 appear (the virtual and real gateway addresses answered R1's ping). Restore the exclusion and `clear ip dhcp conflict *`.

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] Clients in VLANs 10 and 30 lease addresses from R1 through either relay
- [ ] Every lease uses the HSRP virtual gateway
- [ ] R1 learns its outside address and a default route \[254/0\] by DHCP
- [ ] PC1 reaches 192.0.2.8
- [ ] Both experiments reproduced and explained
- [ ] File saved (Chapters 20, 21 and 23 build on it)

### 14.10 Lab 14.2 — DHCP challenge

On a copy of Lab 14.1 apply: (1) R1 `ip dhcp pool WIRELESS` → `network 10.10.31.0 255.255.255.0`; (2) R1 `ip dhcp pool STAFF` → `default-router 10.10.10.2`; (3) DSW2 `interface vlan 10` → `no ip helper-address 10.0.0.1` → `ip helper-address 10.0.0.9`; (4) R1 `no ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.30.1 10.10.30.10`. Renew every client and diagnose using Figure 14.3.

**Answer key (hide until done)**

| Fault | Symptom | Evidence | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| WIRELESS pool network does not contain giaddr 10.10.30.2/.3 | PC2 gets 169.254.x.x | No pool matches the giaddr; no binding in 10.10.30.x | `network 10.10.30.0 255.255.255.0` |
| STAFF gateway is DSW1's real IP | Works now, but VLAN 10 loses its gateway if DSW1 fails | `ipconfig /all` gateway 10.10.10.2 | `default-router 10.10.10.1` |
| DSW2 helper points at 10.0.0.9 | Hidden: DSW1 still relays, so clients work until DSW1 fails | `show ip interface vlan 10` on DSW2 | Correct the helper address |
| Exclusion removed | Only visible once fault 1 is fixed: .1–.3 land in the conflict table | `show ip dhcp conflict` | Restore the exclusion, clear conflicts |

Faults 2 and 3 are the dangerous kind: nothing breaks until the failure that redundancy was meant to survive.

### 14.11 Chapter 14 summary notes (exam revision)

- **DORA:** Discover (broadcast) → Offer → Request (broadcast) → Ack. Server UDP **67**, client UDP **68**. Also NAK, DECLINE, RELEASE, INFORM.
- **Lease:** renew by unicast at **T1 = 50 %**, rebind by broadcast at **T2 = 87.5 %**. No server at all → Windows uses **169.254.x.x** (APIPA).
- **IOS server:** `ip dhcp excluded-address` (global) + `ip dhcp pool` with `network`, `default-router`, `dns-server`, `domain-name`, `lease`. The server pings before offering; answered addresses go to `show ip dhcp conflict`.
- **Relay:** `ip helper-address <server>` on the **client-facing** interface/SVI; the relay fills **giaddr** with its own interface IP and unicasts to the server; the server picks the pool whose network contains giaddr and needs a **route back**.
- **IOS client:** `ip address dhcp` on the interface; default gateway installed as **AD 254**; `show dhcp lease`.
- **Verify:** `show ip dhcp binding`, `show ip dhcp pool`, `show ip dhcp conflict`, `show ip dhcp server statistics`, `show ip interface` (helper), `ipconfig /all`.
- With HSRP: `default-router` = virtual IP; exclude virtual and real addresses; relay on both gateways.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "`ip helper-address` goes on the interface toward the server" | On the interface that receives the client broadcast |
| "The relay forwards the broadcast as a broadcast" | It converts it to a unicast to the server |
| "The server picks the pool from the client's MAC" | From giaddr (or, with no relay, the interface it arrived on) |
| "169.254.x.x means the DHCP server gave a bad address" | It means no DHCP server answered at all |
| "The excluded-address command goes inside the pool" | It is a global configuration command |

### 14.12 Practice questions — Chapter 14

**1. (Recall)** Put the DHCP messages in order and say which are broadcast from the client: Request, Offer, Discover, Acknowledgment.

**2. (Recall)** Which UDP ports does DHCP use?

A. 53 and 54  B. 67 and 68  C. 69 and 70  D. 161 and 162

**3. (Concept)** A Windows PC shows 169.254.33.17. What does that tell you?

**4. (Configuration)** Write the commands to create a pool VOICE for 10.10.20.0/24 with gateway 10.10.20.1 and DNS 10.10.99.53, excluding .1 to .10.

**5. (Relay)** The DHCP server is 10.0.0.1. Clients are in VLAN 30 behind a Layer 3 switch. Where exactly do you configure `ip helper-address 10.0.0.1`?

**6. (Relay)** What value does the relay place in giaddr, and what does the server use it for?

**7. (Troubleshooting)** The relay is correct, the pool exists, but clients in 10.10.30.0/24 get no address. The server's routing table has no route to 10.10.30.0/24. Explain the failure.

**8. (Output interpretation)** `show ip dhcp conflict` lists 10.10.10.1, .2 and .3. What happened and how do you fix it?

**9. (Timers)** A client has an 8-hour lease. When does it first try to renew, and how?

**10. (IOS client)** R1's G0/0/1 uses `ip address dhcp`, and you also configure `ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 203.0.113.9`. Which default route is used, and why?

**11. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which settings commonly cause clients to get an address but fail to reach other subnets?

A. Wrong `default-router`  B. Missing `ip helper-address`  C. Gateway set to a router's real IP that has failed  D. Pool exhausted  E. Missing `excluded-address`

**12. (Troubleshooting)** `show ip dhcp server statistics` shows DHCPDISCOVER counters rising but DHCPOFFER at 0. Where is the problem?

**13. (Security)** Users in VLAN 10 suddenly get addresses in 192.168.0.0/24. What is the likely cause?

**14. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** Clients in VLAN 40 cannot get addresses. A network assistant recommends: *"Add `ip helper-address 10.0.0.1` to R1's G0/0/0 interface facing DSW1."* Is that right?

### 14.13 Answer key with explanations

1. **Discover → Offer → Request → Acknowledgment.** Discover and Request are broadcast by the client (the Request is broadcast so every offering server learns the choice).
2. **B.** 67 is the server, 68 the client. 53 is DNS; 69 TFTP; 161/162 SNMP.
3. No DHCP server answered, so Windows assigned itself an **APIPA** address. Work through Figure 14.3 from the client port toward the server.
4. `ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.20.1 10.10.20.10` → `ip dhcp pool VOICE` → `network 10.10.20.0 255.255.255.0` → `default-router 10.10.20.1` → `dns-server 10.10.99.53`.
5. On `interface vlan 30` of the Layer 3 switch — the interface that receives the clients' broadcasts (and on the second gateway too, if there is one).
6. The relay's own IP address on the client's subnet (for example 10.10.30.2). The server uses it to choose the pool whose network contains it, and sends its replies back to that address.
7. The server receives the relayed Discover but cannot send the Offer back to giaddr 10.10.30.x because it has no route to that subnet. Add the route (or fix the routing protocol).
8. The server pinged those addresses before offering them and they answered: they belong to the gateway (virtual IP and both real SVI addresses) because they were never excluded. Add `ip dhcp excluded-address 10.10.10.1 10.10.10.10` and `clear ip dhcp conflict *`.
9. At **T1 = 4 hours** (50 %), by **unicast** to the server that granted the lease. If that fails, it rebinds by broadcast at 7 hours (87.5 %).
10. The **static** route (AD 1) beats the DHCP-learned default (AD 254) for the same prefix 0.0.0.0/0.
11. **A and C.** Both leave the client with a gateway that cannot forward its traffic. B and D stop the client getting an address at all; E causes conflicts.
12. Requests reach the server, so relay and routing toward it work; the server has **no pool matching** the request (network/giaddr mismatch) or the pool is exhausted.
13. A **rogue DHCP server** — often a home router plugged into a wall port. Find its port from the MAC table and prevent it with DHCP snooping (Chapter 23).
14. **No.** The helper must be on the interface that receives the client broadcasts: `interface vlan 40` on DSW1 and DSW2. R1 is the server; it does not relay. Also check that a pool for 10.10.40.0/24 exists — Lab 14.1 has none, because printers use static addresses.

**Related objectives that reuse this chapter:** 1.6 client connectivity (Chapter 17), 4.7.a DHCP snooping (Chapter 23).

## Chapter 15 — The troubleshooting toolkit

**Objective covered:** **2.4 Troubleshoot basic Layer 2/Layer 3 connectivity and device operations using show commands (including show logs), ping, extended ping, trace route, and packet capture output.** This chapter gathers the tools you have used one at a time since Chapter 1 into one method, and adds extended ping, traceroute in depth and reading packet captures.

### 15.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Follow a structured troubleshooting method instead of guessing.
2. Choose the right `show` command for each layer and for device health.
3. Read IOS log messages and know which ones matter.
4. Interpret ping and extended ping results, including every IOS result symbol.
5. Explain how traceroute works and read its output, including timeouts and loops.
6. Read packet capture output for ARP, ICMP, TCP and DHCP.
7. Work a realistic trouble ticket from symptom to fix.

### 15.2 A troubleshooting method

Random changes create new faults and hide the original one. Use the same steps every time:

1. **Define the problem precisely.** Who is affected, what fails (all traffic, one application, one destination), since when, what changed.
2. **Gather facts** with show commands, logs, ping and traceroute — before touching the configuration.
3. **Compare with the baseline**: documentation (Chapter 8), saved configurations, how it worked yesterday.
4. **Form a hypothesis** that explains *all* the symptoms.
5. **Change one thing**, and record what you changed.
6. **Verify** the fix from the user's point of view; if it did not work, undo the change.
7. **Document** the cause and the fix.

**Approaches to narrowing it down:**

| Approach | How | Best when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bottom-up | Physical → data link → network → transport → application | Hardware or cabling suspected; new installations |
| Top-down | Application first, then down the stack | One application fails, everything else works |
| Divide and conquer | Start at Layer 3 with ping; success → look higher, failure → look lower | Most general problems — the usual CCNA choice |
| Follow the path | Check each device hop by hop from source to destination (and back) | Multi-hop routing faults |
| Compare | Diff a working device or configuration against the broken one | Many similar devices (access switches, branches) |

Figure 15.1 maps the layered checks to the commands that answer them.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch15/fig-15-01-divide-and-conquer.svg" alt="Figure 15.1 — Divide-and-conquer troubleshooting: the question and the commands at each layer" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 15.1</b> — Divide-and-conquer troubleshooting: the question and the commands at each layer</sub></p>

In Figure 15.1 each band is a layer: its question on the left, the commands that answer it on the right. The highlighted Network band is where divide and conquer starts.

### 15.3 Show commands for connectivity and device operations

| Layer / area | Command | What to look for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Device health | `show version` | IOS version, uptime, **last reload reason**, memory, configuration register, licence |
| Device health | `show processes cpu sorted` / `show processes cpu history` | Sustained high CPU (a loop or broadcast storm, a process stuck) |
| Device health | `show environment` / `show inventory` | Power supplies, fans, temperature; modules and serial numbers (platform-dependent) |
| Configuration | `show running-config`, `show startup-config` | Differences mean unsaved changes |
| Physical / data link | `show ip interface brief`, `show interfaces status`, `show interfaces <int>` | up/down, err-disabled, speed/duplex, errors (Chapter 1) |
| Data link | `show vlan brief`, `show interfaces trunk`, `show interfaces switchport` | Port in the right VLAN; trunk carrying it (Chapter 5) |
| Data link | `show mac address-table [address \| interface \| vlan]` | Which port a host is really on |
| Data link | `show spanning-tree [vlan]`, `show etherchannel summary`, `show cdp neighbors` | Blocking ports, bundles, neighbors (Chapters 6, 8, 9) |
| Network | `show ip arp` / `show arp` | IP-to-MAC mapping; `Incomplete` = no ARP reply |
| Network | `show ip route [address]`, `show ip protocols`, `show ip ospf neighbor` | Route present, next hop, protocol health (Chapters 10–12) |
| Network | `show standby brief`, `show ip dhcp binding` | Gateway redundancy and DHCP (Chapters 13–14) |
| Logs | `show logging` | Events with times (15.4) |

**Filter long output** with a pipe, available on every show command: `| include <text>`, `| exclude <text>`, `| begin <text>`, `| section <text>`. Example: `show running-config | section interface Vlan10`, or `show logging | include LINEPROTO`.

```
DSW1# show version | include uptime|Last reload|Version
Cisco IOS XE Software, Version 16.03.02
DSW1 uptime is 2 weeks, 3 days, 4 hours, 12 minutes
Last reload reason: power-on
```

An uptime of a few minutes on a device nobody restarted, or a reload reason such as a crash, is a clue in itself. (Version strings vary by platform.)

### 15.4 Reading logs with `show logging`

IOS records events — interfaces going up or down, neighbors forming, errors — as **log messages**. On the console you see them as they happen; remotely (SSH) you see them only after `terminal monitor`; and `show logging` lists those kept in the **logging buffer** in RAM.

```
DSW1# show logging
Syslog logging: enabled (0 messages dropped, 0 flushes, 0 overruns)
    Console logging: level debugging, 212 messages logged
    Buffer logging:  level informational, 188 messages logged
Log Buffer (16384 bytes):
*Oct 15 10:02:13.457: %LINK-3-UPDOWN: Interface GigabitEthernet1/0/2, changed state to down
*Oct 15 10:02:14.461: %LINEPROTO-5-UPDOWN: Line protocol on Interface GigabitEthernet1/0/2, changed state to down
*Oct 15 10:02:14.470: %EC-5-UNBUNDLE: Interface Gi1/0/2 left the port-channel Port-channel2
*Oct 15 10:05:40.112: %SYS-5-CONFIG_I: Configured from console by admin on vty0 (10.10.99.50)
```

Every message has the same structure (Chapter 25 covers syslog servers and severities in full):

| Part | Example | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Timestamp | `*Oct 15 10:02:13.457` | When; `*` means the clock is not synchronised (NTP) |
| Facility | `LINEPROTO` | The part of IOS that raised it |
| Severity | `5` | 0 (emergency) to 7 (debugging); lower is more serious |
| Mnemonic | `UPDOWN` | Short code for the event |
| Description | `Line protocol on Interface …` | Human-readable detail |

**Messages worth recognising:**

| Message | Points to | Chapter |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `%LINK-3-UPDOWN` / `%LINEPROTO-5-UPDOWN` | Physical link / line protocol changed state | 1 |
| `%CDP-4-DUPLEX_MISMATCH` | Duplex mismatch with the neighbor | 1, 8 |
| `%CDP-4-NATIVE_VLAN_MISMATCH` | Native VLAN differs on a trunk | 5 |
| `%PM-4-ERR_DISABLE` | Port err-disabled (BPDU guard, port security, channel misconfiguration) | 6, 9, 23 |
| `%SPANTREE-2-BLOCK_BPDUGUARD`, `%SPANTREE-2-ROOTGUARD_BLOCK` | STP protection acted | 9 |
| `%SW_MATM-4-MACFLAP_NOTIF` | A MAC address moving between ports: likely a loop | 9 |
| `%IP-4-DUPADDR` | Duplicate IP address on the segment | 2 |
| `%OSPF-5-ADJCHG` | OSPF neighbor state changed | 12 |
| `%HSRP-5-STATECHANGE` | HSRP role changed | 13 |
| `%SYS-5-CONFIG_I` | Someone changed the configuration — and who | 18 |

**Useful logging settings** (global configuration): `service timestamps log datetime msec` (real dates in messages), `logging buffered 16384 informational` (bigger buffer), and on the console line `logging synchronous` (messages no longer interrupt your typing).

### 15.5 Ping

`ping` sends **ICMP Echo Request** (type 8) messages and waits for **Echo Reply** (type 0). A reply proves the whole path works **in both directions** at Layer 3.

```
DSW1# ping 10.10.40.11
Type escape sequence to abort.
Sending 5, 100-byte ICMP Echos to 10.10.40.11, timeout is 2 seconds:
.!!!!
Success rate is 80 percent (4/5), round-trip min/avg/max = 1/1/2 ms
```

The first `.` is normal: the router was waiting for an **ARP** reply before it could send the first packet.

| IOS symbol | Meaning | Usual cause |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `!` | Echo Reply received | Success |
| `.` | Timed out | No route on the way **or the way back**, a filter dropping silently, host down, ARP pending (first packet) |
| `U` | ICMP Destination Unreachable received | A router on the path has no route, or an ACL rejected it |
| `Q` | Source quench | Congestion (rare today) |
| `M` | Could not fragment | Packet too big with DF set: an MTU problem |
| `?` | Unknown packet type received | Unexpected ICMP reply |
| `&` | Packet lifetime exceeded | TTL expired: a routing loop |

Windows prints `Reply from …`, `Request timed out.`, or `Reply from 10.0.0.1: Destination host unreachable.` — the last one comes from a router, not the target, and tells you which router gave up.

### 15.6 Extended ping

A plain ping from a router uses the **exit interface's address** as the source. That hides return-path problems: the far end may know how to reach the link subnet but not your LANs (Chapter 11). **Extended ping** lets you choose the source, size, count and other options.

**Interactive form** (privileged EXEC, type `ping` with no address):

```
DSW2# ping
Protocol [ip]:
Target IP address: 10.10.40.11
Repeat count [5]: 20
Datagram size [100]: 1500
Timeout in seconds [2]:
Extended commands [n]: y
Source address or interface: vlan 10
Type of service [0]:
Set DF bit in IP header? [no]: yes
Validate reply data? [no]:
Data pattern [0x0000ABCD]:
Loose, Strict, Record, Timestamp, Verbose[none]:
Sweep range of sizes [n]:
Sending 20, 1500-byte ICMP Echos to 10.10.40.11, timeout is 2 seconds:
Packet sent with a source address of 10.10.10.3
Packet sent with the DF bit set
!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!!
Success rate is 100 percent (20/20), round-trip min/avg/max = 1/2/4 ms
```

**One-line form** (same result): `ping 10.10.40.11 source vlan 10 repeat 20 size 1500 df-bit`.

| Option | Why you would change it |
| --- | --- |
| **Source address or interface** | Test the return route to a specific LAN — the most important option |
| Repeat count | Catch intermittent loss (100 or 1000 pings) |
| Datagram size + **DF bit** | Find MTU problems: large DF packets fail with `M` while small ones succeed |
| Sweep range of sizes | Find the exact largest size that passes |
| Timeout | Slow links (satellite, congested WAN) |

**Reading a pair of results.** From DSW2, `ping 10.10.40.11` succeeds but `ping 10.10.40.11 source vlan 10` fails: the printer can reach DSW2's VLAN 40 address (same subnet) but cannot answer a VLAN 10 address — so its **default gateway** is wrong or missing. Lab 15.1 uses exactly this test.

### 15.7 Traceroute

Ping says *whether* a destination answers; **traceroute** shows *the path* and where it stops. It relies on the TTL field (Figure 15.2):

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch15/fig-15-02-traceroute-ttl.svg" alt="Figure 15.2 — How traceroute discovers each hop with rising TTL values" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 15.2</b> — How traceroute discovers each hop with rising TTL values</sub></p>

Figure 15.2 runs top to bottom in time. Each probe travels one router further than the last before its TTL runs out, and the router where it dies reveals itself in the reply.

1. The source sends probes with **TTL 1**. The first router decrements TTL to 0, drops the probe and returns **ICMP Time Exceeded** from its own address — that is hop 1.
2. Probes with **TTL 2** reach the second router, which returns Time Exceeded — hop 2.
3. TTL keeps rising until a probe reaches the **destination**, which answers differently (ICMP Port Unreachable for UDP probes, Echo Reply for ICMP probes) and the trace ends.
4. Each hop is normally probed **three** times, so you see three round-trip times per line.

| Implementation | Probe type |
| --- | --- |
| Cisco IOS `traceroute` | UDP to high ports (starting at 33434) |
| Linux / macOS `traceroute` | UDP by default (`-I` for ICMP) |
| Windows `tracert` | ICMP Echo Request |

```
C:\> tracert 192.0.2.8
Tracing route to 192.0.2.8 over a maximum of 30 hops
  1     1 ms    <1 ms    <1 ms  10.10.10.2
  2     1 ms     1 ms     1 ms  10.0.0.1
  3     2 ms     1 ms     1 ms  192.0.2.8
Trace complete.
```

Note hop 1: the **real** address of DSW1 (10.10.10.2), not the HSRP virtual IP — the Active router answers from its own interface address.

```
DSW1# traceroute 10.20.20.1
Type escape sequence to abort.
Tracing the route to 10.20.20.1
  1 10.0.0.1 1 msec 1 msec 0 msec
  2 198.51.100.1 1 msec 1 msec 1 msec
  3 198.51.100.2 2 msec 1 msec 1 msec
  4 198.51.100.1 2 msec 2 msec 1 msec
  5 198.51.100.2 2 msec 2 msec 2 msec
  ...
```

The same two addresses alternating is a **routing loop**: R1 sends 10.20.20.0/24 to the ISP by its default route, and the ISP sends it back by its 10.0.0.0/8 route. Neither has a specific route, so the packet bounces until TTL expires.

| Traceroute shows | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `*` | No reply within the timeout for that probe |
| `* * *` from some hop onward | The path stops there: the next router has no route, a filter drops probes, or replies cannot get back |
| `!H` / `!N` / `!P` | Host / network / protocol unreachable returned |
| `!A` | Administratively prohibited — an ACL rejected it (Chapter 20) |
| Repeating addresses | Routing loop |
| Final hop never reached, but ping works | Probes (UDP high ports) filtered while ICMP Echo is allowed |

Traceroute shows the **forward** path only. The return path may differ (asymmetric routing), so a stop at hop *n* can also mean hop *n* cannot route its Time Exceeded **back** to you.

### 15.8 Packet capture output

When show commands disagree with what users report, a **packet capture** shows exactly what is on the wire. You must be able to read capture output; Figure 15.3 walks through one.

**Ways to capture:**

| Method | How | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Wireshark / tcpdump on a host | Capture on the PC's own NIC | Sees only that host's traffic (and broadcasts) |
| **SPAN** (port mirroring) on a switch | `monitor session 1 source interface gi1/0/5 both` + `monitor session 1 destination interface gi1/0/24` (global configuration) | Copies a port's or VLAN's traffic to a port where a capture PC listens |
| IOS XE **Embedded Packet Capture** | `monitor capture CAP interface g0/0/0 both match any` → `monitor capture CAP start` → `show monitor capture CAP buffer brief` (privileged EXEC) | Captures on the router itself; syntax varies by release |
| Packet Tracer **Simulation mode** | Event List, filter by protocol, click a packet to see each layer | Packet Tracer's stand-in for a capture |

```
No. Time      Source          Destination     Protocol Info
1   0.000000  0050.7966.6801  Broadcast       ARP      Who has 10.10.10.1? Tell 10.10.10.11
2   0.000412  0000.0c9f.f00a  0050.7966.6801  ARP      10.10.10.1 is at 0000.0c9f.f00a
3   0.000530  10.10.10.11     192.0.2.8       ICMP     Echo (ping) request id=0x0001, seq=1, ttl=128
4   0.002110  192.0.2.8       10.10.10.11     ICMP     Echo (ping) reply   id=0x0001, seq=1, ttl=253
5   5.101000  10.10.10.11     10.10.40.11     TCP      49812 → 9100 [SYN] Seq=0
6   5.102300  10.10.40.11     10.10.10.11     TCP      9100 → 49812 [RST, ACK] Seq=1 Ack=1
7   9.800000  10.10.10.11     10.10.40.11     TCP      49813 → 631 [SYN] Seq=0
8   9.801100  10.10.40.11     10.10.10.11     TCP      631 → 49813 [SYN, ACK] Seq=0 Ack=1
9   9.801300  10.10.10.11     10.10.40.11     TCP      49813 → 631 [ACK] Seq=1 Ack=1
```

(Wireshark shows MAC addresses with vendor names such as `Cisco_9f:f0:0a`; the layout here is simplified.)

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch15/fig-15-03-reading-a-capture.svg" alt="Figure 15.3 — Reading the capture above: four conversations and what each proves" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 15.3</b> — Reading the capture above: four conversations and what each proves</sub></p>

Figure 15.3 groups the nine frames into conversations, each with its verdict. The amber pair is the one that matters for a "cannot print" ticket: the network works, the printer's raw-print service on port 9100 does not.

**Patterns to recognise:**

| You see | It means |
| --- | --- |
| ARP request, no reply | Target not on this subnet/VLAN, down, or wrong VLAN |
| ARP reply with 0000.0c9f.fXXX / 0000.0c07.acXX | The gateway is an HSRP virtual IP (Chapter 13) |
| Echo request, no reply | Path or return path broken, or ICMP filtered |
| TTL in a reply lower than expected | Count the hops: 255 → 253 means two routers decremented it |
| TCP SYN → **RST** | Host reachable, but **nothing listens** on that port: an application issue, not a network one |
| TCP SYN repeated, no answer | Filtered (ACL, firewall) or no route back |
| SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK | Connection established (three-way handshake) |
| DHCP Discover repeated, no Offer | DHCP problem (Chapter 14) |
| Many identical broadcasts per second | Broadcast storm: a loop (Chapter 9) |

### 15.9 Which tool finds which fault

| Fault | First symptom | Tool that proves it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cable unplugged / bad | `down/down` | `show ip interface brief`, `show interfaces status` |
| Port err-disabled | `err-disabled` status | `show interfaces status err-disabled`, `show logging` |
| Host in the wrong VLAN | Wrong subnet from DHCP, or no address | `show vlan brief`, `show mac address-table address <mac>` |
| VLAN missing on a trunk | Hosts in one VLAN cannot cross switches | `show interfaces trunk` (allowed / forwarding lists) |
| Host's gateway wrong | Same-subnet pings work, other subnets fail | `ipconfig /all`; **extended ping** from the far side |
| Route missing on the return path | Ping from the router works, from the LAN fails | **Extended ping** with `source`; `show ip route` on each hop |
| Routing loop | `&` / TTL expired; traceroute repeats hops | **traceroute**, `show ip route <dest>` |
| ACL blocking | `U` or `!A`, or silence for one protocol only | `show access-lists` counters (Chapter 20), capture |
| Application not listening | Ping works, TCP gets RST | **Packet capture** |
| Loop / broadcast storm | High CPU, MAC flapping | `show processes cpu sorted`, `show logging` (`MACFLAP`) |

### 15.10 Packet Tracer Lab 15.1 — Four trouble tickets (Advanced)

**Objective:** 2.4. **Start from:** your Lab 14.1 file (Figure 14.4). **Host ports** (from earlier labs): PC1 SW1 Fa0/1, PC2 SW1 Fa0/2, PC3 SW2 Fa0/1, Printer1 SW2 Fa0/2.

Ask a classmate to apply the faults below without telling you which ticket each belongs to, or apply them yourself, close the file, and come back the next day. Then work each ticket with the method in 15.2 and record: symptom, the commands you ran, the evidence, the cause, the fix and the verification.

```
! Fault set for Lab 15.1 (instructor or classmate only)
! SW2
interface fastethernet0/1
 switchport access vlan 30
! Printer1 (Desktop > IP Configuration): default gateway 10.10.40.254
! R1
no ip route 10.10.0.0 255.255.0.0 10.0.0.2
ip route 10.10.0.0 255.255.255.0 10.0.0.2
! SW1: no shutdown Fa0/3, then cable a spare 2960 switch to it
```

**The tickets, as users report them**

1. *"PC3 has a strange address and cannot reach the file server in VLAN 10."*
2. *"Nobody in Staff can print, but the printer's own screen says its network is fine."*
3. *"Since this morning nobody reaches the Internet, and new PCs get no address at all."*
4. *"The network port in the meeting room is dead."*

**Simulation-mode capture task:** before fixing ticket 2, switch to Simulation, filter on ARP and ICMP, and ping Printer1 from PC1. Open the Echo Request at Printer1 and then Printer1's outgoing frame: note which MAC address Printer1 tries to resolve (its gateway, 10.10.40.254, which nothing owns).

**Answer key (hide until done)**

| Ticket | Evidence | Cause | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | PC3 `ipconfig` shows 10.10.30.x; SW2 `show vlan brief` lists Fa0/1 in VLAN 30 | Access VLAN wrong | `switchport access vlan 10`, then renew PC3 |
| 2 | From DSW2: `ping 10.10.40.11` succeeds, `ping 10.10.40.11 source vlan 10` fails; Printer1 settings show gateway .254 | Printer's default gateway wrong, so it cannot answer other subnets | Gateway 10.10.40.1 |
| 3 | PC1 `tracert 192.0.2.8` stops after hop 1 (`* * *` from hop 2); R1 `show ip route 10.10.10.11` matches only the default route to the ISP; DHCP renewals fail because R1's Offers to giaddr 10.10.10.2 also follow that default | R1 lost its route back to most campus subnets (/24 covers only 10.10.0.0–10.10.0.255) | Restore `ip route 10.10.0.0 255.255.0.0 10.0.0.2` |
| 4 | SW1 `show logging`: `%SPANTREE-2-BLOCK_BPDUGUARD` and `%PM-4-ERR_DISABLE` on Fa0/3; `show interfaces status` shows err-disabled | BPDU guard (Lab 9.1) stopped a user's switch | Remove the switch, `shutdown` / `no shutdown` Fa0/3 |

Ticket 3 is the instructive one: the forward path works (hop 1 answers), so the clue is **which device cannot route back**. Extended ping from R1 sourced from 10.0.0.1 to 10.10.10.11 fails the same way.

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] Each ticket documented with symptom, commands, evidence, cause, fix, verification
- [ ] Extended ping used to prove ticket 2 without touching the printer first
- [ ] Traceroute used to locate ticket 3
- [ ] Logs used to explain ticket 4
- [ ] Simulation-mode capture shows the printer ARPing for the wrong gateway

### 15.11 Lab 15.2 — Tool drills (Intermediate)

On the repaired Lab 14.1 network:

1. **Ping symbols.** From PC1, `ping 10.10.77.1` (an address that exists nowhere). Explain the result. *(DSW1 sends it to R1, R1 to the ISP by its default route, and the ISP back to R1 by its 10.0.0.0/8 route: a loop, so PC1 reports* `TTL expired in transit`*; IOS would show* `&`*.)*
2. **Traceroute a loop.** From DSW1, `traceroute 10.10.77.1`, and identify the two addresses that repeat.
3. **Return path.** From DSW2, run `ping 192.0.2.8`, then `ping 192.0.2.8 source loopback0`. Predict each result first from R1's routing table: R1 can return traffic only to campus addresses it has routes for (10.10.0.0/16 from Lab 5.1, plus whatever you added in Lab 10.1). If the loopback-sourced ping fails, add one summary route on R1, `ip route 10.0.0.0 255.0.0.0 10.0.0.2`, test again, and explain why R1's connected 10.0.0.0/30 still wins for its own link (longest match, Chapter 10).
4. **Logs.** On SW2, `shutdown` then `no shutdown` Fa0/24, then `show logging | include UPDOWN` and read the four messages (link and line protocol, down then up).
5. **Device health.** On DSW1, read `show version` and record the uptime, IOS version and last reload reason.

### 15.12 Chapter 15 summary notes (exam revision)

- **Method:** define → gather facts → compare with baseline → hypothesis → change one thing → verify → document. **Divide and conquer** starts with ping at Layer 3.
- **Device health:** `show version` (uptime, reload reason, image), `show processes cpu sorted`, `show environment`. Filter with `| include`, `| section`, `| begin`, `| exclude`.
- **Logs:** `show logging`; format `timestamp %FACILITY-SEVERITY-MNEMONIC: text`; `terminal monitor` for SSH sessions. Know UPDOWN, ERR\_DISABLE, BPDUGUARD, MACFLAP, DUPADDR, ADJCHG, STATECHANGE, CONFIG\_I.
- **Ping symbols:** `!` reply, `.` timeout, `U` unreachable, `Q` source quench, `M` cannot fragment, `?` unknown, `&` TTL exceeded. First `.` = ARP.
- **Extended ping:** choose **source**, repeat, size, DF bit, sweep. One line: `ping <dst> source <int|ip> repeat <n> size <n> df-bit`. Proves return routes and MTU.
- **Traceroute:** TTL 1, 2, 3…; Time Exceeded names each hop; IOS/Linux use UDP high ports, Windows ICMP. `*` = timeout; repeating hops = loop; `!A` = ACL; forward path only.
- **Captures:** SPAN, Embedded Packet Capture, Wireshark, Packet Tracer Simulation. SYN→RST = nothing listening; SYN with no reply = filtered or no route back.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "A successful ping from the router proves users can reach it" | It used the router's link address; test with `source` the users' subnet |
| "The first `.` in a ping means packet loss" | Usually the ARP wait |
| "Traceroute shows the return path" | It shows the forward path only |
| "`* * *` means that router is down" | It may simply not reply to probes, or its replies cannot return |
| "A TCP RST means the network is broken" | The SYN arrived; the host refused the port |

### 15.13 Practice questions — Chapter 15

**1. (Ping symbols)** A ping from a router prints `UUUUU`. What does that mean?

**2. (Ping symbols)** Which symbol shows a packet too large to forward with DF set?

A. `!`  B. `.`  C. `M`  D. `&`

**3. (Extended ping)** Why use `ping 10.10.40.11 source vlan 10` on DSW2 instead of a plain ping?

**4. (Traceroute)** Explain how traceroute learns the address of hop 2.

**5. (Traceroute output)** A traceroute shows hops 1 and 2, then `* * *` until the limit. Give two possible causes.

**6. (Traceroute output)** A traceroute alternates between 198.51.100.1 and 198.51.100.2. What is happening?

**7. (Logs)** Interpret `%LINEPROTO-5-UPDOWN: Line protocol on Interface GigabitEthernet1/0/2, changed state to down` — facility, severity and meaning.

**8. (Logs)** You are connected by SSH and see no log messages while you shut an interface. Why, and how do you fix it?

**9. (Capture)** A capture shows `10.10.10.11 → 10.10.40.11 TCP [SYN]` three times, one second apart, with no reply. What does this suggest?

**10. (Capture)** The same SYN is answered by `[RST, ACK]`. What does that tell you?

**11. (Device health)** Which command shows how long a switch has been running and why it last restarted?

**12. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which approaches suit a problem where one web application fails but email and file sharing work?

A. Top-down  B. Bottom-up starting at the cable  C. Compare with a working client  D. Replace the switch  E. Reload the router

**13. (Filtering output)** Write a command that shows only the running-configuration section for interface Vlan10.

**14. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A user cannot reach a server. A network assistant reports: *"Ping from the core router to the server succeeds, so the network is fine and the problem must be the server."* What is missing from that conclusion?

### 15.14 Answer key with explanations

1. Each probe was answered by an **ICMP Destination Unreachable**: a router on the path has no route to the destination (or an ACL rejects it).
2. **C.** `M` = could not fragment. `!` success, `.` timeout, `&` TTL exceeded.
3. A plain ping uses DSW2's VLAN 40 address, which the printer can answer as a neighbor. Sourcing from VLAN 10 tests whether the printer can return traffic to **another subnet** — which depends on its default gateway (and on the routes back).
4. It sends probes with **TTL 2**. Hop 1 decrements to 1 and forwards; hop 2 decrements to 0, drops the probe and returns ICMP **Time Exceeded** from its own address.
5. The next router has no route onward; a firewall or ACL drops the probes or the ICMP replies; the next router cannot route its replies back to the source. (Also: devices configured not to send Time Exceeded.)
6. A **routing loop**: each router points to the other for that destination, and the probe bounces until TTL expires.
7. Facility **LINEPROTO**, severity **5** (notification). Layer 2 line protocol on Gi1/0/2 went down — check the cable, the far end, or keepalive problems.
8. Log messages go to the console by default; a VTY session needs `terminal monitor` (privileged EXEC).
9. The SYN gets no answer: it is **filtered** (ACL or firewall) or lost on the way, or the server's reply has **no route back**. The host may also be down.
10. The network delivered the SYN and the host replied: the host is **reachable but nothing is listening** on that port (service stopped, wrong port).
11. `show version` (uptime and last reload reason).
12. **A and C.** Lower layers clearly work for other applications, so start at the application; comparing with a working client isolates client-specific settings. D and E are random changes.
13. `show running-config | section interface Vlan10`.
14. The ping came from the **router's own address**, not from the user's subnet, so it proves neither the user's path nor the **return route** to the user's subnet, nor that the application port is reachable. Test from (or sourced from) the user's subnet, then check the port with a capture or a TCP test.

## Chapter 16 — Wireless principles

**Objective covered:** **1.5 Describe wireless principles** — **1.5.a** band and channel selection, **1.5.b** RF characteristics, **1.5.c** security protocols, **1.5.d** cause of interference. This is a *describe* objective: you must explain these ideas and apply them to scenarios, not configure radios from the CLI. Chapter 7 covered the switch ports that APs and controllers plug into.

### 16.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Explain how an 802.11 WLAN is organised (AP, SSID, BSSID, BSS, ESS) and compare the 802.11 standards.
2. Choose bands, channels and channel widths for a set of access points.
3. Explain RF terms — dBm, RSSI, SNR, attenuation, reflection, multipath — and use them to judge signal quality.
4. Compare WEP, WPA, WPA2 and WPA3, Personal and Enterprise modes, and 802.1X.
5. Identify common causes of wireless interference and their symptoms.
6. Build a secured WLAN with a sensible channel plan in Packet Tracer.

### 16.2 802.11 basics

A **wireless LAN (WLAN)** carries Ethernet-like frames over radio. The **access point (AP)** is the bridge between the radio and the wired network.

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| **SSID** | The network name users see (up to 32 characters), for example *Staff-WiFi* |
| **BSS** (basic service set) | One AP radio and the clients associated to it |
| **BSSID** | The MAC address of that AP radio — identifies the BSS |
| **ESS** (extended service set) | Several APs advertising the same SSID on the same network, so clients **roam** between them |
| **IBSS** (ad hoc) | Clients talking directly with no AP |
| **Association** | A client joining a BSS (after authentication) |
| **Beacon** | Frame an AP broadcasts about 10 times a second to advertise its SSIDs and capabilities |

**Wi-Fi is half duplex and shared.** Only one station can transmit on a channel at a time, so 802.11 uses **CSMA/CA** (collision *avoidance*): listen before sending, wait a random back-off, and expect an acknowledgment for every unicast frame. More clients and more APs on the same channel mean more waiting.

| Standard | Wi-Fi name | Bands | Maximum data rate (theoretical) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 802.11b | — | 2.4 GHz | 11 Mb/s |
| 802.11a | — | 5 GHz | 54 Mb/s |
| 802.11g | — | 2.4 GHz | 54 Mb/s |
| 802.11n | Wi-Fi 4 | 2.4 and 5 GHz | 600 Mb/s |
| 802.11ac | Wi-Fi 5 | 5 GHz | about 6.9 Gb/s |
| 802.11ax | Wi-Fi 6 / 6E (6E adds 6 GHz) | 2.4, 5 and 6 GHz | about 9.6 Gb/s |
| 802.11be | Wi-Fi 7 | 2.4, 5 and 6 GHz | about 46 Gb/s |

Real throughput is far lower — often less than half — because of the shared medium, protocol overhead and distance. Faster standards rely on wider channels, more **spatial streams** (MIMO: several antennas sending different data at once) and denser modulation, which need a strong, clean signal.

### 16.3 Bands and channels (1.5.a)

A **band** is a range of frequencies; a **channel** is a slice of a band that one BSS uses. Which channels you may use, and at what power, is set by each country's regulator — always check local rules (in Nepal, the Nepal Telecommunications Authority).

|  | 2.4 GHz | 5 GHz | 6 GHz (Wi-Fi 6E / 7) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Channels | 1–13 in most countries (1–11 in the USA), 5 MHz apart, each about 20–22 MHz wide | About 25 non-overlapping 20 MHz channels (36–165), grouped into U-NII bands | Up to 59 non-overlapping 20 MHz channels where the full band is allowed |
| **Non-overlapping** | Only **1, 6 and 11** | All of them at 20 MHz | All of them |
| Range / penetration | Longest, best through walls | Shorter | Shortest |
| Interference | Crowded: neighbours, Bluetooth, microwave ovens | Much less; some channels need **DFS** (avoid radar) | Very little; only new devices; **WPA3 required** |
| Typical use | Coverage, old and IoT devices | Most enterprise traffic | High-density, high-speed areas |

**Why 1, 6 and 11?** A 2.4 GHz channel is about 22 MHz wide but channels are only 5 MHz apart, so neighbouring channel numbers overlap (Figure 16.1). Channels five apart do not overlap, which leaves three usable channels in most of the world.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch16/fig-16-01-24ghz-channel-overlap.svg" alt="Figure 16.1 — 2.4 GHz channels 1–13 and why only 1, 6 and 11 avoid overlap" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 16.1</b> — 2.4 GHz channels 1–13 and why only 1, 6 and 11 avoid overlap</sub></p>

Figure 16.1 draws every 2.4 GHz channel to scale on one frequency axis. The three highlighted arcs never touch each other, while each grey arc overlaps one or two of them.

**Channel width.** 802.11n and later can **bond** adjacent 20 MHz channels into 40, 80, 160 (and on Wi-Fi 7, 320) MHz channels. Wider channels carry more data but leave fewer separate channels for neighbouring APs. In 2.4 GHz use 20 MHz only; in busy 5 GHz offices 20 or 40 MHz is common; 6 GHz has room for 80 MHz and wider.

**Choosing channels for several APs (Figure 16.2):**

1. Neighbouring APs use **different, non-overlapping** channels; reuse a channel only for APs far enough apart not to hear each other strongly.
2. On 2.4 GHz, alternate 1, 6, 11 in a honeycomb pattern.
3. Set **transmit power** so cells overlap by roughly 15–20 % for roaming — not more; louder is not better.
4. Prefer 5 GHz (and 6 GHz) for clients; consider turning off some 2.4 GHz radios in dense areas.
5. In a controller-based WLAN, **Radio Resource Management (RRM)** on the WLC chooses channels and power automatically and adapts to interference.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch16/fig-16-02-channel-reuse-plan.svg" alt="Figure 16.2 — A 2.4 GHz channel reuse plan with channels 1, 6 and 11" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 16.2</b> — A 2.4 GHz channel reuse plan with channels 1, 6 and 11</sub></p>

Figure 16.2 is the honeycomb from step 2: each colour is one channel, and wherever two cells overlap they carry different channels, so neighbours never take turns on the same frequency.

Two failures of channel planning are tested often: **co-channel interference** (APs on the *same* channel hear each other and must take turns — capacity drops, but nothing is corrupted) and **adjacent-channel interference** (APs on *overlapping* channels such as 1 and 3 corrupt each other's frames — worse).

### 16.4 RF characteristics (1.5.b)

Radio frequency (RF) signals are electromagnetic waves.

| Term | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| **Frequency** | Cycles per second, in hertz (Hz). Wi-Fi uses 2.4, 5 and 6 GHz |
| **Wavelength** | Length of one cycle: about 12.5 cm at 2.4 GHz, 6 cm at 5 GHz. Shorter waves lose strength faster and pass through obstacles less well |
| **Amplitude** | Signal strength |
| **Modulation** | How data is encoded on the wave; denser modulation (more bits per symbol) needs a cleaner signal |

**Power and decibels.** Wireless power is tiny and spans a huge range, so it is expressed in **dBm** — decibels relative to 1 milliwatt.

| Rule | Example |
| --- | --- |
| 0 dBm = 1 mW | — |
| **+3 dB doubles** the power, −3 dB halves it | 20 dBm = 100 mW, 17 dBm = 50 mW |
| **+10 dB is ten times** the power, −10 dB a tenth | 10 dBm = 10 mW, −10 dBm = 0.1 mW |
| Received Wi-Fi signals are negative dBm | −65 dBm is **stronger** than −80 dBm |

**Signal quality measures:**

| Measure | Meaning | Typical guidance |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **RSSI** (received signal strength) | How strong the AP's signal is at the client, in dBm | −67 dBm or better for voice and video; −70 dBm adequate for data; −80 dBm poor |
| **Noise floor** | Background RF energy on the channel | Often about −90 to −95 dBm |
| **SNR** (signal-to-noise ratio) | Signal minus noise, in dB | 25 dB or more good; below 15 dB unreliable |

Example: RSSI −65 dBm with a noise floor of −92 dBm gives **SNR = 27 dB** — good. The same RSSI next to a microwave oven raising the noise to −75 dBm gives only 10 dB, and the connection struggles even though the "bars" look full.

**How signals behave on the way:**

| Effect | What happens | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Free-space path loss | Signal weakens with distance (faster at higher frequency) | 5 GHz cells are smaller than 2.4 GHz cells |
| **Absorption** | Material soaks up energy | Water, people, concrete walls |
| **Reflection** | Signal bounces off smooth surfaces | Metal cabinets, glass, lift shafts |
| Refraction | Signal bends passing between materials | Air layers, glass |
| Scattering | Signal spreads in many directions | Rough surfaces, foliage, dust |
| Diffraction | Signal bends around an obstacle, leaving an RF shadow behind it | Corners, pillars |
| **Multipath** | Copies arrive by different paths at slightly different times | Reflections indoors; MIMO turns it into an advantage, older radios suffer |

**Antennas and EIRP.** An **omnidirectional** antenna radiates in all horizontal directions (ceiling APs); a **directional** antenna (patch, Yagi, dish) focuses energy one way for corridors, warehouses or point-to-point bridges. Antenna **gain** is in **dBi**. The power that actually leaves the antenna is **EIRP = transmitter power + antenna gain − cable loss**: for example 17 dBm + 6 dBi − 2 dB = 21 dBm. Regulators limit EIRP, not just transmitter power.

### 16.5 Wireless security protocols (1.5.c)

Radio reaches the car park and the next building, so a WLAN must **authenticate** who joins and **encrypt** what they send.

| Protocol | Year | Encryption | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Open | — | None | Guest only, with a captive portal; data visible to anyone |
| **WEP** | 1999 | RC4, static key | **Broken** — cracked in minutes; never use |
| **WPA** | 2003 | **TKIP** (RC4-based) | Interim fix; deprecated |
| **WPA2** | 2004 | **AES-CCMP** | Still widely used; secure with a strong passphrase |
| **WPA3** | 2018 | AES-CCMP or **GCMP-256** (192-bit Enterprise mode) | Current; **SAE** replaces PSK; **PMF** (protected management frames) mandatory; required on 6 GHz |

**Personal versus Enterprise:**

|  | Personal | Enterprise |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credentials | One shared passphrase for everyone (WPA2-**PSK**, WPA3-**SAE**) | Each user or device has its own credentials |
| Authentication | Done by the AP itself | **802.1X / EAP** with a **RADIUS** server (for example Cisco ISE) |
| When someone leaves | Change the passphrase on every device | Disable one account |
| Typical use | Home, small office, IoT | Organisations |

**WPA3-Personal (SAE)** fixes WPA2-PSK's weakness: with PSK, an attacker who captures the four-way handshake can try passwords offline; SAE ("Dragonfly") forces every guess to be an online exchange and gives **forward secrecy**. **OWE (Enhanced Open)** encrypts open guest networks without a password.

**802.1X roles** (Figure 16.3): the **supplicant** (client software), the **authenticator** (the AP, or the WLC in a controller-based network) and the **authentication server** (RADIUS). Common EAP methods: **EAP-TLS** (certificates on both sides — strongest), **PEAP** (server certificate plus the user's password inside a TLS tunnel), and Cisco's **EAP-FAST**. The same RADIUS server can also place the user in a VLAN, which links to AAA in Chapter 18.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch16/fig-16-03-8021x-flow.svg" alt="Figure 16.3 — WPA2/WPA3-Enterprise: 802.1X authentication between supplicant, authenticator and RADIUS server" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 16.3</b> — WPA2/WPA3-Enterprise: 802.1X authentication between supplicant, authenticator and RADIUS server</sub></p>

Figure 16.3 runs downward in time. The green Access-Accept is the decision point; only after it and the four-way handshake does the thick blue line of encrypted user traffic start.

**Not security:** hiding the SSID (it still appears in client probes) and MAC filtering (MAC addresses are easily copied) only add inconvenience.

### 16.6 Causes of interference (1.5.d)

| Cause | Effect | Remedy |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Co-channel interference (CCI)** — nearby APs on the same channel | APs and clients take turns; capacity drops | Better channel plan, lower power, fewer 2.4 GHz radios |
| **Adjacent-channel interference (ACI)** — overlapping channels (for example 1 and 3) | Corrupted frames, retries | Only 1, 6, 11 in 2.4 GHz; avoid overlapping bonded channels |
| **Non-Wi-Fi devices** in 2.4 GHz: microwave ovens (about 2.45 GHz), Bluetooth, cordless phones, baby monitors, wireless cameras | Noise floor rises, SNR falls | Move to 5/6 GHz; move or replace the device |
| **Radar** on 5 GHz DFS channels | AP must leave the channel at once; clients briefly disconnected | Avoid DFS channels near airports or weather radar |
| **Physical obstacles** — metal, concrete, water, lift shafts | Absorption, reflection, dead spots | Survey and place APs accordingly |
| **Too much transmit power** | Large overlapping cells, more CCI, "sticky" clients | Lower power, more APs |
| **Too many SSIDs** | Each SSID sends beacons; airtime wasted | Keep to about three or four SSIDs |
| **Rogue or neighbour APs** | Extra CCI; possible security risk | Rogue detection on the WLC |
| **Hidden node** — two clients cannot hear each other but both reach the AP | Collisions at the AP | Better AP placement; RTS/CTS |

**Symptoms** users report: slow Wi-Fi despite "full bars" (good RSSI, poor SNR), frequent drops, low data rates and high retry counts. **Tools:** the client's RSSI and SNR readings, WLC dashboards and RRM reports, a **spectrum analyzer** to see non-Wi-Fi sources (Cisco CleanAir does this in the AP), and a site survey.

### 16.7 Packet Tracer Lab 16.1 — A secure two-AP WLAN with a channel plan (Beginner–Intermediate)

**Objectives:** 1.5.a, 1.5.c (1.5.b and 1.5.d as observation and discussion). **New file.** **You will:** build an ESS of two APs with one SSID on non-overlapping channels, secure it with WPA2-Personal (AES), connect laptops by DHCP, and test what happens with wrong security settings.

**Packet Tracer limits:** Packet Tracer shows association and a simple signal indicator, but it does **not** model RF interference, SNR or channel overlap realistically. Channel planning here is configuration practice; 16.3 and 16.6 explain the real-world effects.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch16/fig-16-04-lab-16-1-topology.svg" alt="Figure 16.4 — Lab 16.1 topology: an ESS of two APs on channels 1 and 6" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 16.4</b> — Lab 16.1 topology: an ESS of two APs on channels 1 and 6</sub></p>

In Figure 16.4 the wired part is at the top and the two radio cells below; where the cells overlap, a laptop can roam between APs without changing SSID or address.

**Devices:** 1 × ISR4331 (R-W), 1 × 2960 (SW-W), 2 × AccessPoint-PT (AP-A, AP-B), 2 × Laptop-PT (L1, L2).

| Device | Connection | Settings |
| --- | --- | --- |
| R-W | G0/0/0 → SW-W Fa0/24 | 192.168.30.1/24, DHCP pool WLAN |
| AP-A | Port 0 → SW-W Fa0/1 | SSID Staff-WiFi, channel 1, WPA2-PSK, AES |
| AP-B | Port 0 → SW-W Fa0/2 | SSID Staff-WiFi, channel 6, WPA2-PSK, AES |
| L1, L2 | Wireless | WPC300N wireless card, DHCP |

**Step 1 — router and DHCP**

```
enable
configure terminal
hostname R-W
interface gigabitethernet0/0/0
 ip address 192.168.30.1 255.255.255.0
 no shutdown
exit
ip dhcp excluded-address 192.168.30.1 192.168.30.10
ip dhcp pool WLAN
 network 192.168.30.0 255.255.255.0
 default-router 192.168.30.1
end
copy running-config startup-config
```

**Step 2 — access points** (Config tab → Port 1, the wireless port)

1. AP-A: SSID **Staff-WiFi**, Channel **1**, Authentication **WPA2-PSK**, pass phrase `Lab16-Strong-Passphrase`, Encryption **AES**.
2. AP-B: identical, but Channel **6**. Same SSID and security on both APs makes them one ESS, so laptops can roam.

**Step 3 — laptops.** Power off each laptop (Physical tab), remove the wired NIC module, insert **WPC300N**, power on. Then Desktop → PC Wireless → Connect: choose Staff-WiFi, enter the passphrase. (Alternatively Config → Wireless0: SSID, WPA2-PSK, key, DHCP.)

**Verification and expected results**

| Check | How | Expected |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Association | Dashed line from each laptop to an AP | Each laptop associated to the nearest AP |
| Address | Laptop `ipconfig` | 192.168.30.11+, gateway .1 |
| Reachability | Laptop `ping 192.168.30.1` | Success |
| Leases | R-W `show ip dhcp binding` | Two wireless clients |
| Roaming | Drag L1 from near AP-A to near AP-B | Association line moves to AP-B; ping resumes |

**Experiments**

1. **Wrong passphrase:** reconnect L2 with one wrong character. It never associates, and gets no address.
2. **Security mismatch:** set L2 to WPA2-PSK with **TKIP** instead of AES. Association fails: both sides must agree on the cipher.
3. **Personal versus Enterprise (optional; depends on your version):** if AP-A offers **WPA2** (Enterprise), add a Server-PT at 192.168.30.5 with the **AAA** service as a RADIUS server, enter the AP as a client with a shared secret, create user *alice*, and point AP-A at 192.168.30.5. Connect L1 as alice. If the option is missing, use Figure 16.3 instead.
4. **Channel discussion:** change AP-B to channel 2. Packet Tracer still works — explain from Figure 16.1 why a real network would now suffer adjacent-channel interference, and set it back to 6.

**Questions after the lab**

1. Why do both APs use the same SSID but different channels?
2. Which channel would a third AP in between them use?
3. Why is WPA2-PSK with a short passphrase weaker than WPA3-SAE with the same passphrase?

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] Two APs, one SSID, channels 1 and 6, WPA2-PSK AES
- [ ] Both laptops associated, addressed by DHCP and pinging the gateway
- [ ] Roaming observed
- [ ] Wrong passphrase and cipher mismatch reproduced
- [ ] Questions answered (third AP: channel 11)

### 16.8 Chapter 16 summary notes (exam revision)

- **Terms:** SSID (name), BSS (one AP radio + clients), BSSID (AP radio MAC), ESS (many APs, one SSID, roaming), IBSS (ad hoc). Wi-Fi is half duplex, **CSMA/CA**.
- **Standards:** 11b/g 2.4 GHz; 11a 5 GHz; 11n Wi-Fi 4; 11ac Wi-Fi 5 (5 GHz); 11ax Wi-Fi 6/6E (6E adds 6 GHz); 11be Wi-Fi 7.
- **2.4 GHz:** longest range, crowded, only **1, 6, 11** non-overlapping. **5 GHz:** many non-overlapping channels, DFS on some. **6 GHz:** most channels, WPA3 required, new devices only.
- **Channel plan:** neighbours on different non-overlapping channels; 20 MHz in 2.4 GHz; moderate power; RRM automates it on a WLC. CCI = same channel (sharing); ACI = overlapping channels (corruption).
- **RF:** 0 dBm = 1 mW; +3 dB ×2; +10 dB ×10; RSSI −67 dBm for voice; **SNR = signal − noise**, ≥ 25 dB good. Absorption, reflection, refraction, scattering, diffraction, multipath. EIRP = Tx + gain − loss.
- **Security:** WEP broken; WPA = TKIP (deprecated); **WPA2 = AES-CCMP**; **WPA3 = SAE, PMF**, GCMP-256 in 192-bit Enterprise. Personal = shared key; Enterprise = **802.1X/EAP + RADIUS** (supplicant, authenticator, authentication server).
- **Interference:** CCI, ACI, microwaves, Bluetooth, cordless phones, radar (DFS), obstacles, too much power, too many SSIDs.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "−80 dBm is stronger than −60 dBm" | Closer to zero is stronger: −60 dBm is 100 times stronger |
| "Full bars means good Wi-Fi" | Bars show RSSI; poor SNR or congestion still makes it slow |
| "Hidden SSIDs are secure" | The SSID still appears in client probes; use WPA2/WPA3 |
| "More transmit power is always better" | It enlarges cells, increases interference and mismatches clients |
| "Channels 1, 2 and 3 give three separate channels" | They overlap heavily; use 1, 6, 11 |

### 16.9 Practice questions — Chapter 16

**1. (Recall)** Which three 2.4 GHz channels do not overlap?

A. 1, 5, 9  B. 1, 6, 11  C. 2, 7, 12  D. 1, 2, 3

**2. (Concept)** What is the difference between an SSID and a BSSID?

**3. (Calculation)** An AP transmits at 20 dBm. How many milliwatts is that? What is 23 dBm?

**4. (Calculation)** A client measures RSSI −68 dBm and a noise floor of −90 dBm. What is the SNR, and is it adequate?

**5. (Design)** Three APs line a corridor and each cell overlaps the next. Assign 2.4 GHz channels.

**6. (Interference)** Users near the canteen report Wi-Fi drops every lunchtime on 2.4 GHz only. What is the likely cause and remedy?

**7. (Security)** Which protocol uses AES-CCMP and is the minimum acceptable for a new WLAN?

A. WEP  B. WPA (TKIP)  C. WPA2  D. Open with MAC filtering

**8. (Security)** What advantage does WPA3-SAE have over WPA2-PSK?

**9. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which are roles in 802.1X?

A. Supplicant  B. Authenticator  C. Root bridge  D. Relay agent  E. Designated router

**10. (Concept)** Why do 5 GHz cells cover less area than 2.4 GHz cells at the same power?

**11. (Concept)** What is DFS, and which band uses it?

**12. (Interference)** Two neighbouring APs both use channel 6. Name this type of interference and its effect.

**13. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A network assistant suggests: *"Users complain Wi-Fi is slow, so raise every AP to maximum transmit power and use 40 MHz channels on 2.4 GHz."* Is that good advice?

### 16.10 Answer key with explanations

1. **B.** Channels 5 MHz apart overlap; channels 1, 6 and 11 are 25 MHz apart.
2. The **SSID** is the network name, shared by every AP in an ESS. The **BSSID** is the MAC address of one AP radio, unique to each BSS.
3. 20 dBm = **100 mW** (+10 dB ×10 twice from 1 mW). 23 dBm = **200 mW** (+3 dB doubles).
4. SNR = −68 − (−90) = **22 dB** — usable for data but below the 25 dB often wanted for voice and video.
5. **1, 6, 11** in turn (or 1, 6, 1 if only two channels were needed); neighbours must differ.
6. A **microwave oven** (about 2.45 GHz) raising the noise floor. Move users to 5 GHz, or move or shield the AP; a spectrum analyzer confirms it.
7. **C.** WEP is broken, WPA/TKIP is deprecated, and MAC filtering is not encryption.
8. SAE resists **offline dictionary attacks** (each password guess needs a live exchange) and provides **forward secrecy**; WPA3 also requires protected management frames.
9. **A and B** (the third is the authentication server). Root bridge, relay agent and DR belong to STP, DHCP and OSPF.
10. Higher frequency signals lose strength faster with distance (path loss) and are absorbed more by walls.
11. **Dynamic Frequency Selection**: on some **5 GHz** channels APs must check for radar and leave the channel if radar appears.
12. **Co-channel interference**: they must share airtime and take turns, so capacity drops (frames are not corrupted).
13. **No.** Maximum power enlarges cells and increases co-channel interference, and 40 MHz in 2.4 GHz leaves only one usable non-overlapping channel. Investigate SNR, channel plan and client load; use 5/6 GHz for capacity.

### 16.11 Chapter 16 figure checklist

All 4 figures were rendered, embedded beside the text they explain, and checked visually after rendering.
