## Chapter 17 — Client connectivity on Windows, macOS and Linux

**Objective covered:** **1.6 Troubleshoot wired and wireless client connectivity (IP configuration, network reachability, and wireless security parameters on Windows, MacOS, and Linux).** Earlier chapters fixed the network side; this chapter starts from the user's computer, because many "network" tickets are solved there.

### 17.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Check a client's link, IP address, mask, gateway and DNS on Windows, macOS and Linux.
2. Recognise broken configurations from command output: APIPA, wrong mask, wrong gateway, missing DNS, duplicate address, IPv6 without a global address.
3. Test reachability in a logical order and say what each failure means.
4. Read a Wi-Fi client's SSID, BSSID, channel, signal and security settings on each operating system.
5. Match client wireless security settings to the WLAN and fix mismatches.

### 17.2 A client-side method

Work outward from the client, one question at a time (Figure 17.1):

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch17/fig-17-01-client-check-ladder.svg" alt="Figure 17.1 — Client connectivity checks in order, with the usual cause when each one fails" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 17.1</b> — Client connectivity checks in order, with the usual cause when each one fails</sub></p>

Read Figure 17.1 left to right. The highlighted last step is where most users start complaining ("the website is down"), but you start at the first box.

1. **Link** — is the cable connected or the Wi-Fi associated to the right SSID?
2. **Address** — does the client have a valid address and mask for this network (not 169.254.x.x)?
3. **Gateway** — is a default gateway set, in the same subnet, and does it answer?
4. **Remote IP** — can it reach an address beyond the gateway?
5. **Names** — is a DNS server set, and do names resolve (Chapter 22)?
6. **Application** — does the service itself answer (port, proxy, firewall on the client)?

Stop at the first "no" — everything after it depends on it.

### 17.3 Commands on each operating system

All commands below run in a terminal: **Command Prompt or PowerShell** on Windows, **Terminal** on macOS and Linux. Commands that change settings need administrator rights (Run as administrator, or `sudo`). Linux distributions differ; the commands shown use the standard `iproute2` tools and NetworkManager (`nmcli`), present on Ubuntu and most desktops.

| Task | Windows | macOS | Linux |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Addresses, mask, gateway | `ipconfig`, `ipconfig /all` | `ifconfig en0`, `networksetup -getinfo "Wi-Fi"` | `ip address show` (`ip a`) |
| Routing table / default gateway | `route print` | `netstat -rn` | `ip route show` |
| DNS servers | `ipconfig /all` | `scutil --dns` | `resolvectl status` (or `cat /etc/resolv.conf`) |
| Release / renew DHCP | `ipconfig /release`, `ipconfig /renew` | `sudo ipconfig set en0 DHCP` (or Renew DHCP Lease in Settings) | `sudo dhclient -r` then `sudo dhclient`, or `nmcli connection down <name>` / `up <name>` |
| ARP / neighbor cache | `arp -a` | `arp -a` | `ip neigh` |
| Ping (4 packets) | `ping 10.10.10.1` (4 by default) | `ping -c 4 10.10.10.1` (continuous without `-c`) | `ping -c 4 10.10.10.1` |
| Trace the path | `tracert` (ICMP) | `traceroute` (UDP) | `traceroute` or `tracepath` |
| Name lookup | `nslookup host` | `nslookup host`, `dig host` | `dig host`, `nslookup host` |
| Flush DNS cache | `ipconfig /flushdns` | `sudo dscacheutil -flushcache; sudo killall -HUP mDNSResponder` | `resolvectl flush-caches` |
| Link speed / state | `Get-NetAdapter` (PowerShell) | `ifconfig en0` (media, status lines) | `ethtool eth0`, `ip link` |
| Wi-Fi status | `netsh wlan show interfaces` | Option-click the Wi-Fi icon, or `sudo wdutil info` | `nmcli device wifi list`, `iw dev wlan0 link` |

**Interface names** differ: Windows uses names like *Ethernet* and *Wi-Fi*; macOS uses `en0` (often Wi-Fi on laptops) and `en1`; Linux uses names like `eth0`, `enp3s0` and `wlan0`/`wlp2s0`. Use the name your system shows. On macOS the old `airport` command-line tool has been removed in recent releases; use `wdutil` or the Option-click menu.

### 17.4 Reading a client's IP configuration

The same healthy laptop in VLAN 30 (Lab 14.1 addressing), as each operating system shows it:

```
C:\> ipconfig /all
Wireless LAN adapter Wi-Fi:
   Connection-specific DNS Suffix  . : campus.example
   Physical Address. . . . . . . . . : 3C-A9-F4-12-34-56
   DHCP Enabled. . . . . . . . . . . : Yes
   Link-local IPv6 Address . . . . . : fe80::1c2b:3d4e:5f60:7a8b%12(Preferred)
   IPv4 Address. . . . . . . . . . . : 10.10.30.25(Preferred)
   Subnet Mask . . . . . . . . . . . : 255.255.255.0
   Lease Obtained. . . . . . . . . . : Thursday, 15 October 2026 10:14:02
   Lease Expires . . . . . . . . . . : Friday, 16 October 2026 10:14:02
   Default Gateway . . . . . . . . . : 10.10.30.1
   DHCP Server . . . . . . . . . . . : 10.0.0.1
   DNS Servers . . . . . . . . . . . : 10.10.99.53
```

```
$ ip -4 address show wlp2s0                        # Linux
3: wlp2s0: <BROADCAST,MULTICAST,UP,LOWER_UP> mtu 1500 state UP
    inet 10.10.30.26/24 brd 10.10.30.255 scope global dynamic wlp2s0
       valid_lft 86012sec preferred_lft 86012sec
$ ip route
default via 10.10.30.1 dev wlp2s0 proto dhcp metric 600
10.10.30.0/24 dev wlp2s0 proto kernel scope link src 10.10.30.26
```

```
$ ifconfig en0                                     # macOS
en0: flags=8863<UP,BROADCAST,SMART,RUNNING,SIMPLEX,MULTICAST> mtu 1500
	ether 8c:85:90:ab:cd:ef
	inet 10.10.30.27 netmask 0xffffff00 broadcast 10.10.30.255
	status: active
$ netstat -rn | grep default
default            10.10.30.1         UGScg           en0
```

Three things to notice: Linux shows the mask as a **prefix** (/24), macOS as **hexadecimal** (0xffffff00 = 255.255.255.0), Windows as dotted decimal. `dynamic` (Linux) and `DHCP Enabled: Yes` (Windows) mean the address came from DHCP. (Exact layout varies with the OS version.)

**Broken configurations and what they mean:**

| You see | Meaning | Next step |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 169.254.x.x / 255.255.0.0, no gateway | No DHCP server answered (APIPA) | Chapter 14 flowchart; check link and VLAN first |
| Address correct, mask 255.255.0.0 instead of /24 | Client thinks other subnets are local and ARPs for them instead of using the gateway (Figure 17.2); it works only if the router answers with proxy ARP, which hides the fault | Fix the mask (static entry or DHCP pool) |
| Gateway 10.10.31.1 with address 10.10.30.25/24 | Gateway outside the client's subnet: unreachable | Correct the gateway |
| `(Duplicate)` after the address (Windows), or a log about a conflict | Another device uses the same address | Find it via ARP and MAC tables; fix static addresses or DHCP exclusions |
| No DNS servers, or a public one inside a private network | Names fail although pings by IP work | Fix DHCP `dns-server` or the static setting |
| Only an `fe80::` IPv6 address | No Router Advertisements: no global IPv6 address (Chapter 3) | Check `ipv6 unicast-routing` and the gateway interface |
| 192.168.1.x on a campus laptop | A static address left over from home | Switch the adapter back to DHCP |

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch17/fig-17-02-mask-decides-delivery.svg" alt="Figure 17.2 — How a client's mask decides between its gateway and direct delivery" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 17.2</b> — How a client's mask decides between its gateway and direct delivery</sub></p>

Figure 17.2 follows the same packet with two masks. With /24 (left) the client correctly uses its gateway; with /16 (right) it wrongly expects the printer on its own segment. The green and red boxes are the outcomes.

### 17.5 The reachability ladder

Test in this order, one rung at a time:

1. `ping 127.0.0.1` (or `ping ::1`) — the TCP/IP stack works. A failure means a damaged OS network stack, rare.
2. `ping <own address>` — the adapter has that address.
3. `ping <default gateway>` — Layer 1–3 to the first hop: link, VLAN, mask, ARP. Failure: back to the link, VLAN or Wi-Fi association.
4. `ping <remote IP>`, for example 192.0.2.8 — routing beyond the gateway **and back**. Failure with step 3 working: a routing, ACL or NAT problem past the gateway (Chapter 15).
5. `ping <name>`, for example `ping www.campus.example` — DNS. A failure here while step 4 works points to DNS (Chapter 22).
6. Test the application: open the page, `curl`, or `Test-NetConnection server -Port 443` (PowerShell).

**Firewalls can lie.** Windows Defender Firewall blocks incoming ICMP by default on public networks, so a PC that does not answer ping may still be fine. Test reachability from the PC outward, or use an application-level test.

### 17.6 Wireless client status and security parameters

A Wi-Fi client connects only when **every** security setting matches the WLAN (Chapter 16):

| Setting | Must match | Typical mistake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| SSID | Exact name (case-sensitive) | Joining *staff-wifi* or a look-alike rogue SSID |
| Security type | WPA2/WPA3, Personal or Enterprise | Old profile set to WPA2-Personal after the WLAN moved to Enterprise |
| Cipher | AES (CCMP); GCMP for WPA3 192-bit | Client forced to TKIP |
| Passphrase (Personal) | Exact | Typo, or an old saved password after a change |
| EAP method and credentials (Enterprise) | PEAP or EAP-TLS, correct username/password or certificate | Expired certificate; wrong domain in the username |
| Server certificate trust (Enterprise) | Client trusts the RADIUS server's certificate | User clicks through warnings — or the client refuses an untrusted server |
| Capabilities | Band and standard the client supports | Old laptop cannot see a 6 GHz-only or WPA3-only SSID |
| Protected management frames | Required for WPA3 | Old driver without PMF |

**Reading Wi-Fi status on each OS:**

```
C:\> netsh wlan show interfaces                   (Windows)
    State                  : connected
    SSID                   : Staff-WiFi
    BSSID                  : 70:7d:b9:aa:bb:01
    Radio type             : 802.11ax
    Authentication         : WPA2-Enterprise
    Cipher                 : CCMP
    Band                   : 5 GHz
    Channel                : 36
    Receive rate (Mbps)    : 573.5
    Signal                 : 88%
    Profile                : Staff-WiFi
```

```
$ nmcli -f IN-USE,BSSID,SSID,CHAN,SIGNAL,SECURITY device wifi list     (Linux)
IN-USE  BSSID              SSID        CHAN  SIGNAL  SECURITY
*       70:7D:B9:AA:BB:01  Staff-WiFi  36    82      WPA2 802.1X
        70:7D:B9:AA:BB:11  Staff-WiFi  1     64      WPA2 802.1X
        A4:2B:B0:11:22:33  Guest       6     71      --
$ iw dev wlp2s0 link
Connected to 70:7d:b9:aa:bb:01 (on wlp2s0)
        SSID: Staff-WiFi
        freq: 5180
        signal: -56 dBm
```

```
$ sudo wdutil info                                  (macOS, excerpt)
    SSID     : Staff-WiFi
    RSSI     : -56 dBm
    Noise    : -92 dBm
    Channel  : 5g36/80
    Security : WPA2 Enterprise
```

What these tell you: which **SSID** and **AP radio (BSSID)** the client chose, the **band and channel**, signal strength (Windows shows a **percentage**, Linux and macOS **dBm**), the **security type and cipher** actually negotiated, and the data rate. macOS also gives the noise floor, so you can work out SNR directly: −56 − (−92) = 36 dB. Field names and layout differ between OS versions; macOS may hide the SSID unless the terminal has location permission.

**Two modern client behaviours that surprise people:**

- **Private (random) MAC addresses.** Windows, macOS, iOS and Android can use a different random MAC per network. A DHCP reservation or MAC-based rule written for the hardware MAC then fails. Check the address the client actually uses (`ipconfig /all`, `ip link`) or turn the feature off for that network.
- **Captive portals.** Guest networks often intercept web traffic until the user accepts terms. The client has an address and DNS, but everything else seems blocked until the portal page is completed.

### 17.7 Troubleshooting client connectivity

| Symptom | Likely cause | Check / fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Wi-Fi "can't connect to this network" | Wrong passphrase or security type, old saved profile | Forget the network, reconnect; compare security settings |
| Enterprise Wi-Fi fails for one user | Wrong credentials, expired certificate, untrusted server certificate | RADIUS logs (Chapter 18); client certificate store |
| Connected but 169.254.x.x | DHCP not reaching the client (VLAN, relay, pool) | Chapter 14; `ipconfig /renew` |
| Connected, valid address, nothing off-subnet | Wrong or missing gateway, wrong mask | `ipconfig /all` / `ip route`; Figure 17.2 |
| Pings by IP work, names fail | No or wrong DNS server | `nslookup`; DNS settings (Chapter 22) |
| Slow or dropping Wi-Fi | Low SNR, interference, far from the AP, sticky client on a distant BSSID | Signal/noise readings; channel plan (Chapter 16) |
| Works on Wi-Fi, not on cable (or reverse) | Different VLAN per access method; static IP on one adapter | Compare both adapters' settings |
| Works at home, not on campus | Static address, DNS or proxy left configured | Switch back to DHCP/automatic |
| One device blocked, others fine | MAC-based filter or reservation vs private MAC | Use the device's current MAC |
| Remote PC "down" but users work | Host firewall blocks ICMP | Test with an application port, or allow ICMP |

| Interface name |  |  |
| IPv4 address and mask (in the OS's own format) |  |  |
| Default gateway |  |  |
| DNS servers |  |  |

**Part B — Packet Tracer break-fix.** On a copy of your Lab 14.1 file, change the PCs' IP settings (Desktop → IP Configuration) as below, then diagnose each from the PC's Command Prompt only (`ipconfig /all`, `ping`, `tracert`, `arp -a`):

| PC | Change | Your diagnosis |
| --- | --- | --- |
| PC1 | Static 10.10.10.50, mask **255.255.0.0**, gateway 10.10.10.1 |  |
| PC2 | Static 10.10.30.50/24, gateway **10.10.31.1** |  |

**Part C — Wireless parameters.** In your Lab 16.1 file, on L2 type the SSID as `staff-wifi` (lower case) and connect; then correct the SSID but choose **WPA-PSK** instead of WPA2-PSK. Record what each mistake looks like from the laptop.

**Answer key (hide until done)**

| Case | What you see | Explanation | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PC1 /16 | Pings to PC3 (same VLAN) work; pings to 10.10.40.11 may **still work**; `arp -a` shows an entry for 10.10.40.11 with the **gateway's MAC** | Proxy ARP on the SVIs answered for the printer, hiding the wrong mask | Mask 255.255.255.0; optionally test with `no ip proxy-arp` on the VLAN 10 SVIs, if your version accepts it |
| PC2 gateway | Pings inside VLAN 30 work, everything else fails | Gateway outside the subnet | Gateway 10.10.30.1 |
| PC3 home settings | Nothing works, not even the gateway | Wrong subnet entirely | Back to DHCP |
| L2 lower-case SSID | No association | SSIDs are case-sensitive | `Staff-WiFi` |
| L2 WPA-PSK | No association | Security type must match (WPA2) | WPA2-PSK, AES |

### 17.9 Chapter 17 summary notes (exam revision)

- **Order:** link → address → gateway → remote IP → names → application. Stop at the first failure.
- **IP settings:** Windows `ipconfig /all`; macOS `ifconfig en0`, `networksetup -getinfo`; Linux `ip address`, `ip route`, `resolvectl status`. Masks appear as dotted decimal (Windows), **hex** (macOS 0xffffff00) or **prefix** (Linux /24).
- **Renew:** Windows `ipconfig /release` + `/renew`; macOS `sudo ipconfig set en0 DHCP`; Linux `dhclient` or `nmcli connection down/up`.
- **Paths:** `tracert` (Windows, ICMP), `traceroute` (macOS/Linux, UDP). macOS/Linux `ping` is continuous unless `-c`.
- **Wi-Fi status:** `netsh wlan show interfaces` (signal in %), `nmcli device wifi list` / `iw dev <if> link` (dBm), macOS Option-click or `sudo wdutil info` (RSSI and noise).
- **Match:** SSID (case-sensitive), security type, cipher, passphrase or EAP credentials, server certificate trust, band/PMF capability.
- **Signs:** 169.254 = no DHCP; gateway outside subnet = no off-subnet access; IPv6 only fe80:: = no RAs; private MAC breaks MAC-based rules; captive portal blocks until accepted.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "If I can ping the gateway, DNS must work" | DNS is a separate check (step 5) |
| "0xffffff00 is a strange IP address" | It is macOS's hex form of the mask 255.255.255.0 |
| "No reply to ping means the PC is off" | Host firewalls often drop ICMP |
| "The Wi-Fi password is right, so security matches" | The security type and cipher must match too |
| "A wrong mask always breaks connectivity" | Proxy ARP can hide it until someone disables proxy ARP |

### 17.10 Practice questions — Chapter 17

**1. (Recall)** Which command shows the DNS servers on a Windows client?

A. `ipconfig`  B. `ipconfig /all`  C. `route print`  D. `arp -a`

**2. (Recall)** Which Linux command shows the default gateway?

**3. (Output interpretation)** macOS shows `inet 10.10.30.27 netmask 0xfffffe00`. What is the prefix length?

**4. (Output interpretation)** A Windows client shows IPv4 10.10.30.25, mask 255.255.255.0, gateway 10.10.3.1. What will fail and why?

**5. (Diagnosis)** A user can ping 192.0.2.8 but `ping www.campus.example` says the host could not be found. Where is the problem?

**6. (Diagnosis)** A Linux laptop has only an `fe80::` IPv6 address on Wi-Fi while Windows laptops on the same SSID have 2001:db8:acad:30:: addresses. What do you check first on the Linux laptop?

**7. (Wireless)** `netsh wlan show interfaces` reports `Authentication : WPA2-Personal`, but the corporate SSID uses WPA2-Enterprise and the user cannot connect. What is wrong?

**8. (Wireless)** A macOS client shows RSSI −74 dBm and noise −88 dBm. Calculate SNR and judge the connection.

**9. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which commands renew a DHCP lease?

A. `ipconfig /renew` (Windows)  B. `ip route flush` (Linux)  C. `sudo ipconfig set en0 DHCP` (macOS)  D. `arp -d *`  E. `netsh wlan show interfaces`

**10. (Concept)** Why might a DHCP reservation for a phone's MAC address stop working after the phone joins the network again?

**11. (Diagnosis)** A laptop works at home but not on campus Ethernet. `ipconfig` shows 192.168.1.40/24, gateway 192.168.1.1. What happened?

**12. (Ladder)** Ping to 127.0.0.1 and to the PC's own address succeed; ping to the gateway fails. Which layers do you check next?

**13. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A user's laptop shows 169.254.10.7. A digital assistant suggests: *"Set a static IP of 169.254.10.1 as the default gateway on the laptop."* Is that sensible?

### 17.11 Answer key with explanations

1. **B.** Plain `ipconfig` omits DNS servers and DHCP details; `route print` shows routes; `arp -a` shows the ARP cache.
2. `ip route show` (look for the `default via` line).
3. 0xfffffe00 = 255.255.254.0 = **/23** (fe = 11111110).
4. Everything off the local subnet fails: the gateway 10.10.3.1 is **not in 10.10.30.0/24**, so the client cannot reach it. Local hosts still work.
5. **DNS**: IP reachability works, name resolution does not. Check the DNS server setting and `nslookup`.
6. Whether it receives **Router Advertisements** / has SLAAC enabled for that connection (NetworkManager's IPv6 method), since the network clearly provides global addresses to others.
7. The saved profile uses the wrong **security type** (Personal instead of Enterprise). Forget the network and reconnect with 802.1X credentials.
8. SNR = −74 − (−88) = **14 dB** — too low for reliable service; expect slow, dropping connections. Move closer to an AP, reduce interference or add coverage.
9. **A and C.** B changes routes, D clears the ARP cache, E only displays Wi-Fi status.
10. The phone may use a **private (random) MAC** for that network, so the MAC no longer matches the reservation.
11. The adapter keeps a **static** configuration from home. Set it back to obtain an address automatically (DHCP).
12. The local link: cable or Wi-Fi association, VLAN on the switch port, the mask and gateway settings, then ARP (`arp -a`).
13. **No.** 169.254.x.x means DHCP failed; inventing a gateway in the APIPA range cannot route anywhere. Find out why DHCP failed (link, VLAN, relay, pool — Chapter 14).

Both figures were rendered, embedded beside the text they explain, and checked visually after rendering. Figure 17.2 had its divider running through the footnote; it was shortened and re-published.

| Figure | Title | Section | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 17.1 | Client checks in order, with usual causes | 17.2 | Rendered, embedded, checked |
| 17.2 | How the mask decides gateway or direct delivery | 17.4 | Rendered, embedded, checked after one fix |

## Chapter 18 — Secure device access: local users, SSH and AAA

**Objective covered:** **4.1 Configure network devices with local usernames and as an AAA client (TACACS+ and RADIUS) for management.** This chapter is about who may log in to a router or switch to manage it, how they prove it, and how that is recorded. Network access for users (802.1X, Chapter 16) uses the same AAA ideas.

### 18.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Secure console and remote access with local usernames, strong password hashing and privilege levels.
2. Configure SSH version 2 and disable Telnet.
3. Explain authentication, authorization and accounting and compare TACACS+ with RADIUS.
4. Configure an IOS device as a TACACS+ and RADIUS client with local fallback.
5. Explain the difference between a server *rejecting* a login and a server being *unreachable*.
6. Verify and troubleshoot AAA logins.

### 18.2 Local usernames, privilege and password storage

A single shared line password (`password cisco` under `line vty`) cannot tell administrators apart and cannot be revoked for one person. **Local usernames** fix that on a small network.

```
R1(config)# enable algorithm-type scrypt secret Str0ng-Enable-2026      ! type 9 hash
R1(config)# username netadmin privilege 15 algorithm-type scrypt secret N3t-Admin-2026
R1(config)# username helpdesk privilege 1 algorithm-type scrypt secret H3lp-Desk-2026
R1(config)# security passwords min-length 10
R1(config)# login block-for 120 attempts 3 within 60       ! slow down password guessing
R1(config)# service password-encryption                     ! hides any remaining type 0 passwords as type 7
R1(config)# line console 0
R1(config-line)# login local
R1(config-line)# exec-timeout 10 0
```

| Password type in the configuration | Meaning | Use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | Plain text | Never leave it |
| 7 | Reversible (`service password-encryption`) | Only hides text from shoulder-surfers; decodable in seconds |
| 5 | MD5 hash (`secret` on older IOS) | Weak by today's standards |
| 8 | PBKDF2-SHA-256 | Good |
| **9** | **scrypt** | Best available; `algorithm-type scrypt` |

**Privilege levels:** **1** = user EXEC (`>`, show commands only), **15** = privileged EXEC (`#`, everything); levels 2–14 can be customised. A user with `privilege 15` lands directly in privileged EXEC; a level 1 user must type `enable` and know the enable secret.

### 18.3 SSH for remote management

Telnet sends everything, including passwords, in clear text. **SSH** encrypts the session. Configure SSH version 2 and allow only SSH on the VTY lines:

```
R1(config)# hostname R1
R1(config)# ip domain-name campus.example                  ! needed for the RSA key name
R1(config)# crypto key generate rsa modulus 2048           ! RSA key pair for SSH
R1(config)# ip ssh version 2
R1(config)# ip ssh time-out 60
R1(config)# ip ssh authentication-retries 3
R1(config)# line vty 0 15
R1(config-line)# login local
R1(config-line)# transport input ssh                       ! no Telnet
R1(config-line)# exec-timeout 10 0
```

Verify with `show ip ssh` (version, timeouts) and `show ssh` (active sessions); connect from a PC with `ssh -l netadmin 10.10.99.2` (Windows, Linux, macOS and Packet Tracer). Restrict which subnets may reach the VTY lines with an ACL and `access-class` (Chapter 20).

Local accounts do not scale: with 50 switches, a password change means 50 logins, and there is no central record of who did what. That is what AAA solves.

### 18.4 AAA: authentication, authorization, accounting

**AAA** moves user accounts off each device to a central server:

| Function | Question | Example |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Authentication** | Who are you? | Username and password checked by the server |
| **Authorization** | What may you do? | Privilege 15 for network engineers, privilege 1 or a limited command set for help desk |
| **Accounting** | What did you do, and when? | Login/logout times and every command entered, sent to the server |

The router or switch is the **AAA client** (also called the network access server, NAS); the **AAA server** is typically **Cisco ISE**, or another TACACS+/RADIUS server. Each client and the server share a **secret key** that protects their messages. Figure 18.1 shows a login.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch18/fig-18-01-aaa-tacacs-login.svg" alt="Figure 18.1 — An administrator's SSH login authenticated, authorized and accounted by a TACACS+ server" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 18.1</b> — An administrator's SSH login authenticated, authorized and accounted by a TACACS+ server</sub></p>

In Figure 18.1 time runs downward. The two green replies are the server's decisions (who you are, what you may do); the dashed blue arrow is accounting, which keeps running for the whole session.

### 18.5 TACACS+ versus RADIUS

|  | **TACACS+** | **RADIUS** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Origin | Cisco; now documented as RFC 8907 | Open standard (RFC 2865/2866) |
| Transport | **TCP 49** | **UDP 1812** (authentication), **1813** (accounting); old ports 1645/1646 |
| Encryption | **Whole payload** | **Password only**; the rest is visible |
| AAA functions | **Separated** — authentication, authorization and accounting can use different servers | Authentication and authorization **combined** in one reply |
| Per-command authorization | **Yes** — the server can allow or deny each command | No |
| Typical use | **Device administration** (who may configure routers and switches) | **Network access** — 802.1X, Wi-Fi, VPN users; also device administration |

**Rule of thumb for the exam:** TACACS+ for administrators managing devices; RADIUS for users joining the network. Both are on the syllabus as AAA clients for **management**, and both work for it.

**How the server sets the privilege level.** After a successful login, the server's authorization reply can carry a privilege level (for TACACS+ `priv-lvl=15`; for RADIUS a Cisco attribute such as `shell:priv-lvl=15`). The device uses it only if **exec authorization** is configured (18.6); otherwise users arrive at privilege 1.

### 18.6 Configuring IOS as an AAA client

**Step 0 — protect yourself from lock-out.** `aaa new-model` immediately changes how logins work. Before entering it, create a local privilege 15 user, keep a console session open, and save the configuration only after testing.

**TACACS+ (current IOS XE syntax)**

```
DSW1(config)# username netadmin privilege 15 algorithm-type scrypt secret N3t-Admin-2026   ! local fallback account
DSW1(config)# aaa new-model
DSW1(config)# tacacs server ISE-TAC
DSW1(config-server-tacacs)# address ipv4 10.10.99.20
DSW1(config-server-tacacs)# key T4cacs-Sh4red-Key
DSW1(config-server-tacacs)# exit
DSW1(config)# aaa group server tacacs+ ADMIN-TAC
DSW1(config-sg-tacacs+)# server name ISE-TAC
DSW1(config-sg-tacacs+)# ip tacacs source-interface Vlan99      ! requests always come from 10.10.99.2
DSW1(config-sg-tacacs+)# exit
DSW1(config)# aaa authentication login VTY-AUTH group ADMIN-TAC local
DSW1(config)# aaa authorization exec VTY-AUTH group ADMIN-TAC local if-authenticated
DSW1(config)# aaa accounting exec default start-stop group ADMIN-TAC
DSW1(config)# aaa accounting commands 15 default start-stop group ADMIN-TAC
DSW1(config)# aaa authentication login CONSOLE local               ! console never depends on the network
DSW1(config)# line vty 0 15
DSW1(config-line)# login authentication VTY-AUTH
DSW1(config-line)# authorization exec VTY-AUTH
DSW1(config-line)# transport input ssh
DSW1(config-line)# line console 0
DSW1(config-line)# login authentication CONSOLE
```

**RADIUS (current IOS XE syntax)**

```
SW1(config)# aaa new-model
SW1(config)# radius server ISE-RAD
SW1(config-radius-server)# address ipv4 10.10.99.20 auth-port 1812 acct-port 1813
SW1(config-radius-server)# key R4dius-Sh4red-Key
SW1(config-radius-server)# exit
SW1(config)# aaa group server radius ADMIN-RAD
SW1(config-sg-radius)# server name ISE-RAD
SW1(config-sg-radius)# exit
SW1(config)# aaa authentication login default group ADMIN-RAD local
SW1(config)# aaa authorization exec default group ADMIN-RAD local if-authenticated
SW1(config)# aaa accounting exec default start-stop group ADMIN-RAD
```

The list named **`default`** applies to every line that has no named list; a **named list** (VTY-AUTH) applies only where you reference it. **Older syntax**, still accepted by many releases and used by Packet Tracer: `tacacs-server host 10.10.99.20 key T4cacs-Sh4red-Key` and `radius-server host 10.10.99.20 key R4dius-Sh4red-Key`, with `group tacacs+` / `group radius` in the method lists.

| Command | Mode | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `aaa new-model` | Global | Turns on AAA; method lists now control logins |
| `tacacs server <name>` / `radius server <name>` + `address`, `key` | Global → server | Defines a server and its shared key |
| `aaa group server tacacs+ \| radius <name>` | Global | Groups servers; failover to the next server in the group |
| `aaa authentication login <list> <method1> <method2> …` | Global | Who may log in, and in which order to ask |
| `aaa authorization exec <list> …` | Global | Fetches the privilege level for the session |
| `aaa accounting exec \| commands <level> <list> start-stop …` | Global | Records sessions and commands |
| `login authentication <list>` / `authorization exec <list>` | Line | Applies named lists to console or VTY |
| `ip tacacs source-interface` / `ip radius source-interface` | Group or global | Fixes the source address the server sees |

### 18.7 Method lists: error versus failure

A method list is tried **left to right** (Figure 18.2). The next method is used **only if the current one gives no answer** — server unreachable, timed out, or not configured (**ERROR**). If the server answers **"reject"** (**FAIL**), the login is refused and IOS does **not** try the local database.

So `group ADMIN-TAC local` means: "use TACACS+; only if every TACACS+ server is unreachable, use the local accounts". This is deliberate — a person removed from the AAA server must not get in with an old local password.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch18/fig-18-02-aaa-method-list.svg" alt="Figure 18.2 — AAA method list: ERROR moves to the next method, FAIL stops the login" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 18.2</b> — AAA method list: ERROR moves to the next method, FAIL stops the login</sub></p>

Figure 18.2 reads from the login on the left. Only the amber ERROR path leads down to the local database; a red FAIL from the server ends the attempt.

### 18.8 Verifying and troubleshooting AAA

| Command | Use it to check |
| --- | --- |
| `show running-config \| section aaa\|tacacs\|radius` | Method lists, servers, keys (keys appear encrypted) |
| `show aaa servers` | Each server's state (UP/DEAD), requests, responses, timeouts |
| `show tacacs` / `show radius statistics` | Per-protocol counters |
| `test aaa group ADMIN-TAC netadmin <password> legacy` | Test a login against the server without logging out |
| `show users` / `who` | Who is logged in, from where, on which line |
| `debug aaa authentication`, `debug tacacs`, `debug radius` | Watch the exchange (briefly) |
| Server logs (ISE live logs) | The server's reason for a reject: bad password, unknown device, wrong secret |

| Symptom | Likely cause | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Locked out right after `aaa new-model` | No local user, or method list pointing at an unreachable server with no fallback | Console access; create the local user first next time |
| Every login fails, server log shows "invalid shared secret" or no record | Key differs, or the device is not added as a client on the server | Match keys; add the device's **source IP** as a client |
| Server marked DEAD, logins use local | No route to the server, ACL blocking TCP 49 / UDP 1812, wrong source interface | Ping the server sourced from the AAA source interface; fix routing or ACLs |
| Valid user rejected, local account not tried | Server answered reject (FAIL); fallback applies only to ERROR | Fix the user on the server |
| Login works but lands at `>` (privilege 1) | No `aaa authorization exec`, or server not returning priv-lvl 15 | Configure exec authorization; set the shell profile on the server |
| Console also asks the server | `default` list applies to the console | Separate named list `CONSOLE` with `local` |
| No accounting records | Accounting not configured, or wrong port 1813/1646 on RADIUS | Configure accounting; match ports |

### 18.9 Packet Tracer Lab 18.1 — SSH with TACACS+ and RADIUS, and local fallback (Intermediate–Advanced)

**Objective:** 4.1. **Start from:** your Lab 14.1 file. **You will:** add an AAA server in the management VLAN, make DSW1 a TACACS+ client and SW1 a RADIUS client for SSH logins, keep local accounts as fallback, and prove the ERROR-versus-FAIL rule.

**New device:** 1 × Server-PT (AAA-1) cabled to **SW1 Fa0/5**.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch18/fig-18-03-lab-18-1-topology.svg" alt="Figure 18.3 — Lab 18.1: AAA-1 in VLAN 99 serving DSW1 (TACACS+) and SW1 (RADIUS)" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 18.3</b> — Lab 18.1: AAA-1 in VLAN 99 serving DSW1 (TACACS+) and SW1 (RADIUS)</sub></p>

Figure 18.3 shows only the devices this lab touches; the rest of the campus (Figure 14.4) is unchanged. The two dashed arrows are the two AAA protocols, each from its client to the same server.

| Item | Value |
| --- | --- |
| AAA-1 | 10.10.99.20/24, gateway 10.10.99.1 (VLAN 99 virtual IP) |
| SW1 Fa0/5 | `switchport mode access`, `switchport access vlan 99` |
| AAA clients on the server | DSW1 10.10.99.2, TACACS, key `T4cacs-Sh4red-Key`; SW1 10.10.99.11, RADIUS, key `R4dius-Sh4red-Key` |
| Server user | `alice` / `Al1ce-2026` |
| Local fallback user (on DSW1 and SW1) | `netadmin` / `N3t-Admin-2026` |

**AAA-1:** Desktop → IP Configuration (static address above); Services → **AAA** → On; add the two clients (Client Name, Client IP, Secret, Server Type) and user *alice*.

**DSW1 — TACACS+ client** (Packet Tracer uses the older server syntax; `algorithm-type scrypt` may be unavailable, so plain `secret` is used)

```
configure terminal
enable secret En4ble-2026
username netadmin privilege 15 secret N3t-Admin-2026
ip domain-name campus.example
crypto key generate rsa
! answer 2048 when asked for the modulus
ip ssh version 2
aaa new-model
tacacs-server host 10.10.99.20 key T4cacs-Sh4red-Key
aaa authentication login VTY-AUTH group tacacs+ local
aaa authentication login CONSOLE local
line vty 0 15
 login authentication VTY-AUTH
 transport input ssh
line console 0
 login authentication CONSOLE
end
```

**SW1 — RADIUS client**

```
configure terminal
enable secret En4ble-2026
username netadmin privilege 15 secret N3t-Admin-2026
ip domain-name campus.example
crypto key generate rsa
ip ssh version 2
aaa new-model
radius-server host 10.10.99.20 key R4dius-Sh4red-Key
aaa authentication login default group radius local
line vty 0 15
 transport input ssh
end
```

(SW1's VTY lines use the `default` list automatically; so does its console — acceptable here because of the local fallback, but in production give the console its own local list as on DSW1.) Test every login **before** `copy running-config startup-config`.

**PT note:** Packet Tracer's AAA server cannot send a privilege level, so server users arrive at `>` and use `enable` with the enable secret. Real servers (ISE) return privilege 15 with exec authorization (18.6).

**Tests and expected results** (from PC1's Command Prompt)

| Test | Command | Expected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | `ssh -l alice 10.10.99.2` | Logged in (`DSW1>`) | TACACS+ PASS |
| 2 | `ssh -l netadmin 10.10.99.2` | **Refused** | Server answers reject (FAIL): local not tried |
| 3 | `ssh -l alice 10.10.99.11` | Logged in on SW1 | RADIUS PASS |
| 4 | On SW1 `shutdown` Fa0/5, then `ssh -l netadmin 10.10.99.2` | Logged in after a delay | Server unreachable (ERROR): local used |
| 5 | Same, `ssh -l alice 10.10.99.2` | Refused | alice exists only on the server |
| 6 | Restore Fa0/5; on DSW1 change the key to `Wrong-Key` | alice refused; netadmin often allowed | An unusable reply is usually treated as an error, so a key mismatch can look like a dead server |
| 7 | `telnet 10.10.99.2` from PC1 | Refused | `transport input ssh` |

Restore the correct key, then save on both switches.

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] SSH version 2 on DSW1 and SW1; Telnet refused
- [ ] alice logs in through TACACS+ (DSW1) and RADIUS (SW1)
- [ ] Test 2 refused, test 4 allowed — and you can explain why
- [ ] Console on DSW1 uses local accounts only
- [ ] Configurations saved after testing

### 18.10 Chapter 18 summary notes (exam revision)

- **Local accounts:** `username <name> privilege 15 algorithm-type scrypt secret <pw>` (type 9); `enable algorithm-type scrypt secret`; type 7 is reversible; `login local` on lines; `login block-for`; `exec-timeout`.
- **SSH:** hostname + `ip domain-name` + `crypto key generate rsa modulus 2048` + `ip ssh version 2` + `transport input ssh` + `login local` (or an AAA list).
- **AAA:** Authentication (who), Authorization (what), Accounting (record). Device = AAA client; server = ISE or similar; shared key per client.
- **TACACS+:** TCP 49, encrypts all, separates A/A/A, per-command authorization — device administration. **RADIUS:** UDP 1812/1813 (1645/1646), encrypts the password only, combines authentication and authorization — network access.
- **Config:** `aaa new-model`; `tacacs server`/`radius server` + `address` + `key`; `aaa group server`; `aaa authentication login <list> group <grp> local`; `aaa authorization exec`; `aaa accounting exec|commands`; `login authentication <list>` on lines. Older syntax: `tacacs-server host … key …`.
- **Method lists:** left to right; next method only on **ERROR** (no answer), never on **FAIL** (reject).
- **Verify:** `show aaa servers`, `test aaa group …`, `show users`, `debug aaa authentication`, server logs.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "`local` after the server is used when the password is wrong on the server" | Only when the server does not answer |
| "RADIUS encrypts the whole packet" | Only the password; TACACS+ encrypts the payload |
| "`service password-encryption` makes passwords safe" | Type 7 is reversible; use `secret` with scrypt |
| "`aaa new-model` changes nothing until lists are written" | It applies default login behaviour immediately |
| "The server identifies the device by hostname" | By the source IP of the request plus the shared key |

### 18.11 Practice questions — Chapter 18

**1. (Recall)** Which protocol and port does TACACS+ use?

A. UDP 49  B. TCP 49  C. UDP 1812  D. TCP 1812

**2. (Compare)** Give two differences between TACACS+ and RADIUS.

**3. (Configuration)** Write the minimum commands to allow only SSH version 2 on VTY lines 0–15 with local logins, given hostname R1 and domain campus.example.

**4. (Concept)** `aaa authentication login default group tacacs+ local` is configured. The TACACS+ server rejects user *bob*, who also exists locally. Does bob get in?

**5. (Concept)** In the same configuration, the TACACS+ server is powered off. Can *netadmin* (local only) log in?

**6. (Password types)** A configuration line reads `username ops password 7 0822455D0A16`. What does this tell you about its security?

**7. (Troubleshooting)** All server logins fail; the server log shows the requests arriving from 10.0.0.2 but the device is registered on the server as 10.10.99.2. What is the fix?

**8. (Troubleshooting)** Administrators authenticate through RADIUS but land at the `>` prompt. What is missing?

**9. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which are true of RADIUS?

A. Uses UDP  B. Encrypts the entire payload  C. Commonly used for 802.1X  D. Provides per-command authorization  E. Cisco proprietary

**10. (Design)** Why should the console line use a separate method list with only `local`?

**11. (Accounting)** Which command records every privileged-level command an administrator types?

**12. (Recall)** What does `login block-for 120 attempts 3 within 60` do?

**13. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A network assistant proposes: *"To be safe, configure `aaa authentication login default local group tacacs+` so you can always log in locally."* What is wrong with this?

### 18.12 Answer key with explanations

1. **B.** TACACS+ uses TCP 49. UDP 1812 is RADIUS authentication.
2. Any two: TCP vs UDP; whole payload encrypted vs password only; separate vs combined authentication/authorization; per-command authorization (TACACS+ only); Cisco-originated vs open standard; typical use device admin vs network access.
3. `ip domain-name campus.example` → `crypto key generate rsa modulus 2048` → `ip ssh version 2` → `username …` → `line vty 0 15` → `login local` → `transport input ssh`.
4. **No.** A reject is a FAIL; the local method is used only on ERROR (no answer).
5. **Yes** (after the timeout). The server gives no answer, an ERROR, so IOS moves to the local database.
6. It is a **type 7** password: reversible, offering almost no protection. Replace it with `username ops algorithm-type scrypt secret …`.
7. Either register 10.0.0.2 as the client on the server, or (better) fix the source with `ip tacacs source-interface Vlan99` / `ip radius source-interface Vlan99` so requests come from 10.10.99.2.
8. `aaa authorization exec …` on the device (and a privilege-15 shell profile on the server).
9. **A and C.** RADIUS encrypts only the password, has no per-command authorization, and is an open standard.
10. So that console access never depends on the network or a server: it is the way back in when AAA or routing is broken.
11. `aaa accounting commands 15 default start-stop group <tacacs-group>` (command accounting is a TACACS+ feature).
12. After 3 failed logins within 60 seconds, all logins are blocked for 120 seconds (quiet mode), slowing password-guessing attacks.
13. With `local` first, IOS checks local accounts and only asks TACACS+ if the local database gives no answer — so centralised control and per-user revocation are lost, and stale local passwords stay valid. Put the server group first and `local` last as a fallback.

| Figure | Title | Section | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 18.1 | SSH login through TACACS+: AAA steps | 18.4 | Rendered, embedded, checked |
| 18.2 | Method list: ERROR vs FAIL | 18.7 | Rendered, embedded, checked after one fix |
| 18.3 | Lab 18.1 topology | 18.9 | Rendered, embedded, checked |

**Objective completed:** **4.1** — local usernames, SSH, and IOS as a TACACS+ and RADIUS AAA client for management.

**Related objectives that reuse this chapter:** 4.2 SCP/SFTP authentication (Chapter 19), 4.6 VTY `access-class` (Chapter 20), 5.5 Ansible SSH access (Chapter 26).

**Next:** Chapter 19 — **4.2 Manage device configuration and software files using secure file transfer operations with SFTP/SCP**.

## Chapter 19 — Managing configurations and images with SCP and SFTP

**Objective covered:** **4.2 Manage device configuration and software files using secure file transfer operations with SFTP/SCP.** You back up and restore configurations, copy software images, check them and boot from them — over encrypted, authenticated transfers.

### 19.1 Learning objectives

After this chapter you can:

1. Navigate IOS file systems and use the `copy` command correctly.
2. Explain why TFTP and FTP are unsuitable for production and what SCP and SFTP add.
3. Configure an IOS device as an SCP server and use it as an SCP and SFTP client.
4. Back up, restore and replace configurations safely.
5. Upgrade a software image: check space, transfer, verify the hash, set the boot variable, reload.
6. Troubleshoot failed transfers.

### 19.2 Files on an IOS device

| File system | Holds | Survives reload? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `flash:` / `bootflash:` | Software images, saved files, sometimes the configuration | Yes |
| `nvram:` | `startup-config` | Yes |
| `system:` | `running-config` (in RAM) | No |
| `usbflash0:` | USB stick, where present | Yes |

```
DSW1# show file systems           ! every file system, size and free space
DSW1# dir flash:                  ! files, sizes, and bytes free at the bottom
DSW1# show version | include System image
System image file is "flash:packages.conf"
```

The **copy** command always reads **source, then destination**: `copy <from> <to>`. `copy running-config startup-config` saves; `copy startup-config running-config` **merges** the saved file into the running configuration (it does not erase lines that exist only in the running configuration).

On Catalyst 9000 and other IOS XE platforms the switch usually runs in **install mode**: it boots `packages.conf`, which points to package files expanded from the `.bin` image. Older devices run in **bundle mode** and boot the `.bin` directly. Both appear in `show version`.

### 19.3 Why not TFTP or FTP?

| Protocol | Transport | Authentication | Encryption | Verdict |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| TFTP | UDP 69 | **None** | None | Labs, PXE/phone boot only; anyone can read or replace files |
| FTP | TCP 20/21 | Username and password in **clear text** | None | Avoid on untrusted networks |
| **SCP** | **SSH, TCP 22** | SSH login (local or AAA) | **Yes** | Recommended; IOS can be server **and** client |
| **SFTP** | **SSH, TCP 22** | SSH login | **Yes** | Recommended; IOS XE is an SFTP **client** only |

SCP and SFTP both run inside SSH, so they reuse everything from Chapter 18: RSA keys, SSH version 2, local or AAA users.

### 19.4 SCP and SFTP: who starts the transfer?

There are two directions to think about (Figure 19.1):

- **The device is the server** — an administrator's PC connects to the switch and pushes or pulls files. Only **SCP** works this way on IOS XE.
- **The device is the client** — the switch connects to a file server (a Linux backup server, for example) with the `copy` command. Both **SCP** and **SFTP** work this way.

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch19/fig-19-01-scp-server-vs-client.svg" alt="Figure 19.1 — SCP with the switch as server (left) and SCP/SFTP with the switch as client (right)" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 19.1</b> — SCP with the switch as server (left) and SCP/SFTP with the switch as client (right)</sub></p>

Figure 19.1 compares the two directions. The arrow shows which side opens the SSH connection; the grey panels hold the commands typed on that side.

**IOS as an SCP server.** SCP needs SSH (Chapter 18), and IOS must be able to check the user's privilege, so AAA **exec authorization** is required:

```
DSW1(config)# username netadmin privilege 15 algorithm-type scrypt secret N3t-Admin-2026
DSW1(config)# aaa new-model
DSW1(config)# aaa authentication login default local          ! or group <AAA servers> local
DSW1(config)# aaa authorization exec default local            ! required for SCP
DSW1(config)# ip scp server enable
```

From a Linux, macOS or Windows 10/11 PC with OpenSSH:

```
$ scp -O cat9k_iosxe.17.12.04.SPA.bin netadmin@10.10.99.2:flash:/cat9k_iosxe.17.12.04.SPA.bin   # push an image
$ scp -O netadmin@10.10.99.2:flash:/DSW1-2026-10-15.cfg .                                        # pull a saved file
```

The `-O` option matters: OpenSSH 9.0 and later use the SFTP protocol inside `scp` by default, which the IOS XE SCP server does not accept; `-O` selects the original SCP protocol. To pull the running configuration, save it to flash first (`copy running-config flash:DSW1-2026-10-15.cfg`).

**IOS as an SCP or SFTP client.** Credentials can go in the URL or be prompted for. Set the source address the server sees with `ip ssh source-interface`:

```
DSW1(config)# ip ssh source-interface Vlan99
DSW1# copy running-config scp://netadmin@10.10.99.30/backups/DSW1-2026-10-15.cfg
Address or name of remote host [10.10.99.30]?
Destination username [netadmin]?
Destination filename [backups/DSW1-2026-10-15.cfg]?
Password:
!
5124 bytes copied in 1.204 secs (4256 bytes/sec)

DSW1# copy running-config sftp://netadmin@10.10.99.30//backups/DSW1-2026-10-15.cfg
DSW1# copy scp://netadmin@10.10.99.30/images/cat9k_iosxe.17.12.04.SPA.bin flash:
```

Note the URL forms: `scp://user@host/path`, and `sftp://user@host//path` (the double slash marks an absolute path in Cisco's SFTP examples). Exact prompts vary by release. A password typed into a URL (`user:password@host`) is visible on screen and in history — let IOS prompt for it instead.

Sources: [Cisco IOS Secure Copy configuration](https://www.cisco.com/en/US/docs/ios-xml/ios/sec_usr_ssh/configuration/15-1s/sec-secure-copy.html); [Configuring SSH File Transfer Protocol, Catalyst 9300, IOS XE 17.10](https://www.cisco.com/c/en/us/td/docs/Sanity_test/INC9625337/b_1710_sec_9300_cg1/m9_1710_sec_sftp_cg.html).

### 19.5 Backing up and restoring configurations

| Goal | Command | Effect |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Back up off the device | `copy running-config scp://netadmin@10.10.99.30/backups/DSW1-2026-10-15.cfg` | Encrypted copy on the backup server |
| Keep a local copy | `copy running-config flash:DSW1-2026-10-15.cfg` | File in flash for quick rollback |
| Add lines from a file | `copy scp://…/DSW1-snippet.cfg running-config` | **Merges**: adds and changes lines, deletes nothing |
| Return exactly to a saved state | `configure replace flash:DSW1-2026-10-15.cfg` | **Replaces** the running configuration, removing lines not in the file |
| Automatic versions | `archive` → `path flash:archive/$h-` → `write-memory` | Saves a numbered copy every time you `write memory`; list them with `show archive` |

The merge-versus-replace difference is a classic trap: restoring a backup with `copy … running-config` onto a device that has extra, wrong configuration leaves the wrong lines in place.

### 19.6 Upgrading a software image

Figure 19.2 shows the full workflow. The steps:

<p align="center">
  <img src="assets/figures/ch19/fig-19-02-image-upgrade-workflow.svg" alt="Figure 19.2 — Software image upgrade workflow with hash verification" width="100%">
</p>
<p align="center"><sub><b>Figure 19.2</b> — Software image upgrade workflow with hash verification</sub></p>

Figure 19.2 runs left to right along the top, then back along the middle row. The highlighted hash check is the gate: the red dashed path sends a mismatched file back to be copied again before anything touches the boot settings.

1. **Choose the image** from Cisco's software download site, matching the exact platform, and record its published **SHA-512** (or MD5) hash.
2. **Check space:** `dir flash:` — the bytes free must exceed the image size (install mode needs room to expand it too). Delete old images only after checking which one is running.
3. **Copy securely:** `copy scp://netadmin@10.10.99.30/images/cat9k_iosxe.17.12.04.SPA.bin flash:` (or push it from a PC with `scp -O`).
4. **Verify integrity:** `verify /sha512 flash:cat9k_iosxe.17.12.04.SPA.bin` and compare with Cisco's hash. A mismatch means corruption or tampering — delete and copy again.
5. **Point the device at it:**
   - Bundle mode: `boot system flash:cat9k_iosxe.17.12.04.SPA.bin`, then `copy running-config startup-config`.
   - Install mode (Catalyst 9000): `install add file flash:cat9k_iosxe.17.12.04.SPA.bin activate commit` — it expands the packages, updates `packages.conf` and reloads.
6. **Back up the configuration** first (19.5), and reload in a maintenance window.
7. **Confirm** with `show version` (new version, uptime minutes) and normal operation; keep the old image until you are sure.

If the device cannot find a valid image at boot it drops to **ROMMON** (`rommon 1 >`), from where an image is booted manually — a reason to check `show boot` before every reload.

### 19.7 Verifying and troubleshooting transfers

| Command | Use it to check |
| --- | --- |
| `show file systems`, `dir flash:` | Space and files |
| `verify /sha512 flash:<file>` (or `/md5`) | Image integrity against the published hash |
| `show boot` / `show bootvar` | Which image the next reload will use |
| `show version` | Running image and how the device last booted |
| `show ip ssh`, `show running-config \| include scp` | SSH version, SCP server enabled |
| `debug ip scp` | Watch an SCP session (briefly) |

| Symptom | Likely cause | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `scp` from a PC: connection closed or protocol error | OpenSSH 9+ using SFTP mode | `scp -O` |
| SCP to the device refused | `ip scp server enable` missing, SSH not configured | Configure SSH and enable the SCP server |
| "Permission denied" though the password is right | User below privilege 15, or no `aaa authorization exec` | Privilege 15 user; add exec authorization |
| Copy stops part-way | Not enough flash | `dir flash:`; delete unused files |
| Hash mismatch | Corrupted or wrong file | Copy again; check the filename and platform |
| Server never sees the connection | Routing, ACL blocking TCP 22, wrong source address | `ip ssh source-interface`; check ACLs (Chapter 20) |
| Restored backup, old wrong lines still present | `copy` merged instead of replacing | `configure replace` |
| Device boots to ROMMON after upgrade | Boot variable points to a deleted or incomplete image | Boot the image manually, fix `boot system`, save |

### 19.8 Lab 19.1 — File management, from Packet Tracer to real SCP and SFTP (Intermediate)

**Objective:** 4.2. **Packet Tracer limitation:** Packet Tracer's file server offers **TFTP and FTP** only, and most versions do not accept SCP or SFTP in the `copy` command. Part A therefore practises the IOS file workflow with those protocols (and shows why they are insecure); Part B practises SCP and SFTP for real.

**Part A — Packet Tracer (start from your Lab 18.1 file)**

1. Add a Server-PT **FILES** at 10.10.99.30/24, gateway 10.10.99.1, cabled to **SW2 Fa0/5** (access VLAN 99). On it, check that **TFTP** and **FTP** are on (Services), and add an FTP user `netadmin` / `Ftp-2026` with write permission.
2. On DSW1, back up by TFTP and by FTP:

```
DSW1# copy running-config tftp:
Address or name of remote host []? 10.10.99.30
Destination filename [DSW1-confg]? DSW1-2026-10-15-tftp.cfg
DSW1# configure terminal
DSW1(config)# ip ftp username netadmin
DSW1(config)# ip ftp password Ftp-2026
DSW1(config)# end
DSW1# copy running-config ftp:
Address or name of remote host []? 10.10.99.30
Destination filename [DSW1-confg]? DSW1-2026-10-15-ftp.cfg
```

3. **See why FTP is unsafe:** repeat the FTP copy in Simulation mode, filter on FTP and open the packets: the username and password appear readable. TFTP needed no password at all.
4. **Merge, not replace:** on DSW1 add `interface loopback 99` → `ip address 10.255.99.1 255.255.255.255`, then `copy tftp: running-config` from your backup. The loopback **is still there** — the copy merged. Remove it by hand with `no interface loopback 99`.
5. **Files and boot variables on SW1 (2960):** `dir flash:`, `show version | include image`, copy a newer 2960 image from the server (`copy tftp: flash:` — Packet Tracer's server lists the images it holds), then `boot system flash:<new image>`, `copy running-config startup-config`, `reload`, and confirm with `show version`.
6. If your Packet Tracer version accepts `ip scp server enable` and `scp:` in `copy`, repeat step 2 with SCP; otherwise continue with Part B.

**Part B — real SCP and SFTP**

1. In your **Linux VM** (Lab 4.1) install an SSH server (`sudo apt install openssh-server` on Ubuntu), create `~/backups`, and from your host computer:

```
scp notes.txt student@<vm-ip>:~/backups/          # copy to the VM
sftp student@<vm-ip>                              # interactive: ls, put, get, bye
sha512sum notes.txt                               # compare hashes on both sides
```

2. **With a real IOS device** — a lab switch or router, or an IOS virtual node in **Cisco Modeling Labs Free** (check Cisco's site for the current free tier) — configure SSH and the SCP server (19.4), then from your computer `scp -O` a file to `flash:`, and from the device `copy running-config scp://student@<vm-ip>/backups/R1.cfg` and the same with `sftp://`. Verify with `dir flash:` and `verify /sha512`.

**Questions after the lab**

1. Which protocols showed the password on the wire, and which did not?
2. Why did the loopback survive the restore in Part A step 4, and which command would have removed it?
3. What must you check before `reload` after changing `boot system`?

**Completion checklist**

- [ ] Configuration backed up by TFTP and FTP in Packet Tracer; FTP credentials seen in Simulation
- [ ] Merge behaviour demonstrated and explained
- [ ] SW1 upgraded with a boot variable and confirmed with `show version`
- [ ] SCP and SFTP transfers done with the Linux VM, hashes compared
- [ ] (If available) SCP to and from a real or virtual IOS device

### 19.9 Chapter 19 summary notes (exam revision)

- **File systems:** `flash:` (images, files), `nvram:` (startup-config), `system:` (running-config). `show file systems`, `dir flash:`. `copy <source> <destination>`.
- **TFTP** UDP 69, no authentication; **FTP** TCP 20/21, clear-text credentials; **SCP** and **SFTP** run over **SSH, TCP 22**, authenticated and encrypted.
- **IOS SCP server:** SSH + `aaa new-model` + `aaa authentication login …` + **`aaa authorization exec …`** + privilege 15 user + `ip scp server enable`. PC side: `scp -O file user@device:flash:/file` (OpenSSH 9+ needs `-O`).
- **IOS as client:** `copy running-config scp://user@host/path`, `copy running-config sftp://user@host//path`, `copy scp://… flash:`; `ip ssh source-interface`. IOS XE is an **SFTP client only**.
- **Restore:** `copy … running-config` **merges**; `configure replace` **replaces**. `archive` keeps versions.
- **Upgrade:** pick image + hash → `dir flash:` → copy → **`verify /sha512`** → `boot system` (bundle) or `install add … activate commit` (install mode) → back up → reload → `show version`. Bad boot variable → ROMMON.

**Common conceptual mistakes**

| Mistake | Correct idea |
| --- | --- |
| "`copy tftp: running-config` restores the old configuration exactly" | It merges; use `configure replace` |
| "SCP and SFTP need extra ports opened" | Both use SSH on TCP 22 |
| "Any SSH user can SCP files" | IOS requires exec authorization and normally privilege 15 |
| "A successful copy means the image is good" | Verify the hash against Cisco's published value |
| "IOS XE can be an SFTP server" | It is an SFTP client; use SCP to push files to it |

### 19.10 Practice questions — Chapter 19

**1. (Recall)** Which file transfer protocols encrypt both credentials and data? (Choose two.)

A. TFTP  B. FTP  C. SCP  D. SFTP  E. HTTP

**2. (Recall)** Which port do SCP and SFTP use?

**3. (Configuration)** List the commands, beyond SSH and a user account, needed to make an IOS router an SCP server.

**4. (Configuration)** Write the command that backs up the running configuration of R1 to 10.10.99.30, folder backups, file R1.cfg, user netadmin, using SCP.

**5. (Concept)** A restored backup still contains a wrong static route you added after the backup was taken. Why, and how do you avoid it?

**6. (Upgrade)** Put in order: reload; `verify /sha512`; `dir flash:`; `boot system flash:new.bin`; copy the image; `copy running-config startup-config`.

**7. (Troubleshooting)** `scp image.bin admin@10.10.99.2:flash:/image.bin` from a new Linux laptop fails immediately with a protocol error, though SSH logins work. What should the user change?

**8. (Troubleshooting)** An SCP copy to the switch says "Permission denied"; the user's SSH login works and lands at `>`. What is wrong?

**9. (Concept)** Why should you check `show boot` before reloading after an upgrade?

**10. (Multiple response — choose two)** Which statements about the IOS XE file transfer features are true?

A. IOS XE can act as an SFTP server  B. IOS XE can act as an SFTP client  C. SCP on IOS needs exec authorization  D. TFTP requires a username  E. FTP encrypts the password

**11. (Output interpretation)** `verify /sha512 flash:cat9k_iosxe.17.12.04.SPA.bin` prints a hash that differs from the one on Cisco's download page. What do you do?

**12. (Evaluate an AI assistant)** A network assistant suggests: *"To save time, use TFTP for nightly configuration backups across the WAN; it is lightweight."* Is that good advice?

### 19.11 Answer key with explanations

1. **C and D.** TFTP has no authentication or encryption; FTP and HTTP send credentials in clear text.
2. **TCP 22**, inside SSH.
3. `aaa new-model`, an authentication list (for example `aaa authentication login default local`), `aaa authorization exec default local`, and `ip scp server enable`.
4. `copy running-config scp://netadmin@10.10.99.30/backups/R1.cfg` (enter the password when prompted).
5. Copying a file into the running configuration **merges** it, so lines not in the file remain. Use `configure replace <file>` to return exactly to the saved state.
6. `dir flash:` → copy the image → `verify /sha512` → `boot system flash:new.bin` → `copy running-config startup-config` → reload.
7. Use `scp -O`: newer OpenSSH uses SFTP inside `scp`, which the IOS SCP server does not accept.
8. The user has privilege 1 (or exec authorization is missing). SCP needs `aaa authorization exec` and a privilege 15 user.
9. To be sure the device will boot the intended, present and verified image; a wrong boot variable leaves it in ROMMON.
10. **B and C.** IOS XE is an SFTP client only; TFTP has no username; FTP sends the password in clear text.
11. Do **not** use it: delete the file, download it again from Cisco, copy it again and re-verify. A mismatch means corruption or tampering.
12. **No.** TFTP has no authentication or encryption, so the configurations (with password hashes, keys and topology) cross the WAN readable and the server accepts writes from anyone. Use SCP or SFTP.

| Figure | Title | Section | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 19.1 | Switch as SCP server vs SCP/SFTP client | 19.4 | Rendered, embedded, checked |
| 19.2 | Image upgrade workflow with hash check | 19.6 | Rendered, embedded, checked after one fix |

### 19.13 Chapter 19 progress record
